Section 0: Introduction & Glossary

p1-p3

PDA Technical Report No. 66 (2014) — Application of Single-Use Systems in Pharmaceutical Manufacturing | p1 - p3

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the background of PDA TR66 and its positioning within the single-use systems (SUS) industry
  • Grasp the key success factors for SUS implementation: supplier partnership and risk management
  • Understand what the technical report does and does not cover
  • Become familiar with the key SUS terms and definitions to build a common language for the later sections
1.0 Introduction

Original Text

Single-use technology, often described as single-use systems (SUSs) or single-use equipment, has the potential to transform pharmaceutical manufacturing by offering tremendous opportunities to reduce cost, improve flexibility or cycle time, and shorten the time needed to build a manufacturing process for new, lifesaving drugs. This success, however, is very much dependent on how effectively the industry approaches the development and implementation of single-use technology. Ultimately, a new drug can only be successful if it is effective, safe, and available. Traditionally, only a comprehensive understanding of the drug product and manufacturing process can achieve these goals. This remains true as SUS is introduced in place of traditional reusable equipment. Encouraging an open science- and risk-based dialogue during supplier audits and evaluation of SUS supply chains significantly improves an SUS implementation.

This document is intended to provide the reader with critical concepts or points to consider when implementing an SUS strategy in a pharmaceutical manufacturing process. These concepts are intended to be valid both for chemically synthesized small molecules and for bioprocesses that produce large-molecule biopharmaceutical products. However, to be truly effective, many of these critical concepts must start with the design, supply chain, manufacturing, and distribution of SUSs themselves, as many inherent quality attributes can impact either the product molecule or its production process. Pharmaceutical manufacturers and single-use technology suppliers have become partners whose success is dependent on the control strategies implemented.

This document discusses SUSs that are in either direct or indirect contact with the raw materials, intermediates, and pharmaceutical drug substances or drug products. The document does not intend to discuss disposable items related to laboratory activities, final delivery system to the patient, transfusion bags, packaging, or medical devices.

Successful SUS implementation needs a comprehensive approach balancing the product and process goals achieved by using single-use technology. Section 3, Manufacturing Strategy, of this technical report is intended to present an approach that ties together key considerations when evaluating single-use technology. A well-designed manufacturing strategy will address technical, quality, business, and implementation considerations. Each topic has a dedicated section in this technical report, providing a detailed discussion of the associated considerations.

Determining the optimal manufacturing strategy involves concepts from many disciplines. An effective evaluation will have a balanced viewpoint, with input from engineering, regulatory, quality, project management, and accounting. Balancing risks and rewards of an SUS over a multiple-use system (MUS) will help determine the most appropriate manufacturing strategy. Thus, a structured science- and risk-based approach is recommended and should be consistent with principles described in ICH Guidelines Q6, Q7, Q8, Q9, Q10, and Q11. Primary goals, when developing any manufacturing strategy, should focus on controlling impacts to patient safety, product availability, and product and process understanding (1–6).

Only a formal partnership with an SUS supplier can ensure that quality is as good as or better than what is achieved with traditional systems (e.g., a purchase order is not a partnership). SUS suppliers provide equipment that includes the outsourced, value-added activities that the end user no longer performs. These value-added activities are important for the success of both organizations, and a winning control strategy for SUS has elements in both the supplier’s and the end user’s quality systems.

The concepts and recommendations presented in this technical report were developed over several years of discussion within the task force, at PDA workshops, and at other industry meetings. The authors of this technical report recognize that the conversation regarding how best to implement SUSs is just beginning. Ultimately the success of these systems will be determined by the decisions suppliers and end users make during implementation, and the hope is that this report provides a foundation for the industry to build on.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Single-Use Systems (SUS): Disposable components that replace traditional reusable stainless steel equipment in pharmaceutical manufacturing. These systems are discarded after one use (or one batch/process cycle), so no CIP (clean-in-place) or SIP (steam-in-place/sterilize-in-place) is needed.

Multiple-Use System (MUS): The traditional stainless steel engineered-equipment approach, designed for repeated use. After each use it must be cleaned, sanitized or sterilized, and those processes must be validated.

Control Strategy: The quality control framework established jointly by the drug manufacturer and the SUS supplier. TR66 stresses that this is not the buyer's responsibility alone; the supplier's quality system is equally critical.

Analogy

Think of SUS as the choice between disposable tableware and china. Traditional stainless steel equipment is like the china in a fine restaurant: durable, but it needs a professional dishwasher (CIP) and high-temperature sanitization (SIP), and the cleaning must be verified after every use. SUS is like carefully engineered disposable tableware: you throw it away after use and skip the cleaning step, but you must make sure the disposable items the supplier delivers are themselves clean, safe and consistent in quality.

More importantly, TR66 tells us that you cannot simply "place the next purchase order" and consider the job done. That would be like asking a disposable tableware factory to supply your Michelin-starred restaurant without ever visiting their production line. What you need is a genuine "partnership".

Key Notes

Scope: This document addresses only SUS that have direct or indirect contact with raw materials, intermediates, drug substance or drug product. It does not cover disposable laboratory items, final drug delivery devices, blood bags, packaging materials or medical devices.

Applicable across product types: The concepts in this report apply both to chemically synthesized small-molecule drugs and to biological processes that produce large-molecule biopharmaceuticals. For a CDMO, this means the TR66 framework is applicable whatever type of project you take on.

Alignment with ICH guidelines: The report explicitly recommends that the SUS strategy be consistent with the principles of ICH Q6-Q11, in particular Q8 (Pharmaceutical Development), Q9 (Quality Risk Management) and Q10 (Pharmaceutical Quality System).

"A purchase order is not a partnership." This is a core idea that TR66 repeatedly emphasizes: assuring the quality of SUS requires the drug manufacturer and the supplier to share responsibility.

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: When your CDMO is evaluating whether to convert a biologics line from traditional stainless steel to SUS, TR66 reminds you not to look only at equipment cost savings. You also need to consider:

  • Whether the supplier can deliver reliably over the long term (supply chain risk)
  • The impact of SUS component extractables & leachables on the product
  • Whether you have enough product and process understanding to support the risk assessment
  • Whether the supplier's quality system is aligned with your GMP requirements

Practice Questions

  1. Why does TR66 stress that "a purchase order is not a partnership"? In SUS implementation, what specific aspects does a supplier partnership cover?
  2. Which functions need to contribute to a "balanced manufacturing strategy"? Why is it not enough for the engineering department alone to make the decision?
  3. TR66 applies to both small and large molecules, but in practice which type of product is more likely to use SUS extensively, and why?
2.0 Glossary of Terms

Original Text

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Extractables vs Leachables: This is the most important pair of concepts in the SUS field. A simple way to tell them apart:

  • Extractables: Chemical substances extracted from a material under "extreme conditions" (high temperature, organic solvents, extreme pH). This is a "worst-case" laboratory test.
  • Leachables: Chemical substances that leach from the material under "normal process conditions" and are detected in the final dosage form. These are the ones that truly affect patient safety.
  • In-Process Leachables: Substances that leach during processing but are removed by downstream purification steps or sufficiently diluted, so that they cannot be detected in the final dosage form.

How the three relate: Extractables ⊇ Leachables + In-Process Leachables. Extractables studies are a predictive screening tool; leachables studies provide the final safety confirmation.

Analogy

Think of Extractables as everything that comes off a plastic container when you blast it with a pressure washer: it is deliberately "forced" out by extreme means. Leachables are what actually shows up in the water when you fill the container and use it normally. In-Process Leachables are like impurities that appear midway but are filtered out in the end: they were there, but they had been removed by the time you drink the water.

Key Notes

Closed System: In the SUS context, the concept of a closed system is extremely important. A truly closed system has no interaction with the external environment, which both prevents external contamination from entering and prevents the contents from escaping. This directly affects the environmental classification requirements and the sterility assurance strategy.

Campaigning: Note the definition here: extending the duration of use or the number of batches of a SUS without breaching the sterile barrier. This is not a permission granted by the supplier; evaluating and validating it is the end user's own responsibility. This reflects a core TR66 principle: the responsibility for SUS quality ultimately rests with the user.

Critical Warning

Ready-To-Use: a marketing term, not a scientific claim. TR66 states clearly that Ready-To-Use is merely a supplier marketing term, with "no regulatory or scientific basis supporting its applicability". This is an important reminder for every CDMO professional:

  • Do not waive your own validation responsibility just because a supplier labels a product "Ready-To-Use"
  • The end user must still assess whether the product meets the quality requirements of their specific application
  • This is consistent with the logic of responsibility for Campaigning: the user decides whether quality is fit for purpose

Terminology Hierarchy

Understanding the hierarchy of SUS terms helps with reading the later sections:

  • Single-Use Component (SUC): The smallest unit, such as a length of tubing, a connector or a filter membrane
  • Single-Use System (SUS): A complete system assembled from multiple SUCs, such as a combination of mixing bag, tubing and filter
  • Consumables: A broader concept covering everything that is used on a per-batch basis

Flexible 2D/3D Bag: 2D bags (pillow-shaped) and 3D bags (cuboid-shaped) are among the most common SUS components, used to hold process fluids or product. Because a 3D bag has six sides, it makes better use of the space in its container support.

Practical Application

CDMO terminology in practice:

  • Pressure Decay Test: In day-to-day CDMO operations, a leak test may be needed before every use of a SUS bag. Pressure decay is one of the most common methods: inflate, let the pressure stabilize, and see whether the pressure drops.
  • Opportunity Costs: Something CDMOs often overlook when making decisions. For example, if you choose to expand a stainless steel facility, the capital it ties up cannot go into a flexible SUS line, and you may miss the chance to take on more clinical-stage projects.
  • Deployment: The on-site assembly steps for a SUS. This is one of the biggest operational differences between SUS and traditional equipment: operators must assemble the system for every batch, so operator training and SOP standardization are essential.

Practice Questions

  1. What is the key difference between extractables and leachables? Why are extractables studies usually performed before leachables studies?
  2. Why does TR66 specifically point out that "Ready-To-Use" is a marketing term? What does this suggest for how you evaluate suppliers?
  3. Under what circumstances might a CDMO need to campaign a SUS? What risk factors should be considered?
  4. How does the concept of a closed system affect the cleanroom grade requirements for the SUS operating environment?

Section Summary

  • The transformative potential of SUS: Single-use systems can reduce cost, increase flexibility and shorten facility build time, but success depends on how well the implementation strategy is developed
  • Partnership is the core: "A purchase order is not a partnership." Assuring SUS quality requires the support of both the drug manufacturer's and the supplier's quality systems
  • Risk- and science-based: The SUS strategy should be consistent with the principles of ICH Q6-Q11, with patient safety, product availability and process understanding as the top priorities
  • E&L is a key topic: Understanding extractables and leachables is the foundation of SUS safety assessment and runs through all the later sections
  • The Ready-To-Use myth: This is only a marketing term; the end user always remains responsible for verifying that the SUS meets the quality requirements of their specific application
  • Clearly defined scope: The report covers SUS with direct or indirect contact with the drug product, and excludes laboratory supplies, drug delivery devices and medical devices
1

Section 1: SUS Manufacturing Strategy

p4-p14

PDA Technical Report No. 66 (2014) - Single-Use Systems | p4 - p14

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the four key decision areas of a Single-Use System (SUS) manufacturing strategy: technology, qualification, business drivers, and implementation
  • Master the gated decision pathway for assessing SUS feasibility and its science- and risk-based approach
  • Learn how to evaluate an SUS adoption proposal from the perspectives of technical feasibility, business case, product/process risk, and logistics control
  • Understand the key considerations in SUS implementation: supplier management, change notification, waste handling, and supply chain management

3.0 Points to Consider for Single-Use System Manufacturing Strategy

Original Text

This section provides an introduction to and overview of the most important points to consider and the necessary steps the end user must accomplish in order to implement a successful single-use (SU) strategy, while a more detailed description of these points and a structured implementation strategy can be found in the subsequent sections of this document. Additionally, this section offers a guideline for a structured approach for an initial feasibility evaluation of a single-use system (SUS) manufacturing strategy.

The key to successful SU technology implementation is the development of an effective manufacturing strategy for the molecule or formulation to be produced. Many factors must be considered when an organization is making strategic decisions, and implementation of SU technology is no different. In this report we suggest developing a high-level manufacturing strategy after a science- and risk-based approach with consideration given to four main areas that influence the success of SUS implementation:

  • Technologies and system integration
  • Qualification of assembled products
  • Business drivers
  • Implementation

The resulting manufacturing strategy will then outline the questions for consideration needed for successful implementation of an SUS. This technical report has a dedicated section for each of these areas of consideration. An overview of each will be presented in this section.

As depicted in Figure 3.0-1🔍, each area plays a crucial role in establishing successful use of SUSs. Questions within these areas of focus must be considered and balanced against an organization's needs and competencies when developing a manufacturing strategy (detailed in Section 7.1). Only a comprehensive approach ensures that the primary customer-based goals of patient safety, product availability, product understanding, and process understanding are met when implementing new technology.

"Figure 3.0-1: Key Decision Areas for an SUS Manufacturing Strategy"

Each area has a situation-dependent role in the decision process and may be weighed differently according to the circumstances of the end user. For example, a contract manufacturer may value the ability to shorten changeover time between different products, whereas a large pharmaceutical company may weigh the risk of adopting new technology against utilization of existing capabilities. A small organization may not have the resources (manpower or capital) to invest in a fixed multiple-use system (MUS). Other important factors to consider are:

  • The current stage of drug development
  • The scale of post-launch manufacturing
  • The likelihood of the facility being used to produce other products in the future
  • The risk associated with depending on a sole SUS supplier

A detailed implementation process for SUS is described in Section 7, and this includes details of the workflow for the implementation process, starting with the preparation of the manufacturing strategy reviewed in this section.

A gated approach is used to facilitate the preparation of the manufacturing strategy and decision process. A "gate," as it is used here, is a decision point where the pros and cons of the strategy are addressed using a science- and risk-based approach. A proposal for the effective evaluation of important factors within the gated approach is described in Figure 3.0-2. Each stage is crucial to the successful implementation of a new technology. Negative results at any stage may halt the implementation of the SUS. An example of such a negative decision will be a case in which there will be an adverse effect on patient safety or where there is no feasible solution to a technical problem. The aim of the gated approach is to fail the process quickly when a major problem cannot be solved. The decision pathway is not necessarily linear—the end user may decide to enter the pathway at any given point. However, each stage must be addressed to have a balanced decision process prior to the implementation of an SUS.

A detailed implementation process for SUSs is described in Section 7, and the implementation workflow is developed based on the decisions made in the manufacturing strategy reviewed in this section. An overview of the different implementation stages is described in Figure 7.0-1 and Table 7.0-1. Key documentary outputs for the whole implementation process are listed in Section 8, Appendix I: SUS Facility Overall User Requirement Specification.

The following sections provide an overview of a proposed decision-making process for implementing an SUS strategy.

Figure 3.0-3 offers an overview of how the sections in this document are related and support the structured approach for implementation of SUS.

"Figure 3.0-2: Proposed SUS Decision Pathway" — A gated approach evaluating: Technical Feasibility → Business Case → Product Risk → Process Risk → Process Control Strategy → Implementation Strategy → Logistic Control Strategy
"Figure 3.0-3: Technical Report Structure Overview" — Initial Feasibility Study (Section 3) → Key Drivers (Sections 4-7) → Detailed Implementation Workflow (Section 7)

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Single-Use System, SUS: An engineered process equipment solution assembled from polymeric components and designed for single or short-term use. Compared with a Multiple-Use System (MUS), an SUS eliminates the need for CIP/SIP but introduces new challenges such as supply chain dependency and material compatibility.

Manufacturing Strategy: TR66 stresses that successful SUS adoption does not hinge on a single technology choice but on building a complete manufacturing strategy. This strategy must cover four areas:

  • Technologies: Can the SUS meet the process requirements?
  • Qualification: How is the quality of the assembled product verified?
  • Business Drivers: Is the economic case sound?
  • Implementation: How is it put into practice?

Analogy

Imagine deciding whether a restaurant kitchen should use disposable tableware or porcelain. Disposables (SUS) need no dishwasher (CIP) or high-pressure sterilization (SIP), and changing the menu is faster (rapid changeover), but you must make sure of the following: the supplier can deliver reliably (supply chain), the material will not leach harmful substances (E&L), the volumes and costs make sense (business analysis), and you know what to do with the trash (waste management). This is not a simple "buy or don't buy" question but a strategic decision that needs a comprehensive assessment.

Key Notes

Gated Approach: This is the core decision framework of TR66. Each "gate" is a Go/No-Go decision point, applying a science- and risk-based approach. The design philosophy is "fail fast": if a significant problem that cannot be resolved (for example, one affecting patient safety) is found at any stage, SUS adoption should be stopped immediately.

The decision pathway shown in Figure 3.0-2 contains six key gates:

  1. Technical Feasibility
  2. Business Case
  3. Product Risk
  4. Process Risk
  5. Process Control Strategy
  6. Implementation & Logistics

Note: this pathway is not necessarily linear. End users may enter at any point, but every stage must be properly addressed.

Figure Analysis

Figure 3.0-2 (SUS decision pathway): This figure presents a serial decision flow in which each box represents an assessment area and each diamond represents a decision gate. SUS can be confirmed as viable only when all gates are passed. A negative outcome at any gate may lead to the conclusion that "SUS may not be applicable".

Figure 3.0-3 (report structure overview): This figure shows the three-tier structure of TR66: (1) the initial feasibility study (Section 3), (2) detailed discussion of the four drivers (Sections 4-7), and (3) the detailed implementation workflow (Section 7). The three tiers run from top to bottom, from strategy to execution.

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: As a CDMO (Contract Development and Manufacturing Organization), you produce different biologics for multiple clients at the same time. The greatest value of adopting SUS lies in shorter changeover time and lower cross-contamination risk. But you must also ask: if a critical supplier runs out of stock, can you switch to an alternative within a reasonable time? This is exactly the practical setting for the TR66 gated decision framework.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does TR66 stress that an SUS manufacturing strategy must take a "science- and risk-based" approach rather than rely on economic analysis alone?
  2. In a gated decision, under what circumstances should an SUS proposal be rejected at the very first gate? Give three examples.
  3. For a small CMO, which advantages of SUS are the most attractive, and why?
3.1 Single-Use Technologies

Original Text

SUSs differ in many ways from conventional MUSs. When examining whether a technically feasible single-use solution exists, the end user should evaluate process operating parameters and requirements in relation to traditional systems:

  • Process and product are compatible with materials of construction
  • Process volume, temperature, pH, pressure, and flow rate are within specifications
  • Oxygen, moisture, or light barrier properties of the system are adequate for the process
  • Specific process components can be sterilized
  • Process monitoring and sampling strategy exists for the critical operating parameters
  • Access ports and connection technology are available to perform the operation
  • Mixing technology meets the uniformity needs of the process
  • Weight, size, and physical strength of SUS meet the needs of the process and facility

After an organization determines that a technical solution exists for a single-use solution, other considerations to be included in the final decision to implement an SUS are as follows:

  • Does the implementation of SUS impact existing process equipment that will need modification to optimize use of an SUS?
  • What is the impact on cost and material management of customization, given that custom manufacturing of small quantities is less effective for suppliers? Although there are advantages to using a supplier's standard configurations, in some cases the SUS itself may require customization to accommodate an individual organization's needs.
  • What is the potential impact on facility design and utility requirements?

The end user's approach to the determination of technical feasibility should be recorded in a risk-analysis document that is consistent with the methods outlined in ICH Q9, and it should include process-specific considerations such as:

  • The stage of the SUS application
  • Process performance or capability
  • Process scale
  • Product quality attributes
  • Batch production rates
  • Factors affecting system design (e.g., process compatibility, component reliability, facility design, storage and materials handling, and/or transportation)

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Technical Feasibility: This is the first checkpoint in the gated decision. Before any business or implementation factor is considered, you must first confirm that SUS is technically feasible. The assessment focuses on:

  • Materials of Construction: SUS mainly use polymeric materials, which must be compatible with the process fluid and the product
  • Barrier Properties: The ability of polymer films to block oxygen, moisture, and light directly affects product stability
  • Operating Parameters: The tolerance range of SUS for temperature, pressure, pH, and so on is generally narrower than that of stainless steel systems

Analogy

Assessing the technical feasibility of SUS is like choosing kitchen equipment: you would not use a plastic pot for deep frying (temperature limit), nor a paper cup to store acidic juice for a long time (chemical compatibility). The polymeric materials in SUS have physical and chemical limits, and they must be operated within those limits.

Key Notes

ICH Q9 risk analysis: TR66 explicitly requires that the determination of technical feasibility be documented in a risk analysis that follows the ICH Q9 (Quality Risk Management) methodology. This is not optional; it is a compliance requirement.

The customization dilemma: Using the supplier's standard configuration is more cost-effective, but sometimes customization is needed to meet an organization's requirements. Customization means small production runs, which are less efficient and more costly for the supplier. In addition, the supply risk of customized products is higher.

Practice Questions

  1. List three process scenarios in which SUS may not be technically suitable (hint: consider temperature, pressure, and volume).
  2. Why are the barrier properties of SUS especially important for biologics? How do light and oxygen affect protein drugs?
3.2 Business Drivers for the Adoption of Single-Use Systems

Original Text

The decision between a single-use and a multiple-use process solution is often heavily influenced by a desire to reduce costs, increase the flexibility of a facility, and accelerate the drug development process. To that end, a structural comparative model that analyzes the cost of production, capital, quality, and process development, combined with the time constraints of the project, can be developed. The model's level of accuracy and detail should be suitable for the decision under consideration and should incorporate company-specific information wherever possible. A holistic model takes into account many factors that are useful for deciding if a greenfield facility is needed as opposed to a retrofit of existing applications. Often, a simplified model may be sufficient for an initial decision of whether SUS should be considered. The important point to consider is that an economic analysis needs to be done to support any important decision, and this is best done right from the start as part of developing a high-level manufacturing strategy.

The following lists include examples of elements that may be considered when evaluating the business rationale for implementation of an SUS.

Capital Design

  • Capital investment
  • Equipment costs
  • Utilities costs
  • Facility floor space (cleanroom versus lower classification, production area versus warehouse)
  • Validation—costs of validation clean-in-place and steam-in-place systems, facility environment
  • Cost of retrofitting the facility for a new product or purpose
  • Commissioning—costs associated with start-up and validation, testing, and documentation
  • Cost of capital—borrowing costs

Operating Costs: Day-to-Day Variable Costs

  • Labor
  • Utilities and energy
  • Waste management (solid and liquid, disposal versus recycling)
  • Sterilization
  • Cleaning
  • Setup and changeover time and labor
  • Amount lost to sterility failure or integrity losses
  • Operation model make or buy SUS
  • SUS run costs
  • Inventory
  • Media and buffer
  • Preventive maintenance
  • Value-added activities, quality audits of suppliers
  • Multiple-product (MP) or single-product (SP) facility, changeover, testing, and regulatory submissions

Drug Development and Process Assessment

  • Time-to-market and constraints
  • Number of batches per year
  • Number of products per year
  • Product lifecycle—estimates of product demand over time
  • Increased manufacturing time (may not fit existing manufacturing and quality processes)
  • Process complexity
  • Proximity to final drug presentation or container closures
  • Workforce skill level
  • Cost of active ingredients and intermediates
  • Health, safety, and the environment (HSE) (e.g., biosafety, toxicity, occupational health, and industrial hygiene)
  • Multiple-product or single-product facility

Time

  • Process uptime
  • Construction time
  • Time constraints
  • Regulatory milestones
  • Expected market competition milestones
  • Expected product launch date
  • Product demand growth rate
  • Inventory build time line
  • Equipment and component assembly

Green Manufacturing

  • Environmental concerns for manufacturing process
  • SUS waste generation
  • Resources for waste management
  • Design for sustainability

Value-Added Activities

  • Cost of quality and cost of failure
  • Quality, compliance, validation, calibration, operations
  • Outsourced activities, make versus buy SUS
  • Expedited transfer of manufacturing site to new location, reducing the time required to accommodate demand for additional product

Modeling will be highly dependent on specific circumstances and may result in different outcomes, dependent on model design, scale, timing, facility fit, product demand, and process frequency. For these reasons, it is not possible to make specific model design recommendations in this report. Every organization handles economic and process modeling differently. The concepts are presented in this report so the reader is aware that modeling plays an important role in supporting the decision process. Readers should reach out to others in their organization to decide how to best develop models for their organization. The following tools and resources may be useful to aid the decision process:

  • ICH Q9 Risk Assessment Tools, FMEA, fishbone, fault tree, HACCP
  • Comparative matrix
  • Internal cost databases
  • Cost-of-goods modeling
  • Comprehensive cost models

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Business Drivers: The decision to adopt SUS cannot rest on technical feasibility alone; a complete business analysis model is also needed. TR66 lists six categories of business considerations:

  • Capital Design: One-time investment, including equipment, facility, validation, and so on
  • Operating Costs: Daily variable costs such as labor, energy, and waste
  • Drug Development: Time to market, process complexity, and workforce skills
  • Time: Build time, regulatory milestones, and market competition timelines
  • Green Manufacturing: Environmental impact and waste management
  • Value-Added Activities: Cost of quality and outsourcing strategy

Greenfield Facility: A new facility built from scratch, as opposed to a retrofit of an existing facility. One major advantage of SUS is that it greatly reduces the capital investment needed for a greenfield facility.

Analogy

The business analysis of SUS vs MUS is like assessing the total cost of ownership (TCO) of buying a car. You cannot look only at the price (capital investment); you must also consider fuel consumption (operating costs), servicing (maintenance), insurance (risk management), parking space (facility footprint), and even resale value (future use of the facility). Every organization is different, just as every person has a different commute and driving habits, so the best choice will differ as well.

Cost Comparison Framework

SUS total cost = lower capital investment + higher operating cost (SUS consumables)
             + lower validation cost (no CIP/SIP)
             + lower changeover cost + waste disposal cost

MUS total cost = higher capital investment + lower operating consumables
             + higher validation cost (CIP/SIP/cleaning validation)
             + higher changeover cost + higher water/electricity cost

Basis for the decision: number of batches, number of products, facility type (multi-product/single-product)

Key Notes

Timing of the economic analysis: TR66 specifically stresses that the economic analysis should be carried out "at the outset" as part of developing the high-level manufacturing strategy. Do not wait until the technical assessment is complete before thinking about cost; the two should run in parallel.

No universal recommendation: TR66 states clearly that because every organization's circumstances differ (size, timing, facility, product demand, process frequency), it is not possible to give specific recommendations on model design. The point is that an economic analysis "must be done", not to tell you which option is always better.

Practical Application

CDMO decision scenario: Suppose your CDMO is evaluating whether to build capacity for a new Phase III biologic client:

  • If MUS is chosen: 18-24 months to build, but the cost per batch is lower
  • If SUS is chosen: production can start within 6-9 months, but the SUS consumable cost per batch is higher
  • If demand surges after the product is approved, SUS makes it easier to scale up capacity quickly

In this scenario, time to market and the uncertainty of the product life cycle will be the key decision factors.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does TR66 list Green Manufacturing as one of the business drivers? What are the pros and cons of SUS for environmental sustainability?
  2. For a small CDMO focused on cell therapy, which factors would stand out in its SUS business analysis?
  3. How do "cost of quality and cost of failure" affect the SUS vs MUS decision? Please give an example.
3.3 Qualification and Verification of Suppliers, Materials, Components, and Completed Assemblies

Original Text

Any adverse effect on product quality and, hence, patient safety must be the main concern when the end user performs a risk assessment of an SUS. An assessment of critical operational parameters must be performed in order to determine the potential impact of an SUS on product quality. In this exercise, the end user must take into account the potential for interaction between process fluid and the materials of construction of the process equipment.

3.3.1 Product Risk

The potential impact of the SUS on the product quality is a key area to be assessed through a risk-based approach considering its proximity to the final product, the processing stage, and product requirements. The following are examples of specific considerations for potential interactions between SUS and product fluids:

  • An SUS is a defined system, which may minimize the risk of both microbial and chemical contamination.
  • SUSs are susceptible to generation of particulates during the fabrication and operation of the system.
  • SUS minimizes risk of cross-contamination when they are used once or in campaign manufacturing of a single drug substance.
  • An SUS will contribute to the leachables present in a process. An assessment of impact can be made by 1) Identifying potential leachables through an extractables study and 2) Conducting further studies should an extractables study identify materials of concern.

Product impact considerations typically include:

  • Sterility
  • Degradation, or binding to the product molecule
  • Adsorption of the product molecule or product pool
  • Adsorption of the functional component of the media or buffer
  • Interactions between leachables or their breakdown products with the product molecule or formulation or the process materials of construction
  • Technical limitations of the SUS, which could lead to suboptimal processing, poor cell growth, low yields, or impact to product-critical quality attribute (e.g., limitation of temperature control may impact MAB glycan profile)
  • Changes in the light, gas, moisture, or other barrier to the surroundings, which could cause oxidation or chemical reaction of the product molecule, product pool, or media components

In addition to product quality considerations, incorporating an SUS may affect key operating parameters at different process steps and potentially the control strategy for the entire process. An assessment for each process step, as well as the entire process, should include the following considerations:

  • Impact on system integrity (may be positive or negative)
  • Estimation of risk of equipment or system failure for both SUS and MUS, including downtime for correcting potential failures and backup equipment required (e.g., maintaining an ISO 5/Grade A-B area for connections if an SU connector fails or designing the connection for backup sterile tube welding if a connector fails)
  • Size limitations of an SUS as compared to an MUS
  • Productivity impacts when using SUS as compared to an MUS approach
  • Verification activities, such as system integrity or leaks, compared to an MUS approach
  • Supplier and vendor supply-chain transparency, change control, communication, product quality, and supply security
  • Proximity of the SUS to the final drug product

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Extractables & Leachables, E&L: This is one of the most distinctive risk areas of SUS.

  • Extractables: Chemical substances extracted from SUS materials under accelerated laboratory conditions
  • Leachables: Chemical substances that migrate from SUS materials into the process fluid under actual process conditions

E&L assessment is a two-step process: an extractables study is performed first to identify potential risk substances, and if substances of concern are found, a further leachables study follows.

Cross-contamination: The single-use nature of SUS is in fact an advantage; it minimizes the risk of cross-contamination between products, which is especially important in multi-product facilities.

Analogy

The E&L concept is like heating food in a plastic container. You know plastic may release chemicals into the food at high temperature. Extractables are like testing under extreme conditions in the laboratory to see "what could be released in the worst case"; leachables are "what was actually released during normal use". For pharmaceutical manufacturing this assessment is critical, because any leachable could affect drug quality and patient safety.

Key Notes

"The closer to the final product, the higher the risk": TR66 repeatedly emphasizes the concept of the "proximity" of SUS to the final drug product. Using SUS in upstream processing (such as media preparation) carries far less risk than using it in the final filling step. This is because downstream purification steps can remove many potential contaminants.

Particulates risk: SUS readily generate particulates during manufacture and operation, which is a risk specific to SUS. An appropriate particulate monitoring and control strategy is therefore needed.

Contingency strategy: A contingency plan must be considered for the failure of an SU connector, for example maintaining an ISO 5/Grade A-B environment for aseptic tube welding.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does SUS have an advantage over MUS in reducing cross-contamination? In what situations is this advantage most critical?
  2. The example of the MAB glycan profile being affected by limitations in temperature control illustrates what type of SUS risk?
  3. How would you build a contingency plan for SUS connector failure? What facilities and personnel preparation are needed?
3.4 Process Control Strategy Considerations

Original Text

The monitoring capability of single-use and multiple-use systems may differ. Some points to keep in mind include the following:

  • When deciding between reusable and single-use probes, balance the cost per batch to generate the desired data, control of sensor calibration, cleaning validation requirements, and probe accuracy and reliability.
  • Consider and assess impact from potential differences in automation level; SUSs typically include more manual controls dependent on operator training and execution. Some SUSs may incorporate proprietary support framework and control systems at comparable costs to traditional process skids; however, this significantly limits SUS interchangeability. To avoid this problem, the end user may wish to adopt cross-platform supervisory control and data application (SCADA) systems, which provide greater flexibility and portability.
  • Consider that an SUS may affect how adjustments to the process are made (e.g., adjusting pH, adding solids, foam control); in some cases these adjustments may not be possible with SUS.
  • Note that qualifying and calibrating probes and the measurement system before use may be more complicated or not possible if a single-use probe is built into a presterilized system. Batch documentation practices for recording sensor calibration may be different for SUS.
  • Evaluate dependency on the supplier's and vendor's quality system and qualification activities.
  • Account for the possibility of smaller batch sizes with increased frequency or sub-batch or batch pooling due to smaller container volumes.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

SUS Interchangeability: This is an important TR66 concept. If you adopt a supplier's proprietary control system, it is fully functional but you become "locked in" to that supplier's ecosystem. TR66 recommends considering a cross-platform SCADA system to preserve flexibility.

Single-Use Probes: Sensors in an SUS (such as pH, temperature, and dissolved oxygen probes) may be single-use probes pre-integrated into the system. This creates a calibration challenge: if the probe is already built into a pre-sterilized system, the traditional pre-use calibration procedure may not be applicable.

Analogy

The automation challenge of SUS is like switching from a fully automatic dishwasher to washing by hand. Hand washing (SUS, with more manual operations) requires more operator skill and standardized training, whereas a fully automatic machine (the fixed systems of MUS) relies on the consistency of the equipment itself. Which approach to choose depends on your throughput, workforce quality, and budget.

Key Notes

Greater reliance on operators: SUS typically involve more manual control, which means greater dependence on operator training and execution. This is an important consideration in a GMP environment, because the risk of human error may increase.

Changes to batch strategy: Because SUS containers may be smaller in volume, the batch strategy may need to be adjusted, for example by increasing batch frequency, using sub-batches, or batch pooling. This affects quality control and release strategy.

Practice Questions

  1. If you choose a supplier's proprietary SCADA system, what risks do you face when that supplier ends support? How can a cross-platform SCADA reduce this risk?
  2. A single-use pH probe is pre-integrated into a pre-sterilized system. How do you ensure its measurement accuracy? What alternative calibration strategy is needed?
3.5 Implementation of a Single-Use System

Original Text

Implementation includes all of the activities required for the realization of the end user's manufacturing strategy. Since the manufacturing strategy is an assessment of feasibility informed by evaluation of the considerations from the sections on technology, business drivers, qualification, and implementation in this report, it provides a framework for implementation of the specific situation evaluated. However, each evaluation performed will be unique.

Furthermore, since time-to-clinic and time-to-market are becoming increasingly important factors for making implementation decisions, a comparative implementation assessment has become a crucial requirement. A well-thought-out and thorough implementation plan is important for successful implementation of an SUS. The implementation process is organized as a road map with a series of gateways between stages. The workflow should be organized around the following themes.

3.5.1 Stakeholder Management

Successful implementation of an SUS may require an active analysis of the effects of the implementation on the organization and stakeholder management. Broad implementation of SUS across an organization can impose certain organizational strains and stresses and problems may arise which have to be considered and dealt with.

3.5.2 Risk Management

The use of formal risk-assessment methods during SUS implementation provides a means for quantitative comparisons of risk related to MUS versus SUS to assist in decision making and stakeholder management.

3.5.3 Process Validation and Verification (PVV)

The PVV strategy for an SUS differs from that of an MUS. Therefore, the end user must be aware that traditional qualification activities may have limited value for an SUS implementation without the confidence that the supplier's production processes are of a high enough caliber and the end user's process is sufficiently robust to tolerate any variations of the SUS by the supplier.

3.5.4 Scoping

This segment of the implementation plan contains a detailed description of the technical scope of the SUS project; it complements the manufacturing strategy by providing global guidance and policies to be applied for SU applications across the project.

3.5.5 Project Execution Plan (PEP)

A PEP provides a detailed description of the project, the execution strategy, management, and compliance in order to attain project goals in terms of budget, time, and quality. This includes a policy for the identification and selection of suppliers, which will encourage the creation of a transparent relationship and help prevent the selection of equipment and suppliers from becoming an ad hoc process. An example PEP is shown in Section 9, Appendix II: SUS Facility Project Execution Plan Example.

3.5.6 End-User Requirements

This section of the plan provides a description of the end user's requirements for the SUS, including E&L and standardization in preparation for the equipment procurement process.

3.5.7 Testing and Documentation

Following delivery and installation, the user will develop methods and documentation for equipment SOPs; maintenance; qualification and process validation; the HSE plan; and personnel training.

3.5.8 Supplier Management

The effective management of SUS suppliers must include periodic review meetings involving the analysis of the supplier's performance during the previous period for quality and punctuality. Business review meetings should also include collaborative planning and effective forecasting of future demands in order to take effective advantage of the supplier's resources, lower the costs of maintaining capacity, and eliminate spikes in demand that may affect lead times. Appropriate supply and quality agreements must be in place to clearly communicate expectations to critical suppliers.

Drug master files provided by either the polymer compound or film supplier can be referred to, however, they may not exist for all applications and the level of detail of the information may vary by submission. The polymer compound formulations (type and levels of additives), for example, may only list the highest amount of the additives that can be included in the polymer rather than specifying the actual specification range of additives. In any case the information is not available to the end user so it is preferable to receive the information in the form of a regulatory support file.

3.5.9 Logistics Control Requirements

A logistics control strategy is a critical part of the implementation of an SUS because the end user relies on the supplier's production and quality systems to a higher degree than for the implementation of an MUS. The following factors are important for an effective logistics control strategy.

3.5.9.1 Inventory and Supply Chain Management

A thorough analysis of the entire supply chain is necessary in order to safeguard the supply of important materials for undisrupted SUS production. Such an analysis must encompass the supply and inventory strategy of the vendors who supply components and materials to SUS manufacturers in addition to the supply and inventory strategies of the SUS suppliers themselves. SUS suppliers have a responsibility to protect the security and supply of their finished goods by appropriate risk analyses and risk-mitigation strategies for the materials and components they purchase. It is incumbent on the end user to confirm that these strategies are adequate to maintain the continuity of supply. If the supplier's control strategies fall short of expectations, the end user may mitigate the risk of supply interruption by qualifying secondary sources or putting specific inventory management and supply agreements in place.

3.5.9.2 Waste

Operation of an SUS leads to an increased amount of solid waste compared to an MUS. An SUS waste-management strategy must include:

  • Collecting, storing, and inactivating waste in the facility
  • Avoiding cross-contamination
  • Monitoring the waste-management activities of subcontractors

3.5.9.3 Transportation

All aspects of transportation and holding must be understood for their role in maintaining integrity of the material throughout the supply chain. SU Technology requires additional care be taken during the entire supply chain; transportation, receiving, storage and delivery to the point of use. Some considerations are:

  • Maintain integrity of material throughout the supply chain.
  • An SUS must have a control strategy (such as supplier qualification, ID tests, or statistical analysis) of any product contacting materials to assure performance of critical components. Note that testing incoming components will in many cases be impractical beyond confirming the package and label, since SUS are typically not fully unpacked until the point of use.
  • Ensure proper warehousing and inventory management.
  • SU processes place additional demands on a warehouse. Consider shelf space and support functions for a warehouse and ways of improving delivery time, maintaining integrity, and documenting chain of custody from the vendor.

3.5.9.4 Single Suppliers

In cases where an SUS component, film, or integrated assembly may be unique and proprietary, making it difficult to find and qualify a second supplier, the risk (including mitigation efforts and additional equipment needed to ensure uninterrupted supply) must be recorded in a risk-assessment document.

In some cases, sole source situations may exist with intellectual property (IP) protections, which need to be addressed with special contractual provisions in the event that the supplier is no longer able to provide the SUS in the future.

3.5.9.5 Change Notifications

The end user must make sure that a change notification agreement is in place with suppliers (including third-party suppliers) and that such an agreement provides enough time to assess the effects of any changes on SUS functionality. Predictable, controlled transitions and ongoing operations of an SUS depend on this.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Implementation: TR66 divides SUS implementation into nine topics, covering the full range from stakeholder management to change notification. This reflects that adopting SUS is not only a technical project but also an organizational change project.

PVV - Process Validation and Verification: The validation strategy for SUS is quite different from that for MUS. The traditional IQ/OQ/PQ model may not be fully applicable, because the quality of an SUS depends heavily on the supplier's manufacturing quality and consistency. Supplier management and quality agreements therefore become critically important.

Regulatory Support File: Unlike a Drug Master File (DMF), the information in a regulatory support file can be provided directly to the end user. Because the content of a DMF may not be detailed enough (for example, it may list only the maximum permitted amount of an additive rather than the actual specification range), and because end users cannot access the DMF directly, TR66 recommends obtaining the information in the form of a regulatory support file first.

Analogy

SUS supply chain management is like running a restaurant that depends entirely on delivered ingredients. Unlike a traditional restaurant that can grow its own vegetables and raise its own fish (MUS, with its own equipment), every dish you make depends on outside suppliers. This means you must: (1) confirm consistent supplier quality (supplier qualification), (2) have a second supplier in case of stock-outs (secondary sources), (3) make sure ingredients do not spoil in transit (transport integrity), (4) manage storage and stock well (inventory management), and (5) be notified in advance when a supplier changes a recipe (change notification).

Key Notes

Stakeholder Management: SUS adoption may cause pressure and resistance within the organization. Imagine this: the maintenance team may worry about having less work, the QA team needs to learn new validation approaches, and the warehouse team faces greater space pressure. Effective stakeholder management is the key to success.

Single Supplier Risk: This is one of the biggest strategic risks of SUS. If a critical component (such as a special film or a proprietary connector) has only one supplier, and that supplier cannot deliver for some reason, the entire production line may come to a halt. TR66 requires that such risks be recorded in the risk assessment documentation and protected through contract terms (especially where IP protection is involved).

Key Notes - Logistics

The waste challenge: SUS generate significantly more solid waste than MUS. Waste management is not only an environmental issue but also involves cross-contamination risk (used SUS containing active pharmaceutical ingredients or biological material must be properly inactivated).

Storage needs: SUS demand far more storage than MUS. Large quantities of single-use components need appropriate storage space and environmental control. SUS are usually unpacked fully only at the time of use, so incoming inspection is mainly limited to checking the packaging and labeling.

Change Notification: End users must establish change notification agreements with all suppliers (including third-party suppliers). These agreements must allow enough time to assess the impact of a change on SUS functionality. This is the foundation for keeping SUS operations predictable and stable.

Practical Application

Supply chain risk management scenario: During the COVID-19 pandemic in 2020, the global SUS supply chain tightened severely, and many pharmaceutical manufacturers faced shortages of SUS consumables. This real case illustrates TR66's recommendations on secondary sources and inventory management.

CDMO best practices:

  • Maintain safety stock of at least 3-6 months for critical SUS components
  • Identify at least one alternative supplier for critical components
  • Include a 12-18 month change notification period in supply contracts
  • Audit the quality system and capacity of critical suppliers regularly (at least once a year)

Practice Questions

  1. Why does TR66 say that traditional qualification activities have limited value for SUS? What aspects should the SUS validation strategy focus on more?
  2. If you were the supply chain manager of a CDMO, how would you build an "SUS supply chain risk matrix"? Which components should be classified as highest risk?
  3. What key elements should a change notification agreement contain? How long should the advance notice period be, and why?
  4. Compare SUS and MUS in terms of waste management. Which components of SUS waste need special inactivation treatment?

Section Summary

  • Four decision areas: An SUS manufacturing strategy must comprehensively consider all four areas: Technology, Qualification, Business Drivers, and Implementation; none can be omitted
  • Gated decision: Using the Gated Approach, assess each of the six gates in turn (technical feasibility, business case, product risk, process risk, process control strategy, and logistics control); failure at any one gate means adoption should be stopped
  • Fail Fast principle: The core goal of the gated decision is to be able to stop the adoption process quickly when a significant problem that cannot be resolved is found, avoiding wasted resources
  • E&L assessment: The interaction between SUS materials and the process fluid (especially Extractables & Leachables) is the most critical product risk consideration, and proximity to the final drug product directly affects the risk level
  • Supply chain dependency: SUS depend on suppliers more than MUS do, so supplier management, qualification of a second supplier, inventory management, and change notification agreements are the key elements for ensuring a stable supply
  • Economic analysis: The SUS vs MUS economic analysis must be carried out at the very beginning of strategy development, considering multiple dimensions such as capital design, operating costs, drug development timeline, green manufacturing, and value-added activities
  • Organizational change: SUS adoption is not only a technical project but also an organizational change, and it requires effective stakeholder management to address possible organizational pressure and resistance
2

Section 2: SUS Technologies

p15-p39

PDA Technical Report No. 66 (2014) - Single-Use Systems | p15 - p28

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the functional differences between Single-Use Systems (SUS) and Multiple-Use Systems (MUS), and the framework for comparing them
  • Master the three-tier SUS architecture of components, subassemblies and assemblies, and how quality responsibilities are allocated across the tiers
  • Learn the risk framework for SUS Technical Feasibility Assessment, covering both process risk and product risk
  • Become familiar with the process compatibility factors that influence SUS design, and with facility deployment considerations
  • Recognize common connectors, tube welders and tube sealers, and how to choose among them
  • Understand the properties, advantages and limitations of the plastic materials commonly used in SUS

4.0 / 4.1 Single-Use Technologies and System Integration / Introduction

Original Text

This section describes strategies for comparing single-use and traditional fixed multiple-use manufacturing methods with particular emphasis on the differences between SUS and the equivalent MUS. It also provides a framework for assessing the technical feasibility and risks associated with implementing an SUS. A description of the most common technical attributes of SUSs is also provided. Although SU manufacturing is undergoing rapid technological changes that may affect the specific factors used to compare and contrast SU with MU technologies, the fundamental principles used to assess the feasibility or development of risk assessment is expected to remain unchanged. The methodology and key points presented in this section will continue to be relevant as SUS suppliers continue to innovate.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Single-Use System, SUS: A process equipment system built mainly from plastic polymer materials and designed to be used once and then discarded. It replaces the reusable equipment of a traditional stainless steel system, which requires CIP/SIP.

Multiple-Use System, MUS: Traditional fixed stainless steel equipment that must be cleaned with Clean-In-Place (CIP) and sterilized with Sterilize-In-Place (SIP) before it can be reused.

Technical Feasibility: An evaluation of whether a SUS can meet the functional requirements and regulatory expectations of a specific application. This is the first gate before adopting a SUS.

Analogy

Imagine you run a restaurant and must choose between china tableware and disposable tableware. China (MUS) is durable, but it needs a dishwasher and high-temperature sanitization, takes up space and requires utilities. Disposable tableware (SUS) is thrown away after each use and needs no washing facilities, but the material cost recurs every time, and you must make sure the disposable material does not affect the food (for example through leachables). This chapter helps you build a systematic decision framework for making that choice.

Key Notes

TR66 stresses that although SU technology is evolving quickly, the fundamental principles used to assess feasibility and risk do not change. This means the assessment methodology you learn stays valid over the long term: however the specific products change, the risk assessment mindset remains the same.

4.2 Comparison of MUS and SUS

Original Text

Most stainless steel systems are available in an SU format. Since SU suppliers use less costly polymeric materials, they have developed physical and mechanical operations to produce the functional equivalent unit operation in a format that is designed for an SU. A comparative description of the features of SU and MU technologies is presented in Table 4.2-1.

Table 4.2-1🔍 Comparison of MU and SU Systems

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Sterile Envelope: The closed boundary that maintains sterility during processing. A SUS uses aseptic connectors and tubing welders to establish and open this boundary, whereas an MUS relies on SIP valve arrays.

Peristaltic Pump: The main pump type used in SUS. Its biggest advantage is nonfluid contact: the pump head never touches the product liquid, which lowers the risk of cross-contamination and removes the need to clean the pump head.

2D/3D Bags: 2D bags are flat, welded film bags (like a pillow), while 3D bags have a three-dimensional structure (like a box). 3D bags are more common in large-volume applications (>200L) because they make better use of the space in the external support container.

Analogy

This comparison table is like an equipment list comparing a traditional kitchen with a disposable mobile food truck. The traditional kitchen (MUS) has fixed stainless steel sinks, stoves and piping: durable, but costly to install and clean. The food truck (SUS) uses disposable containers, single-use tubing and peristaltic seasoning pumps: everything is replaced after each use, which eliminates the cleaning process but means material consumption keeps growing.

Key Notes

Note the key difference in the Measurements row: SUS sensors may be calibrated at the factory and cannot be recalibrated during the process. This means:

  • Sensor storage conditions (temperature, humidity, shelf life) directly affect measurement accuracy
  • Stricter supplier quality control and incoming inspection are needed
  • Process validation must confirm that sensor accuracy meets CPP requirements

Practice Questions

  1. Why are SUS pumps mainly peristaltic pumps? What advantages does this offer for sterility assurance compared with the direct-contact pumps used in MUS?
  2. If your process needs to recalibrate a pH sensor during processing, how should you handle this difference when moving from MUS to SUS?

4.3 SU Components and Assembly

Original Text

The primary components for the assembly of an SUS include fittings, tubing, filters, connectors, bags (film), porting (bags), impellers, spargers, valves, sensors, filling needles, pump heads, docking port containers, and outer pouches (dust covers). The individual components do not require environmentally controlled storage rooms; they are stored in sealed bags and must be handled with gloves once opened to avoid contamination during assembly of the SUS. Several of the components can be assembled into a system and supplied presterilized, commonly via gamma irradiation. This is especially convenient for a completed fluid contact pathway of tubing preattached to a bag with all the tubing legs either heat-sealed or terminated with aseptic connectors. These systems are called bag assemblies, and they have tubing sets or tubing manifolds attached to them.

As shown in Figure 4.3-1, fully functional assemblies are often created by connecting components to a set of subassemblies, which in turn forms a functional SUS assembly that is capable of performing specific unit operations.

Figure 4.3-1 Anatomy of an SUS

COMPONENTS
▼
Fittings, Tubing, Filters, Connectors, Film, Ports, Impellers, Spargers, Valves, Sensors, Filling needles, Outer Bag
Quality: Multiple supplier facility control for components
SUBASSEMBLIES
▼
Tube sets, Manifolds, Bags, Sensor assemblies, Filter assemblies
Quality: Supplier control for subassembly | Connections, Sterilization
ASSEMBLIES
▼
Full-capacity unit operations
Quality: End user for final functional assembly | Fitness for use, Sterilization, Deployment

MUSs are assembled in classified and environmentally monitored facilities, and the equipment interior is cleaned by a clean-in-place (CIP) technique. An SUS must be manufactured and assembled in a clean environment because there is no practical method to clean an SU assembly. Some tubing can be purged with ultrapure air or nitrogen for particle removal during fabrication; however, this is not practical for the assembled SUS. The area where the SUS will be assembled must be prepared in advance using standard clean-room practices. The outside of each component package must be wiped with alcohol before transport into the assembly area. Assembly rooms are typically ISO 7 or 8 (Grade B/C or Grade C/D, respectively), and it is normal to conduct a combination of nonviable particle and microbial sampling of the assembly room. The design of the room is similar to that of pharmaceutical manufacturing suites (i.e., HEPA-filtered air, low returns, smooth surfaces, and airlocks for entry and exit). Staff gowning must be appropriate for an ISO 7 or ISO 8 environment. Regular, consistent, and high-quality cleaning of the assembly room is required to maintain area classification and provide a consistent environment for manufacturing SU solutions. SUS components are manufactured and assembled in controlled environments in order to meet acceptable standards for particle, bioburden, and endotoxin levels. Since there are no established testing standards for an SUS, the end user must rely on the supplier's specifications and control strategies for particles, endotoxins, and bioburden. Suppliers should have quality systems in place to ensure their facilities and procedures meet end-user requirements. It is also critical that quality agreements are established between end users and suppliers to ensure that the suppliers' facilities are adequate to deliver an SUS that meets end-user requirements.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The three-tier SUS architecture:

  • Components: The most basic individual parts, such as connectors, tubing, filters, films and sensors. Quality is controlled by multiple part suppliers at their own facilities.
  • Subassemblies: Semi-finished items built from multiple components, such as tube sets, manifolds and bags. Quality is controlled by the assembly supplier, and connection and sterilization (usually gamma irradiation) take place at this stage.
  • Assemblies: The final functional assembly, which the end user builds by connecting multiple subassemblies into a complete system that can perform a specific unit operation.

Gamma Irradiation: The most common sterilization method for SUS. Unlike autoclaving, gamma radiation can penetrate and sterilize items inside sealed packaging, which makes it well suited to preassembled disposable systems.

Quality Agreement: A formal contractual document between the end user and the supplier that clearly defines each party's quality responsibilities and requirements. It is a key element of GMP compliance.

Analogy

The three-tier SUS architecture is like building a computer: the components (CPU, RAM, hard drive) are made and quality-controlled by specialist manufacturers; the subassembly (motherboard + CPU + RAM) is integrated, tested and shipped by an assembler; and the final assembly (the whole computer + monitor + peripherals) is connected by the end user according to need, who then confirms it works. Each tier has a different party responsible for quality.

Key Notes

SUS and MUS differ in one fundamental way: a SUS cannot be cleaned. This means:

  • The cleanliness of the assembly environment directly determines the particulate, bioburden and endotoxin levels of the product
  • The assembly area must be ISO 7 or ISO 8 (equivalent to EU GMP grade B/C or C/D)
  • The outer surface of every component package must be wiped with alcohol before it enters the assembly area
  • Because there are currently no SUS-specific test standards, end users must rely on the supplier's specifications and control strategy, which makes the Quality Agreement critical

Practical Application

For a CDMO, SUS "supply chain quality management" is more complex than operating your own MUS. You manage not only your own equipment but also multiple tiers of suppliers, from components to subassemblies. Recommended measures:

  • A supplier audit program (frequency set by risk level)
  • An incoming quality control (IQC) procedure covering at least particulates, bioburden and endotoxin
  • A complete quality agreement that clearly defines change notification obligations

Practice Questions

  1. Why must SUS assembly take place in a cleanroom rather than an ordinary warehouse environment? If the assembly environment is not clean enough, which quality attributes are most likely to be affected?
  2. Suppose you find that the supplier has changed the polymer formulation of the bag film. What process should you start, and why are the change notification clauses in the quality agreement so important?

4.4 Technical Feasibility and Risk Assessment Framework

Original Text

A technical feasibility assessment is needed to show that a proposed SUS can meet the functional and regulatory requirements expected for a particular application. This assessment is performed after the generation of user requirements (UR), which outline the particular technical requirements for an SUS application. The design of the SUS must be evaluated for each SUS application, taking into consideration process compatibility, facility fit, and the effect of SUS design on critical process parameters (CPPs) and critical quality attributes (CQAs). Figure 4.4-1 delineates potential process and product risk factors and provides a framework for conducting a technical feasibility assessment. In using this figure, one should be aware that process risk may lead to product risk.

Figure 4.4-1 Implementation of an SUS and the Assessment of Drug Process and Product Risk

SUS Technical Feasibility
Process Requirements & Parameters
  • Liquid constituents
  • Volume
  • Temperature
  • Pressure
  • Flow rate
  • Time
  • pH
  • Oxygen sensitivity
  • Light sensitivity
  • Sterility requirements
  • Sampling requirements
  • Solid additions
  • Mixing requirements
  • Cell viability and growth rate
  • Monitoring requirements
  • Mass transfer
  • Heat transfer
Product Requirements & Attributes

Are CQAs still being met?

Process Risk Considerations

Potential Loss of System Integrity:

  • Deployment of SUS and operator training
  • Physical strength of SUS
  • Failure modes and recovery
  • Failure rate of SUS vs MUS
  • Impact on operator safety
  • Preventive maintenance

Potential Process Alteration:

  • Size limitations of SUS vs MUS
  • Facility capacity/productivity
  • Extent of process adjustments required
  • Changes in SUS barrier due to surroundings
  • Changes in time/duration of process

Are CPPs still being met?

Product Risk Considerations

Potential for Added Contaminants:

  • Leachables/extractables of SUS vs MUS
  • Contamination due to manufacturing of SUS
  • Microbial contamination due to SUS
  • Chemical contamination due to SUS
  • Cross contamination from other products

Potential Process Alteration:

  • Adsorption of product or functional component
  • Ability of SUS to catalyze a reaction
  • Suboptimal processing due to technical limitations
  • Change in SUS barrier due to surroundings

Are CQAs still being met?

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

User Requirements (UR): The document that must be produced before a technical feasibility assessment begins. The UR defines the technical specification requirements for the specific SUS application and is the baseline for all later assessments.

Process Risk vs Product Risk: TR66 divides risk into two dimensions:

  • Process risk: loss of system integrity (such as a SUS rupture) or a change in the process (such as a size limitation affecting capacity). Core question: can the CPPs still be met?
  • Product risk: introduction of new contaminants (such as extractables/leachables) or product quality effects resulting from process changes (such as adsorption or catalytic reactions). Core question: can the CQAs still be met?

Key insight: process risk can lead to product risk. For example, a SUS rupture (process risk) can lead to microbial contamination (product risk).

Figure Analysis

The structure of Figure 4.4-1 can be understood as a "double funnel" model:

  • Upper tier (inputs): SUS technical feasibility, process requirement parameters (17 key parameters), and product requirements and attributes
  • Lower tier, left (process risk): loss of system integrity + process change → are the CPPs met?
  • Lower tier, right (product risk): new contaminants + process change → are the CQAs met?

One product risk item deserves special attention: Adsorption. The plastic materials of a SUS may adsorb active ingredients or functional components of the product, reducing potency. This matters particularly for low-concentration biologics (such as monoclonal antibodies and cytokines).

Analogy

A technical feasibility assessment is like an architect's evaluation before building a house: you must confirm not only that the house can be built (technically feasible) but also whether it will be a problem to live in: is the foundation stable (system integrity), will the building materials release formaldehyde (leachables), is the ventilation good (mass transfer), and will noise from the neighbor's renovation carry through (cross-contamination)?

Practice Questions

  1. Figure 4.4-1 lists 17 process requirement parameters. If you were moving a pH 2.0 acidic buffer storage step from a stainless steel tank to a SUS, which parameters would you focus on first, and why?
  2. "Adsorption of product or functional component" is listed as a product risk. Give a concrete biologics process scenario showing how this risk could actually occur.

4.5 Factors Affecting SUS Design

Original Text

A technical feasibility study includes a thorough review of the design of a particular SUS in order to prevent mistakes stemming from component selection and system configuration. The scope of this design review will depend on both the functional objective and complexity of the SUS application in question. An inherent outcome of a technical feasibility study is the potential for identifying that a supplier's off-the-shelf SUS configuration is suitably designed with prequalified components (e.g., as proposed by a supplier). In some cases a customized, user-specified component or system will be identified during a technical feasibility study. Custom SUS configurations with specified components require additional efforts from an end user during a technical feasibility study if the supplier is not experienced with the user-specified components.

4.5.1 Process Compatibility

Another factor to consider during the technical feasibility study of an SUS is process compatibility. This involves an assessment of how each of the components of the SUS, plus the SUS as a complete integrated unit, may impact the process outcome. A description of a process compatibility assessment is provided in Table 4.5.1-1.

Table 4.5.1-1🔍 Assessment of Process Compatibility

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Off-the-shelf vs Custom SUS:

  • Off-the-shelf: A standard configuration that the supplier has already designed and validated. The components are prequalified, so implementation is fast and cost is low.
  • Custom: A configuration made to the user's requirements. If the supplier is unfamiliar with the components the user specifies, the end user must invest additional qualification work.

Process Compatibility: Look not just at individual components but at the effect of the whole SUS, as a complete integrated unit, on process outcomes. This is a system-level assessment.

Key Notes

Table 4.5.1-1🔍 contains several factors that are often overlooked but essential:

  • Gas Barrier: Plastic bags are not completely gas-tight the way stainless steel is. Water vapor loss and oxygen permeation can both affect product stability, especially for oxygen-sensitive biologics. Barrier performance varies with temperature and relative humidity.
  • Pressure: Plastic bags usually have no absolute pressure rating, which is a fundamental difference from stainless steel pressure vessels. Operating under pressure requires an external rigid container for support.
  • Nonspecific Adsorption: Noted specifically in the materials of construction row: plastic materials may adsorb active ingredients, causing significant potency loss in low-concentration products.
