  • Film Permeability: Chemical agents used for wipe-down disinfection may permeate the SUS film and reach the product. This is an easily overlooked contamination route.

Analogy

Process compatibility assessment is like testing whether a newly bought food container can go in the microwave: you must confirm that the material tolerates the temperature (Temperature), will not release harmful substances (Extractables), has a good seal (Gas Barrier), will not deform (Physical Properties) and has the right capacity for your portion (Volume). Each item seems simple, but missing any one of them can end with spoiled food or a cracked container.

Practice Questions

  1. Your process requires storing an intermediate at -20°C for up to 6 months. From Table 4.5.1-1🔍, which factors need special evaluation? List at least 4.
  2. Why is "bags do not carry an absolute pressure rating" a design limitation that needs special attention? In practice, how can this limitation be overcome?

4.6 Facility Impact for SUS Setup and Deployment

Original Text

Although SUSs can be set up and deployed in existing manufacturing facilities, a new or renovated facility may be more effective if a high level of SUS integration is required. To manage the risk of damage to the SUS, assessment of facility design and layout should include SUS material management, storage, and handling during operational setup. Including a defined setup and deployment stage, with standardized activities for each part, is a recognized success factor for SUS implementation and should be a routine part of the workflow and planning for each SUS application.

Setup includes those activities required to deliver the SUS to the point of use. Deployment involves the hands-on steps required to successfully assemble and ready the system for use in a specific SUS application. Examples of the facility setup and deployment assessments are described in Table 4.6-1.

Table 4.6-1🔍 Assessment of SUS Facility Setup and Deployment

4.6.1 Operational Requirements

SUS processes have inherently more manual connection and operational steps than MUS processes, which are often highly automated. Although the initial system design of an SUS generates an initial method of operation, it may be necessary to optimize the process operation for an end user's individual process requirements and facility. Examples of operational requirement assessments are reviewed in Table 4.6.1-1.

Table 4.6.1-1🔍 Assessment of Operational Requirements

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Setup vs Deployment: TR66 divides SUS preparation into two phases:

  • Setup: All activities involved in moving the SUS from the warehouse to the point of use, including material handling, unpacking and environmental preparation
  • Deployment: The hands-on steps of assembling the SUS, connecting the subassemblies and getting the system ready

Standardizing these two phases is regarded as a recognized success factor for adopting SUS.

Changeover Time: A SUS can greatly shorten changeover time between products or batches (because no CIP/SIP is needed), but only if the supply and inventory of SUS materials are properly managed; otherwise the time savings are lost.

Analogy

SUS facility deployment is like a Formula 1 pit stop: swapping in new disposable tires is much faster than repairing tires on site. But if the new tires are not prepared in advance and the crew is not trained on a standard procedure, the pit stop can actually take longer. The time advantage of SUS depends entirely on a standardized deployment process and just-in-time availability of materials.

Key Notes

Facility impacts of SUS adoption that are often overlooked:

  • Greater storage pressure: SUS are consumables and need continuous replenishment. Compared with the fixed equipment of an MUS, SUS greatly increases warehouse space requirements. Supplier lead time and batch frequency directly affect inventory levels.
  • More waste: Table 4.6.1-1🔍 notes that space is needed to remove the SUS after use. The large volume of used plastic waste is an operational challenge specific to SUS.
  • More manual operations: Section 4.6.1 states clearly that SUS involve more manual connections and handling steps than MUS. This means a higher risk of human error and calls for better operator training and error proofing.

Practical Application

As a CDMO you may serve multiple clients with different products at the same time. One of the biggest operational advantages of SUS is rapid changeover: no full CIP/SIP validation is needed between products. To realize this advantage, you need to:

  • Create a standardized SUS configuration list (BOM) for each product
  • Prepare materials ahead of the schedule (supplier lead times are typically 8–16 weeks)
  • Establish a standard operating procedure (SOP) for SUS deployment and periodically requalify operator skills
  • Plan sufficient space for temporary storage and waste disposal of used SUS

Practice Questions

  1. SUS are said to shorten changeover time, but TR66 states that "SUS availability must be closely managed or the potential time savings will be lost". Design a scenario showing how these time savings could be lost.
  2. Looking at Process Connectivity and Disconnectivity in Table 4.6.1-1🔍, why are SUS operations more prone to human error than MUS operations? How would you design error-proofing controls?

4.7 Applications and Technology

Original Text

This section discusses several applications that are common in biopharmaceutical manufacturing, along with their cognate SU technologies. The structure to discussing these applications and technologies is as follows:

  1. A description of the objective of the application
  2. A description of current SU technologies available to meet the application needs
  3. Specific considerations of the technology that one may use as part of a technical feasibility study

4.7.1 Process Connections

Connectors and tube welders are used primarily to connect:

  • Components within an SU subassembly
  • Multiple subassemblies to create a complete operational assembly
  • An assembly to a neighboring unit operation

In addition, connectors are used with tube sealers or disconnectors to disconnect:

  • Components (e.g., bags) within an SU subassembly
  • A subassembly from a complete operational assembly

4.7.1.1 Technological Examples

A wide variety of connectors are commercially available for various user requirements. In general, two-piece connectors consist of two units with mating ends that may be joined to form a connection. Identical mating parts are denoted as "genderless," and nonidentical mating parts are usually denoted as "male" or "female." Although connectors are usually used to create a connection between parts, they may be installed internally within a pipe in order, for example, to open a flow path of a steaming process.

Single-piece insert connectors commonly are variants of standard straight, Y, or T configurations and employ hose barbs that can be inserted into tubing and secured with cable ties or clamps. A variant of a single-piece connector can be made by a specialized overmolding procedure that creates a connection with a uniformly smooth inner bore.

Connectors with integrated valves and resterilizable SIP connectors have the additional functionality of enabling aseptic disconnections and reconnection.

Table 4.7.1.1-1🔍 Functional Categories of Connectors

Table 4.7.1.1-2🔍 Specific Considerations for Connectors

The type of connection technology to be used should be considered when planning an SUS:

  • Tube welder: A tube welder is an alternative type of connection technology that is designed to make a connection by cutting the two pieces of appropriate thermoplastic tubing to be connected and rapidly heating, aligning, and joining the two ends to form an aseptic, integral weld upon cooling. There is no need for a specific connector in the thermoplastic tubing, as the welded thermoplastic forms the seal between the two lines connected. Silicone tubing is not a suitable material for tube welding, as it is not a thermoplastic.
  • Tube sealer: A tube sealer is a type of disconnection technology designed to seal a single thermoplastic tube using heat or energy to fuse together the lumen of one tubing end, segment, or fluid path. The sealed area is then separated from the rest of the tube by cutting it away. Silicone tubing is not appropriate for tube sealing, as it is not a thermoplastic.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The three functional categories of connectors:

  • Adapter type: Hose barbs are used for tube-to-tube or tube-to-equipment connections; tri-clamps are used for standard ports on stainless steel equipment or filters; overmolded connections provide a smooth, seamless inner wall.
  • Flow path diameter: Many standard diameters are available, but it may be hard to find a suitable connector for a nonstandard diameter.
  • Aseptic connection/disconnection: Nonaseptic connectors are used for preassembly before sterilization; aseptic connectors are used to join two subassemblies that have already been gamma sterilized.

Tube Welder: Joins two lengths of thermoplastic tubing into one by heating, cutting, aligning and fusing. The weld needs no connector parts; the thermoplastic material itself forms the seal.

Tube Sealer: Uses heat or energy to fuse the lumen of thermoplastic tubing closed, then cuts it. It is used for aseptic disconnection.

Key Notes

An important limitation of silicone tubing: TR66 states explicitly that silicone tubing cannot be used for tube welding or tube sealing, because silicone is not a thermoplastic. This means:

  • If your process uses a peristaltic pump (which commonly uses silicone tubing), the tubing outside the pump segment must be thermoplastic (such as C-Flex or PharMed BPT) for welding/sealing to be possible
  • When designing a SUS tubing system, decide from the start which locations will need welding/sealing, and use thermoplastic tubing at those locations

Risk considerations in connector selection: With hose barb connectors, watch for leak risk and flow path constriction (which affects flow rate and creates turbulence). Overmolded connections give a smoother inner wall but cost more to manufacture.

Analogy

SUS connection technologies are like the ways of joining plumbing pipes:

  • Hose barb = a household quick-connect pipe fitting: convenient, but may leak
  • Tube welder = heat-fusing two pipes together: the strongest joint, but irreversible
  • Aseptic connector = an aerospace-grade sealed coupling that ensures no outside air enters at the moment of opening
  • Tube sealer = melting the pipe end shut with a heat gun

Each method suits particular situations; the choice depends on whether aseptic operation is required, whether the connection must be remade later, and the flow rate and pressure requirements.

Practice Questions

  1. You are designing a SUS that must aseptically connect an additional sampling bag partway through the process. Which type of connection technology should you choose, and why?
  2. Table 4.7.1.1-2🔍 says Fitness for Use requires confirming that "connectors are qualified for use at pressures required in process". If a connector's pressure rating is insufficient, what is the most likely consequence?
  3. Why are overmolded connections preferred over hose barb connectors in some applications? Analyze from the perspectives of fluid dynamics and sterility assurance.

4.8 Materials of Construction

Original Text

Factors concerning the materials used for constructing the SUS may include inherent safety, traceability, physical properties, and compatibility with other components or product and process materials. The components used for the construction of SUSs are primarily comprised of plastic materials that may vary according to polymer class, molecular weight, and additives. This variability confers a wide range of performance characteristics on the plastic materials. Table 4.8-1🔍 contains a list of plastics commonly used for the production of SU components, along with their trade names, chemical abbreviations, properties, and applications.

Table 4.8-1🔍 Plastics Commonly Used in SUS

Fluoropolymers

Polycarbonate

Polyester

Polyolefin (Polyalkene)

Copolymers

Polyamides (Nylons)

PVC / Vinyl

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Four key considerations in SUS material selection:

  • Inherent Safety: Whether the material itself is safe. For example, PVC may contain DEHP (a phthalate plasticizer) and produces dioxins when incinerated
  • Traceability: Whether the source, lot and processing history of the material can be traced
  • Physical Properties: Strength, flexibility, temperature resistance, transparency and so on
  • Compatibility: Chemical compatibility with the product, process fluids and other components

Extractables & Leachables, E&L: One of the most critical factors in SUS material evaluation. Extractables are substances that can be extracted from the material under extreme conditions; leachables are substances that migrate into the product under actual conditions of use. E&L profiles differ enormously between materials.

Key Notes

Table 4.8-1🔍 contains several material limitations you must remember:

  • PTFE cannot be gamma sterilized: Although PTFE (Teflon) is highly chemically inert, it cannot be sterilized with gamma radiation. This is a major constraint in SUS design, because gamma sterilization is the most common sterilization method for SUS.
  • Gamma sterilization limits for PP: Only stabilized formulations of PP can be gamma sterilized. Unstabilized PP has a reduced shelf life and increased extractables after gamma irradiation.
  • Effect of EVA on pH: Gamma-irradiated EVA lowers the pH of the contacting liquid. For pH-sensitive biologics, this is a risk that must be verified during process development.
  • BPA from PC: Some grades of polycarbonate may leach bisphenol A (BPA), which multiple studies have indicated may have adverse effects.
  • DEHP and environmental issues with PVC: PVC used in fluid-contact applications must be confirmed free of DEHP; incineration produces dioxins, which must be considered as waste disposal regulations become stricter.

Analogy

Choosing SUS materials of construction is like choosing food storage containers at the supermarket:

  • Glass container (PTFE) = the most inert and safest, but cannot go in the microwave (cannot be gamma sterilized) and breaks easily (poor mechanical properties)
  • Stainless steel lunch box (PVDF) = excellent all round but more expensive
  • PP food container = common and handy, but you must confirm it is the "microwave safe" grade (stabilized formulation)
  • PE plastic bag = cheap and flexible, but cannot be heated (cannot be autoclaved)
  • PVC cling film = the cheapest but may contain plasticizers, and its disposal raises environmental concerns

Practical Application

In day-to-day CDMO work, material selection directly affects:

  • The scope and cost of E&L studies: Different materials have different extractables profiles and need targeted analytical methods. PVDF and PE generally have lower E&L, while EVA and PVC need more extensive E&L assessment.
  • Multi-layer film design logic: Modern SUS bags usually use multi-layer coextruded film, for example an inner layer of low-E&L PE/PVDF (product contact), a middle layer of EVOH (gas barrier) and an outer layer of PA (mechanical strength). Understanding the function of each layer helps in assessing risk.
  • Regulatory considerations: The EU and FDA have different control requirements for certain substances (such as BPA and DEHP). Material selection must satisfy the regulations of the target markets at the same time.

Practice Questions

  1. You need to select a material for a SUS filter housing with these requirements: gamma sterilizable, acid/alkali resistant (pH 2-12) and low extractables. From Table 4.8-1🔍, which material would you choose, and why do you rule out the other options?
  2. Why do modern SUS bags generally use multi-layer coextruded film rather than a single material? Explain from the perspectives of gas barrier, mechanical strength and E&L.
  3. A biologics storage bag uses an EVA inner layer, and after gamma sterilization you find that the pH of the stored solution has dropped by 0.3 units. Is this consistent with what Table 4.8-1🔍 would lead you to expect? How should you assess the impact on product quality?
PDA Technical Report No. 66 (2014) — Single-Use Systems | p29–p39

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the material selection and integrity considerations for single-use systems (SUS) in storage and freezing operations
  • Understand the application characteristics of SUS in filtration, centrifugation and chromatography unit operations
  • Understand the dose control and environmental requirements for using SUS in drug product final filling
  • Become familiar with aseptic transfer technology for SUS in isolators and restricted access barrier systems (RABS)
  • Understand the practical considerations for SUS sampling, transportation and sensors

4.9 Storage

Original Text

Appropriate storage is necessary in order to maintain the quality of process solutions (e.g., buffers and culture media), intermediates, and bulk drug substance until they are ready for further processing. Long-term storage of process intermediates often requires rapid, controlled freezing or cryogenic preservation. The storage bag must be qualified for the specific use, with a focus on maintaining bag integrity at the lower of the storage or freezing temperatures.

4.9.1 Technological Examples

A storage bag with a polyethylene fluid contact layer is normally used to store aqueous-based liquid intermediates. Polyethylene polymers (particularly low-density grades) have broad chemical compatibility and very low reactivity to most intermediates and process fluids. Other materials like fluoropolymers are used to address specific process compatibility concerns.

Typical plastics used for freezing applications include poly(tetrafluoroethylene) (PTFE) and poly(ethylene vinyl acetate) (EVA). These materials are particularly good at retaining their mechanical strength at very low freezing temperatures. Specific bag systems have been integrated into commercially available controlled freeze-thaw systems. A gas barrier such as ethylene vinyl alcohol (EVOH) is typically used with EVA to create a multilayered composite film that offers mechanical strength and resistance to gas permeation.

For short-term storage, process intermediates in standard bags are normally kept at ambient temperature or in a refrigerator. Long-term storage of process intermediates often requires rapid, controlled freezing or cryogenic preservation (refer to Section 4.10, Freezing). Factors that affect the storage of process intermediates are summarized in Table 4.9.1-1.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Storage: In biopharmaceutical processes, storage is the "buffer zone" that links the unit operations. Buffers, culture media and process intermediates all have to be held under suitable conditions so that their quality attributes are maintained until the next processing step.

Polyethylene Fluid Contact Layer: PE (especially LDPE, low-density polyethylene) is the most common fluid contact material for SUS bags because of its high chemical inertness and very low reactivity, which suits most aqueous intermediates.

EVOH Gas Barrier: EVA bags combined with EVOH form a multilayer composite film that provides both mechanical strength at low temperature and a barrier to gas permeation. This is especially important for oxygen-sensitive protein drugs.

Analogy

Think of buying fresh food at the supermarket: some items can stay at room temperature (short-term storage), some must go in the refrigerator (chilled storage), and the most delicate need the freezer (frozen storage). The material of the container also matters: plastic boxes, vacuum bags and foil bags each give a different barrier effect. Choosing SUS storage bags follows the same logic: select the material and temperature conditions according to the sensitivity of the intermediate.

Key Notes

Leachables risk: During long-term storage, chemicals in the polymer materials may leach into the process fluid. These leachables can affect not only the quality of the intermediate but can also be "carried" into downstream unit operations and impact the final product. A storage compatibility study is therefore required.

Protection against light and oxygen: Intermediates containing light-sensitive or oxygen-sensitive components need special protective films (such as light-blocking or oxygen-barrier films), which a standard PE bag may not provide.

Practice Questions

  1. Why is low-density polyethylene (LDPE) particularly suitable as the fluid contact layer of SUS storage bags? Which process requirements does its chemical inertness relate to?
  2. If your process intermediate is an oxygen-sensitive monoclonal antibody, how would you select the film layer structure of the storage bag?
  3. For long-term storage, what key test items should a leachables assessment include?

4.10 Freezing

Original Text

The end user may use freezing techniques to separate process pools or intermediates and hold them for a short time at the process temperature and conditions or for a longer duration before another production step. For long durations, the end user may freeze the intermediate directly in an SUS that is designed for freezing. Uncontrolled freezing is performed in standard laboratory freezers, purpose-designed blast cells, or nitrogen freezing chambers. Controlled freeze-and-thaw equipment (e.g., a plate freezer) is used to regulate the rate of freezing and thawing of the contents of each SU bag. To maintain the integrity of the bag containing the frozen process intermediates or bulk drug substances, careful attention must be paid to the reusable storage container design and logistics of shipping and handling.

4.10.1 Technological Examples

  • Unprotected small-scale bag: It is common practice to freeze an unprotected bag (less than 20 L) that is not specifically designed for the application. Compared to other technologies, the failure rates of frozen unprotected bags tend to be higher (Table 4.10.1-1🔍).
  • Separate bag shell — The purpose of a bag shell is to maintain the structural integrity of a frozen bag by protecting it from bumps and drops during handling at low temperatures. Bag shells are made of either rigid plastics or rigid foam construction.
  • Integrated shell-protected bag — The shell design includes a tubing and fitting compartment for security and storage at the most common point of failure. Off-the-shelf International Safe Transit Association (ISTA) and ASTM-qualified shipping containers can also be used.
  • Controlled freeze-thaw system, bag, frame, and logistics equipment — A controlled freeze-thaw system uses contact freezing technology to allow for the reproducible freezing of the contents of the bag. Contact freezing plates maintain freezing distances by maintaining the filled bag between two freezing contact plates. In addition, the rate of heat transfer from the bags is increased in a controlled freeze-thaw system, and this decreases the time required for freezing. Thus, a controlled freeze-thaw system is part of an integrated refrigeration system for maintaining the integrity of the bag and the quality of its contents during fill, transport, freezing, and thawing.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Controlled Freeze-Thaw: Unlike uncontrolled freezing in an ordinary freezer, controlled freeze-thaw systems use contact freezing plates to control the freezing rate precisely. This is especially important for biological drugs (such as proteins and antibodies), because non-uniform freezing causes local concentration effects (cryoconcentration), which may lead to protein aggregation or degradation.

Glass Transition Temperature (Tg): At typical storage temperatures of -40°C to -70°C, most polymer materials fall below their Tg and become brittle. This is why SUS for freezing need special materials such as PTFE or EVA that retain mechanical strength at low temperature.

Analogy

It is like transporting an ice sculpture: you would not carry it in an ordinary cardboard box; you need a dedicated protective shell and cushioning. Freezing bags are the same: an unprotected small bag becomes brittle at low temperature and can crack at the slightest knock (like an ice sculpture shattering). An integrated shell-protected bag is the custom-made shipping case for the ice sculpture.

Key Notes

Personnel training is essential: The document specifically stresses that "effective employee training is essential for successful implementation of SUS freeze-thaw steps". Freezing bags are very fragile at low temperature, and improper handling by operators (such as dropping or moving them abruptly) is one of the main causes of bag breakage.

Tubing connections are the most common failure point: Integrated shell designs specifically include protective compartments for tubing and connectors, because these connection points are the most likely to leak or break at low temperature.

Practical Application

Suppose you are a CDMO setting up a frozen storage process for a customer's monoclonal antibody bulk drug substance. You need to consider the following:

  • Select EVA/EVOH multilayer freezing bags to ensure mechanical strength at -70°C
  • Invest in controlled freeze-thaw equipment (such as Sartorius or CryoFin systems) to ensure a reproducible freezing profile
  • Establish an SOP to train operators in the correct handling of freezing bags
  • Plan a cold chain transportation scheme using ISTA/ASTM-qualified shipping containers

Practice Questions

  1. Why is the failure rate of uncontrolled freeze-thaw higher in unprotected small bags? Explain from both the materials science and the operational perspective.
  2. How does the "contact freezing plate" of a controlled freeze-thaw system improve freezing uniformity? What does this mean for protein quality?

4.11 Filtration

Original Text

Normal flow filtration (NFF) or direct flow filtration (DFF) devices are used in biopharmaceutical manufacturing to remove contaminants such as particulates, microbial cells, and virus particles. Cross flow filtration (CFF) or tangential flow filtration (TFF) devices are used to reduce the volume of a buffer and/or perform buffer exchange as part of the downstream process.

4.11.1 Technological Examples

CFF devices are commercially available in the following structural formats: flat-sheet cassettes, and hollow fiber cartridges. Currently, only a hollow-fiber cartridge can be classified as an SU filter device. Structurally, there is little difference between SU and MU CFF hollow fiber devices. Functionally, an SU CFF hollow fiber device provides a higher degree of cleanliness and sterility compared to that of a SU flat sheet device. Current flat sheet devices marketed as single-use contain a disposable flow path (e.g., ports, gaskets, and the filter device), which is then assembled into a stainless steel holder that provides the compression and support required for filtration. NFF devices have been commercially available in an SU format for many years, and structurally, they contain a depth filter media or membrane for separation via size exclusion.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

NFF vs CFF (normal flow filtration vs cross flow filtration):

  • NFF (Normal Flow Filtration): The liquid passes perpendicular through the filter membrane, and is used to remove particles, microorganisms and virus particles. Both depth filters and sterilizing-grade filters fall into this category.
  • CFF (Cross Flow Filtration): The liquid flows parallel to the membrane surface, and is used for concentration and buffer exchange. In downstream purification it is often called TFF (Tangential Flow Filtration).

Disposable Flow Path: Current SU flat-sheet CFF equipment is in fact "semi-single-use": the product-contact flow path (ports, gaskets, cassettes) is single-use, but the stainless steel holder used for compression and support is reusable.

Analogy

NFF is like a coffee filter: water passes straight down through the paper and the grounds are retained on top. CFF is like a dialysis machine: blood flows along the membrane tube, small molecules pass through the membrane wall and are filtered out, and large molecules keep flowing back to the body. These two completely different filtration principles determine their different roles in the process.

Key Notes

Impact of gamma sterilization vs steam sterilization on integrity testing: This is a very important point that is easy to overlook. Traditional PUPSIT (pre-use post-sterilization integrity testing) arose from the risk that steam sterilization may damage the filter (for example, high temperature causing membrane shrinkage or seal failure). Gamma irradiation does not have these damage mechanisms, so from a "technical perspective" the need to perform PUPSIT on gamma-sterilized sterilizing-grade filters is lower.

Note, however, that from a regulatory standpoint many regulatory authorities still require PUPSIT regardless of the sterilization method. This is an area where the technical view and the regulatory requirement may not fully align.

Practice Questions

  1. Why is hollow fiber CFF more readily classified as fully single-use equipment than flat-sheet CFF? Think from the structural design perspective.
  2. If you are converting a traditional stainless steel filtration system to SUS, what is the most important item to assess?
  3. Do gamma-sterilized SU filters need no integrity testing at all? Distinguish the different meanings of pre-use and post-use testing.

4.12 Centrifugation

Original Text

Centrifugation is often used for cell harvest as an alternative to filtration, and SU formats of this technology are emerging for low shear separations, including harvest clarification; cell concentration, wash, and harvest; and cell separation. These systems utilize a gamma-irradiated single-use module or polymeric liner for product contact and, as such, eliminate the need for CIP or manual cleaning and SIP or hot water for injection (WFI) for bioburden sterilization and control of traditional centrifuges. Flow rates vary depending on the manufacture and design of the equipment, ranging from <10 to 100 L per minute at <100X to 8000X G force. Continuous and batch centrifugation technologies are available.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

SU Centrifugation: Traditional centrifuges require complex CIP/SIP procedures to clean and sterilize the product contact surfaces. SU centrifugation systems use gamma pre-sterilized single-use modules or polymer liners, which directly eliminates the need for cleaning and sterilization.

Range of application: Mainly low-shear separation operations, including harvest clarification, cell concentration/washing/harvesting, and cell separation. Flow rates range from <10 L/min to 100 L/min, and G forces from <100X to 8000X.

Analogy

A traditional centrifuge is like the large dishwasher in a restaurant: it must be thoroughly cleaned and disinfected after every use. An SU centrifugation module is like disposable tableware: it is discarded after use, saving the time and labor cost of cleaning. For a CDMO, this means much shorter changeover time between batches.

Key Notes

Emerging technology: The document uses the word "emerging" to describe SU centrifugation, indicating that this was still a relatively new field when it was written in 2014. More commercial products have appeared since (such as kSep and Carr Centritech), but when selecting one you still need to evaluate its processing capacity, the effect of shear on the product, and the cost-effectiveness of the single-use modules.

Practice Questions

  1. Compared with traditional centrifugation, what advantages does SU centrifugation offer in changeover efficiency and control of cross-contamination risk?
  2. Why does the document stress that SU centrifugation is especially suitable for "low-shear separation"? What effect can high shear have on biological products?

4.13 Chromatography

Original Text

Biomolecules are purified using chromatographic techniques that separate them according to differences in their specific physical or chemical properties. SU formats for various chromatography steps are available.

4.13.1 Technological Examples

Chromatographic media can be divided into two main categories: resin- or bead-based and membrane-based. Membrane-based chromatographic devices are also known as membrane adsorbers, and because they are membrane-based, they share similar physical and structural features with traditional filter devices. In order to produce membrane-based chromatographic devices with specific properties, the membrane is chemically derivatized with ligands.

Disposable bead-based chromatographic devices tend to contain the same resins and have the same general physical structure as traditional packed-bed columns that are made of glass and stainless steel. Bead-based chromatographic devices are commercially available as prepacked units made of plastic structural parts. They are useful for product campaigning because they can be used more than once. As with traditional packed-bed columns, sterilization methods such as gamma irradiation or moist heat cannot be used with disposable bead-based chromatographic devices; instead, rigorous chemical sanitization is used. As with multiple-use columns, the preservative solution in the SU device must be flushed before the unit is used. Conventional chromatography and SU chromatography both require large amounts of process fluids like buffers (refer to Section 4.8.1, Fluid Management, and Section 4.17, Transportation).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Resin-based vs Membrane-based Chromatography:

  • Resin-based (bead-based): Uses the same packed resin as traditional chromatography columns; only the column material changes from glass/stainless steel to plastic. Pre-packed columns can be reused (campaign mode) and suit capture steps that require high binding capacity.
  • Membrane-based (membrane adsorbers): The membrane serves as the support and is chemically modified to carry a ligand. Flow rates are high but binding capacity is low, so they are used mainly for polishing steps, such as removing residual DNA, HCP or viruses.

Chemical Sanitization: Unlike other SUS, pre-packed resin columns cannot be sterilized by gamma irradiation or moist heat (which would damage the resin), so they must be rigorously cleaned and sanitized with chemical agents such as NaOH.

Analogy

A resin-based chromatography column is like premium drip-bag coffee served in a disposable plastic cup: the coffee grounds (resin) are as good as those used in a traditional ceramic pot, only the outer container is now plastic. Membrane-based chromatography is like the filter cartridge of a quick water filter jug: water flows through quickly, which suits final polishing but not large-scale initial separation.

Key Notes

Scalability is critical: The document specifically reminds users to ensure that the chosen method can be scaled up from SU scale to a larger SUS, or transferred back to multi-use systems (MUS). This is especially important for a CDMO, since a customer's product may need to scale from small-batch production to commercial scale.

Buffer volumes: Both traditional and SU chromatography require large volumes of buffer, which involves fluid management (4.8.1) and transportation (4.17). For a CDMO, buffer preparation and supply chain management are an important operational consideration.

Practice Questions

  1. Why are membrane adsorbers suited to polishing steps rather than capture steps? Explain from the perspectives of flow rate and binding capacity.
  2. When running campaign manufacturing with pre-packed SU chromatography columns, what cleaning and sanitization risks need attention?
  3. From a CDMO perspective, what supply chain and inventory management challenges does using SU chromatography columns pose compared with traditional stainless steel columns?

4.14 Drug Product Final Filling

Original Text

The impact of any failures in final fill operations is high, since further processing cannot reduce quality failures at this stage. Common strategies for reducing the risk of failure include performing filling operations in aseptic environments, use of physical barrier systems to separate operators from the process, and validating the sterility of process components (refer to Section 4.15, Isolators).

SU components are widely used in product final filling operations. For example, flexible plastic tubing is used between the filling pump head and needle. Another example is a peristaltic filling system, which employs disposable peristaltic tubing inside the pump head. Both types of tubing are normally replaced between batches.

4.14.1 Technological Examples

An SU dosing system comprises a product hold bag, supply tubing, and a filling needle. These components are assembled in a cleanroom and sterilized using gamma irradiation. The complete assembly is removed from its outer pouch and connected to the filling machine immediately before use. The filling machine may be as simple as a single pump or may contain multiple pumps to achieve commercial production speeds.

SU filling systems are controlled volumetrically or gravimetrically.

  • Volumetric dosing system — This type of system involves a volumetric system that consists of a peristaltic pump and specialized tubing, within which flows the product to be dispensed. Peristaltic pumps work by mechanically compressing the tubing within the pump head to push a specified volume of fluid through the dosing path. Accurate dosing depends on factors such as the mechanical life of the pump tubing. A positive displacement pump-based system relies on the use of either a rotary-piston or rolling-diaphragm pump design. In both cases, fluid is forced out of the pump by the action of the piston. Accurate dosing depends on the precision of the piston stroke length.
  • Gravimetric dosing system — A gravimetric system is normally used in low-throughput applications. It uses an optical sensor to dose a specified weight of the product based on the calculation of the interior volume of a given length of tubing or glass. The entire product path comprises SU components. The accuracy and precision of the system with aqueous solutions is comparable to that of other dosing systems. The dosing speed and accuracy are directly related to the fluid temperature and viscosity. Dosing time is the speed at which a liquid will flow through the tubing based on gravity; thus, viscosity influences dosing time. Relatively small temperature changes over the course of a filling event can affect product viscosity enough to have a significant effect on the dose volume.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Drug Product Final Filling: This is one of the highest-risk steps in the entire production process. The document opens by stating: "the impact of any failure in final filling operations is high since subsequent processing cannot reduce quality defects at this stage". In other words, once filled, there is no way to "put it right".

Volumetric vs Gravimetric:

  • Volumetric: Peristaltic or positive displacement pumps (rotary piston/rolling diaphragm), where the volume is controlled by the degree of pump tubing compression. Faster, but accuracy is affected by tubing aging.
  • Gravimetric: Uses optical sensors and gravity to calculate the fill weight from the volume in the tubing. High accuracy but lower throughput, suited to low-volume applications.

Analogy

Volumetric filling is like pouring water with a measuring cup: you measure a fixed volume against the graduation mark, but the cup itself may deform with use (like the systematic drift caused by pump tubing aging). Gravimetric filling is like weighing precisely on an electronic scale: it is unaffected by container shape, but you have to wait for the scale to settle each time, so it is slower.

Key Notes

Pump tubing life and dose drift: The document specifically mentions "polymeric parts (such as pump tubing) that can lead to a systematic drift in dosing volume". This is a key challenge of SUS filling systems: peristaltic pump tubing gradually deforms (fatigues) under repeated compression, so the volume delivered by each compression slowly drifts. An in-line weight check must be established for real-time monitoring.

VHP compatibility: If the SUS is to be placed in an isolator or RABS decontaminated with VPHP (vapor-phase hydrogen peroxide), the compatibility of the SUS materials with VPHP must be confirmed, including shadowing and permeability.

Endotoxin and particulate control: The filling system must meet multi-pharmacopoeia requirements such as USP <788> (particulate matter) and USP <85> (endotoxin).

Practical Application

Decision matrix for a CDMO evaluating SU filling systems:

  • High-volume products (>100,000 vials/batch): A traditional stainless steel system with SU tubing may still be needed, with multiple peristaltic pump channels in parallel to increase throughput
  • Low-volume/high-value products: A fully SU gravimetric system maximizes accuracy and minimizes cross-contamination risk
  • Campaign manufacturing: Confirm that the service life of the SU components covers the whole campaign period

Practice Questions

  1. Why is final filling regarded as an "irreversible quality gate"? Explain from the perspective of sterility assurance.
  2. How does the "systematic dose drift" of peristaltic pump tubing affect filling accuracy? What monitoring strategy would you recommend?
  3. When transferring SU filling assemblies into an isolator, which VPHP-related risks need to be verified?

4.15 Isolators

Original Text

If the canister of a traditional alpha-beta (A-B) port is replaced with an SU bag or connector, SU technology can be used for the aseptic transfer of liquids, powders, stoppers, plungers, tools, materials, and waste into and out of isolators or restricted-access barrier systems (RABS). To avoid compromising the sterility of the isolator, SU bags are designed to prevent reuse. Categories of an SU product format include:

  • Gamma-sterilizable bag with mating A-B connector — Manufacturers of stoppers use this as packaging for prewashed, siliconized, and sterile ready-to-use stoppers or plungers.
  • Gamma-sterilizable bag with mating A-B connector and tubing set — This bag design includes one or more tubing sets for the transfer of liquids into the isolator or RABS. These are normally available as stand-alone components or as part of a presterilized SUS.
  • Autoclavable bag with mating A-B connector — Manufacturers of stoppers use autoclavable bags as packaging for prewashed, siliconized, and ready-to-sterilize stoppers or plungers. In addition, autoclavable bags are used for in-house stopper processing, tool, and component transfer.
  • A-B connector — A connector is used for liquid transfer. Connectors can be used with either an SUS or an MUS because they can be sterilized by gamma irradiation or autoclaving.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Alpha-Beta (A-B) Port: This is the aseptic transfer port on an isolator. The "Alpha" part is mounted on the isolator wall and the "Beta" part on the transfer container (traditionally a stainless steel canister). Once the two parts are docked, they are opened so that materials can enter or leave without breaching the sterile environment of the isolator. The SUS innovation is to replace the traditional stainless steel Beta canister with a single-use bag, greatly simplifying cleaning and sterilization.

Aseptic Transfer: In an isolator or RABS environment, any movement of material in or out is a potential breach of the sterile barrier. SU bags are designed not to be reused, which avoids the cross-contamination risk that reuse could bring.

Analogy

An A-B port is like the docking port of a space station: the spacecraft (Beta part/SU bag) must dock precisely with the space station (Alpha part/isolator) so that supplies can be passed across without letting outer space (the non-sterile environment) in. Replacing the stainless steel canister with an SU bag is like changing from a reusable space cargo module to a disposable space package: simpler, but you must ensure that every package is fully compatible with the docking port.

Key Notes

Vendor lock-in risk: The document explicitly states that "commercially available aseptic transfer ports have complementary bags that must be obtained from the same suppliers". This means that once you choose one brand of A-B port, you must use that brand's matching bags, creating vendor lock-in. This is a challenge for a CDMO's supply chain diversification strategy.

Anti-static film: Anti-static film may be needed when transferring powders or small parts, to prevent loss or contamination caused by static adhesion.

Practice Questions

  1. Why are SU transfer bags designed to "prevent reuse"? Analyze its importance from the perspective of sterility assurance.
  2. Which types of SU transfer bags are suited to gamma sterilization and which to autoclaving? What are the advantages and disadvantages of each?
  3. When selecting an A-B port system, how do you balance the "vendor lock-in" risk against the need for "system compatibility"?

4.16 Sampling and Laboratory Analysis

Original Text

Sampling is the process of removing a volume of process fluid from an SU assembly. This sample fluid is then usually submitted for laboratory analysis.

4.16.1 Technological Examples

Techniques for obtaining a sample fluid include:

  • Use of a sampling port with or without the aid of a needle to collect the sample
  • Use of a Luer-containing sampling bag or via the connection of a Luer-style syringe to a Luer adapter on the bag
  • Use of a preassembled manifold of sampling bags that may be filled and detached from the assembly one at a time using crimping tools or a tube sealer
  • Use of an SU sample valve integrated with one or more sample bags

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Sampling in SUS: Sampling is a basic quality control operation, but it poses unique challenges in a SUS environment. Every sampling event is an "intrusion" into the sterile barrier, so the sampling design must balance analytical needs against system integrity.

Representative Sample: The document specifically stresses attention to "stagnant parts" in the tubing. Before sampling, the stagnant liquid must be flushed out so that the sample represents the actual state of the process fluid.

Analogy

SUS sampling is like taking items out of a sealed cleanroom: every time the door is opened, the contamination risk increases. A pre-assembled sampling manifold is like a series of "safety drawers": only one drawer is pulled out at a time for sampling, then sealed and cut off after use, without affecting the other drawers or the sterility of the main system.

Key Notes

Limitations of alcohol sanitization: The document explicitly states that "using alcohol to sanitize a port before entry does not ensure sterility": alcohol wiping only reduces bioburden and cannot guarantee sterility. Note also that alcohol may contaminate the sampled liquid and affect the analytical results.

Sample quality verification: The SU sampling containers (bags, tubing) may themselves cause positive or negative interference with the analytical method, or cause sample degradation. These factors must be evaluated before the sampling method is established.

Practice Questions

  1. When designing a SUS sampling plan, why is it necessary to determine the required number of samples in advance? How does this affect the SUS design?
  2. How do "stagnant parts" in the tubing affect sample representativeness? What operational measures would you recommend to address this?
  3. For critical process samples that need long-term storage, how should the effect of the SU container on sample stability be verified?

4.17 Transportation

Original Text

Transportation is the movement of process solutions such as buffers, culture media, WFI, and process intermediates either within a facility or between facilities.

4.17.1 Technological Examples

The supply of process solutions in large-volumes bags (≤1000 L) from the point of manufacture to the point of use is a well-established practice involving the following components:

  • A bag designed to fit a rigid-wall outer container
  • A rigid-wall outer container such as a plastic drum or tote, or a stainless steel bin
  • Secondary packaging materials (e.g., dunnage) and lids or other mechanical devices to suppress the fluid wave action in the bioprocess bag

Transportation of process solutions in bags in small volumes (nominally ≤20 L) is an equally well-established process that requires less complex packaging solutions. The exception is the transportation of frozen material, which requires special temperature-resistant materials and processes (e.g., bags, shells and secondary packaging materials, operating procedures, and cold chain logistics; refer to Section 4.11, Freezing).

Shipping systems must be qualified for their intended use through proper design and testing in consultation with a packaging engineer. The ISTA and ASTM D4169 are good references for testing standards.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Transportation in SUS: Transportation covers not only long-distance transport between sites but also material movement between different rooms or cleanrooms within a site. SUS transportation must take into account multiple factors, including bag protection, suppression of fluid movement, environmental control and maintenance of cleanliness grades.

Progressive Removal of Packaging: When moving SUS bags from a non-classified area into a cleanroom, the outer packaging must be removed layer by layer, with wiping/disinfection at each grade transition point, to ensure that items entering a higher cleanliness grade do not carry in contaminants.

Analogy

This is like the hospital concept of "layer-by-layer disinfection": when surgical instruments are brought into the operating room from outside, they pass through successive stages of unwrapping and disinfection. The outermost wrapping is removed in the corridor, the next layer is removed and wiped in the buffer area, and the sterile pack is opened only inside the operating room. SUS transportation and transfer across cleanliness grades follow the same logic.

Key Notes

Wave Action: During transportation of large bags (up to 1000 L), vigorous sloshing of the liquid can stress the bag seams and tubing connections and increase the risk of leakage. Lids or mechanical devices are therefore needed to suppress the movement.

Existing vs new facilities: The document specifically notes that "material management capability may vary significantly between an existing facility and a new facility". Older facilities may lack sufficient staging areas, corridor space and waste handling capacity to support the large packaging and waste streams of SUS. This is a problem that many plants easily underestimate when introducing SUS.

Practical Application

As a CDMO facility planner, before introducing SUS you need to assess:

  • Staging space: SUS components are bulky but light, and need more storage area than traditional stainless steel systems
  • Waste handling: The large volume of plastic waste after use needs dedicated handling and removal routes
  • Personnel and material flow: Whether the handling routes for large outer containers (totes, drums) are compatible with existing corridors and door widths
  • Forklift access: Ensure forklifts can reach the required areas and that outer containers can be stacked for storage

Practice Questions

  1. Why does the "wave effect" of liquid in the bag need to be suppressed during transportation? How does it relate to bag integrity?
  2. When transferring from one cleanliness grade to another (grade C to B), how should the "progressive removal of packaging" procedure be designed?
  3. What material management challenges may arise when integrating SUS into an existing facility?

4.18 Sensors

Original Text

An SU sensor enhances the functionality of an SUS by providing parametric data that allows for manual or automated process control or monitoring. Modern sensors can be directly integrated into SU systems, and traditional sensors equipped with a fluid-path barrier can be used to measure factors such as temperature and pressure. A hybrid sensor uses a combination of a sensor and a transducer. Although sensor and transducer technologies have been available in many industries for a considerable time, the application of SU sensor technology in bioprocessing is relatively recent.

4.18.1 Technological Examples

SU sensor technology is routinely used to measure factors such as temperature, pressure, and conductivity. Recently, advanced sensors for measuring pH, dissolved oxygen, and CO2, as well as other sensors that employ chemical and/or optical technologies, have become available in an SU format. As the use of SU sensors becomes more widespread, challenges such as their compatibility with sterilization methods and the physical design of the SUS are beginning to emerge.

The dynamic range, accuracy, and robustness of a particular sensor must be taken into account during sensor evaluation for a particular SUS application. Other factors to consider are the need for a common sensing platform across several unit operations (i.e., from small- to large-scale operations); the impact of employing multiple sensing technologies, each with different quantification attributes (such as range, accuracy, and precision); and assessment of sensor calibration requirements and the ease of performing a post-use verification.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

SU Sensor: Sensors are the "eyes and ears" of SUS, providing process parameter data for manual or automated process control and monitoring. SU sensors come in three forms:

  • Directly integrated: The sensor is embedded directly in the SU bag or tubing and discarded together with the system
  • Flow path barrier: A traditional sensor is paired with a flow path barrier (such as a membrane), so the probe does not contact the product directly
  • Hybrid: Combines sensor and transducer, partly single-use and partly reusable

Routine measured parameters: Temperature, pressure and conductivity are mature; SU sensors for pH, dissolved oxygen (DO) and CO2 are newer and use chemical or optical technologies.

Analogy

A sensor in a traditional stainless steel system is like the fixed thermometer and hygrometer at home: installed once, used repeatedly and calibrated regularly. An SU sensor is like a disposable medical thermometer: it is discarded after use, which removes the cross-contamination risk and the recalibration effort, but it costs more and you must ensure that every unit is accurate.

Key Notes

Calibration challenges: Traditional sensors can undergo a full multi-point calibration before use. Because SU sensors are single-use, the calibration strategy is different: some rely on factory pre-calibration and some support simplified pre-use calibration. The ease of post-use verification is also an important evaluation factor.

Consistency across unit operations: The document stresses considering whether the same sensing platform can be used across multiple unit operations. If different steps use different sensing technologies (each with a different measuring range, precision and accuracy), the comparability of data and the consistency of the process control strategy will be affected.

Sterilization compatibility: SU sensors must withstand the chosen sterilization method (usually gamma irradiation) and still maintain measurement accuracy afterwards, which is a major technical challenge.

Practice Questions

  1. How do SU pH sensors differ from traditional glass pH electrodes in calibration and accuracy? What are the advantages and disadvantages of each?
  2. Why should a "common platform across unit operations" be considered when selecting SU sensors? What does this mean for data integrity?
  3. Which characteristics of optical sensors may be affected by gamma sterilization? How would you verify sensor performance after sterilization?

Section Summary

  • Storage (4.9): A PE fluid contact layer is the most common choice; oxygen-sensitive or light-sensitive intermediates need special protective films (such as an EVOH gas barrier layer). Leachables assessment is critical for long-term storage.
  • Freezing (4.10): Controlled freeze-thaw systems use contact freezing plates to ensure uniform freezing; at -40°C to -70°C most polymer materials fall below their glass transition temperature and become brittle. Tubing connections are the most common failure point, and personnel training is essential.
  • Filtration (4.11): NFF and hollow fiber CFF are best suited to the SU format. For gamma-sterilized filters the need for PUPSIT is technically lower (unlike steam sterilization), but regulators may still require it.
  • Centrifugation (4.12): SU centrifugation is an emerging technology that uses gamma pre-sterilized single-use modules/liners, eliminating the need for CIP/SIP, and suits low-shear harvest and clarification operations.
  • Chromatography (4.13): Divided into resin-based (pre-packed columns, usable in campaigns, requiring chemical sanitization) and membrane-based (membrane adsorbers, high flow rate and low capacity, used for polishing steps). Scalability and buffer requirements are key considerations.
  • Final filling (4.14): The highest-risk step: quality defects cannot be remedied after filling. The volumetric and gravimetric dose control methods each have advantages and disadvantages; systematic dose drift from peristaltic pump tubing life is a challenge specific to SUS.
  • Isolators (4.15): SU bags replace the traditional stainless steel Beta canister for aseptic transfer through A-B ports. Vendor lock-in is an important supply chain risk.
  • Sampling (4.16): Every sampling event is an intrusion into the sterile barrier. Pre-assembled sampling manifolds reduce the risk; attention is needed to the effect of stagnant parts in the tubing on sample representativeness and to potential interference of SU containers with analytical methods.
  • Transportation (4.17): Covers both on-site and inter-site transport; suppression of fluid movement in large bags, progressive removal of packaging across cleanliness grades, and the material management capability of existing facilities (staging space, waste handling) are often underestimated challenges.
  • Sensors (4.18): SU sensors provide the parameter data needed for process control; temperature/pressure/conductivity are mature, while pH/DO/CO2 are newer technologies. Calibration strategy, sterilization compatibility and consistency across unit operations are the key evaluation factors.
3

Section 3: Quality Considerations

p40-p60

PDA Technical Report No. 66 (2014): Single-Use Systems | p40 - p53

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the risk-based and science-based approach to qualifying Single-Use System (SUS) suppliers, materials and components
  • Master the framework for classifying the risks that SUS use poses to the patient, the process and the operator
  • Understand the environmental requirements for SUS assembly and the key elements of supplier audits
  • Learn how to apply alternative supplier qualification, interchangeability assessment and supplier quality documentation
  • Gain a thorough understanding of the definition of Extractables and Leachables (E&L), how they are assessed, and their impact on product quality and patient safety

5.0/5.1 Qualification and Verification — Introduction

Original Text

5.0 Qualification and Verification of Suppliers, Materials, Components, and Completed Assemblies

5.1 Introduction

The qualification and verification of suppliers, materials, components, and completed assemblies require attention to several factors. It is important to use a risk-based and science-based approach to qualify both materials and completed assemblies for SUSs in pharmaceutical development and manufacture (18). The assessment of risk must be based on the complexity of the system and its intended use (e.g., product contact versus non-product contact, upstream versus downstream use, short-term contact versus long-term storage). See PDA's Technical Report No. 54 on Quality Risk Management for more information (7). In addition, scientific principles must be applied to 1) identify and monitor extractable materials from films and other components; 2) investigate potential interactions with product CQAs or significant process parameters like cell culture media; and 3) assess the effects of assembly processes such as welding, fusing, mechanical stress, and sterilization (e.g., gamma irradiation) on the materials during manufacturing. In assessing the suitability of the SUS with respect to potential leachables in the final product, extractables may be considered as potential in-process leachables. Finally, the design and functional integrity of the completed SUS must be verified and maintained by appropriately trained end users via suitable processes for construction, packaging, shipping, and deployment.

The supplier of the SUS must provide sufficient data to qualify the materials and components used to assemble the final SUS. In this regard, the end user may use the documents from the supplier to meet most of the verification criteria required to make sure that the SUS is fit for the intended application and purpose. This requires a thorough qualification of the supplier by the end user to ascertain an acceptable supplier quality system, documentation, and an appropriate level of technical capability that extends all the way to the original sources of materials to the supplier. In addition, the suppliers must be able to provide documented evidence of performance claims for off-the-shelf component designs for the SUS. A supplier quality agreement must underpin any custom designs that the end user may need from the supplier.

The first step in a science- and risk-based approach is to identify and assess the risks associated with using an SUS. The end user must identify that the SUS supplier's approach to the design and selection of materials of construction includes components and materials such as:

  • Filters
  • Sensors
  • Polymers
  • Lubricants
  • Slip agents

Supplier quality, manufacturing and analytical capabilities should also be evaluated. The end user must fully qualify and audit a number of potential suppliers before embarking on extensive studies of specific materials because it is preferable to have more than one supplier for the SUS.

It is important for the end user to have access to controlled documentation of the qualification studies conducted on the materials and components of the assembled SUS. Such documentation may include:

  • Batch numbers traceable to the raw materials
  • Product shelf life
  • Certificate of quality
  • Certificate of integrity
  • Endotoxin certification (to required and specific endotoxin level)
  • Animal-free certification
  • Sterilization certification (if sterility is claimed)

A robust change control communication and assessment process between the end user and the supplier (including sub-suppliers) must be established to monitor any changes in processing conditions, equipment, analytical methods, manufacturing site, resins, films, additives, and more. If the supplier does not have an appropriate assessment process in place, the end user may have to perform independent testing. After performing material and component qualification of the assembled products, a functional verification of the SUS should be performed according to the following:

  • Risk assessment — To confirm that the assembled products are fit for purpose
  • Process verification and validation — To confirm that the completed SUS functions within process constraints, including repetitive use or extended storage (if required)
  • Safety — To make sure that the SUS conforms to safety requirements as a pressure vessel (especially for larger volumes) or for containment in the event of rupture or leak

If the SUS assembly includes SU sensors, calibration and additional verification documentation must be supplied.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Risk-based and Science-based Approach: This is the core principle of the whole SUS qualification process. Not every SUS needs the same level of testing; the higher the risk of the application (for example, long-term storage of drug substance), the greater the depth and breadth of qualification. The science-based element stresses data-driven decisions rather than simply following convention.

Supplier Quality Agreement: This is the formal contract between the end user and the SUS supplier. It sets out quality expectations, change control notification obligations, audit rights and so on. It is especially important for custom designs.

Change Control: The SUS supply chain involves several tiers of suppliers (resin manufacturers, film manufacturers, assemblers), and a change at any tier (such as a different additive or manufacturing site) can affect final product quality.

Analogy

Imagine you run a high-end restaurant and decide to switch from ceramic tableware (traditional stainless steel equipment) to high-quality disposable tableware (SUS). You cannot simply buy plastic plates at a hypermarket and start using them. You need to confirm the supplier's food safety certification, material composition and temperature resistance, and make sure the supplier will notify you if they change the raw material formulation. This is just like SUS qualification: from the raw resin to the final assembly, every link has to be verified.

Key Notes

The qualification document package is the basis of an audit: lot traceability, shelf life, quality certificates, integrity certificates, endotoxin certification, animal-origin-free certification and sterilization certification. These documents are not only a compliance requirement; they are also the key tools for tracing back to the root cause when the process goes wrong.

Three elements of functional verification: risk assessment (confirms suitability), process validation and qualification (confirms functionality), and safety (confirms pressure vessel compliance). All three layers of verification are indispensable.

Decision Hierarchy

1. Patient Safety
▼
2. Product CQAs
▼
3. Business and supply chain flexibility

In SUS qualification, every decision must give patient safety the highest priority. Even under heavy supply chain cost pressure, the completeness of qualification cannot be sacrificed.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does TR66 stress that qualification must reach back to the "original sources of materials"? What risks arise if only the final assembler is audited?
  2. If a SUS supplier has no adequate change assessment process, what measures should the end user take? What does this mean for the operating costs of a CDMO?
  3. Give three scenarios showing why the same type of SUS may need different depths of qualification testing in different applications.

5.2 Risks Associated with Using Single-Use Systems

Original Text

The risks associated with the use of SUSs include that to the patient, the process, and operators. The risk to the process may occur as a result of process failure, but it rarely affects patients because of process discontinuation. Normal safety precautions for handling large volumes of liquids, which may also be hazardous, must be maintained. The main focus of this discussion is the risk to the patient. Begin risk assessment by considering the potential for the SUS to interact with product or process fluids. Some of the important parameters are point of use, duration of contact, process conditions (e.g., temperature, presence of solvents, and the ratio of surface area to volume). The greatest risk is associated with long-term (>30 days) storage containers of certain drug products (19). The degree of risk is different for upstream applications because of the ability to use further processing steps to remove modified products or leached compounds and the dilutive effect of multiple filtration and washing steps. Ishikawa (fishbone) diagrams can be used to map the process and then identify and characterize potential areas of concern (Figure 5.2-1).

Figure 5.2-1: Example of an Ishikawa (Fishbone) Diagram for Determining Risk Sources

Table 5.2-1🔍 identifies the risk complexities of SUSs.

*UF – ultrafiltration   †DF – diafiltration

For assembly error or individual component failure, complex systems with multiple valves, manifolds, and sensors may present more risks than simple systems (horizontal axis). Applications that are more demanding or close to the final step may pose higher risks (vertical axis). Condensates may get back to the process if fluid pathways are not designed properly. In order of increasing risk are the following components:

1. Nonfluid contact systems

  • Vent tubing and filter assemblies
  • Samples and sampling systems for repetitive use

Sampling bags are considered a separate category, as the contained product is removed from the process; however, the potential for interactions with bags to interfere with the outcome of assays must be determined.

2. Fluid contact systems

  • Short-term contact, simple components (e.g., bags for cleaning materials, buffer storage and concentrates, antifoam, additives, and media)

Mixing systems have additional requirements for establishing functional performance and are available in a range of designs (refer to Section 4.8.3):

  • Bottom and top mixing
  • Rocking mixing
  • Cleaning, flushing, and draining
  • Long-term storage (storage, holding, and delivery systems with impact on temperature, moisture loss, and gas adsorption)

3. Complex systems

  • Bioreactors, including instrumentation (pH, temperature, pressure, conductivity, gases, nutrients with impact on mixing, cell viability, cell integrity, adhesion, and shear)
  • Predispensed buffer or media additives (concentrates as well as commercial and proprietary blends of media)

4. Product contact and storage systems

  • Tubing, filters, valves, connectors, and manifolds
  • Process intermediate storage containers
  • Bulk drug-substance storage containers
  • Shipping containers
  • Disposable filling needles

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Three categories of risk targets: The risks of SUS use can be divided into risks to the patient (the most critical), to the process (leading to batch failure) and to the operator (safety hazards). TR66 focuses in particular on patient risk.

Key parameters for risk assessment:

  • Point of use: upstream vs downstream; the closer to the final product, the higher the risk
  • Duration of contact: long-term storage of >30 days carries the greatest risk
  • Process conditions: temperature, presence of solvents, surface area/volume ratio

Ishikawa Diagram (fishbone diagram): A quality management tool used to systematically identify all possible causes of a problem, grouping the causes into major categories such as people, machines, materials, methods and environment.

Table Analysis

Table 5.2-1🔍 interpretation: This is a 3x3 risk matrix, with system complexity on the horizontal axis and impact on the process on the vertical axis. Fill and Finish in the lower right corner has both the highest system complexity and the highest process impact, which makes it the highest-risk application scenario.

Note that Freeze/Thaw, although low in system complexity, has a high impact on the process because it bears directly on product quality (protein aggregation, container integrity and so on).

Analogy

Risk classification is like building safety grades: a temporary work shed (non-contact system) has the lowest safety requirements, a residential building (fluid-contact system) is intermediate, and hospitals and nuclear power plants (product-contact and storage systems) have the strictest safety standards. The closer a step is to the "end user" (the patient), the higher the quality requirements.

Key Notes

Upstream vs downstream risk differences: Upstream applications (such as cell culture) carry relatively lower risk because several later purification, filtration and washing steps can remove or dilute leachables. Downstream applications (such as drug substance storage) have no subsequent purification opportunity and therefore carry the highest risk.

Four risk classes (from low to high):

  1. Non-fluid-contact systems: vent lines, sampling systems
  2. Fluid-contact systems: buffer bags, media bags
  3. Complex systems: bioreactors, premixed media
  4. Product-contact and storage systems: tubing, valves, drug substance storage containers

Practice Questions

  1. In Table 5.2-1🔍, why is Purification/Product Storage classified as "medium complexity + high impact" rather than "high complexity + high impact"?
  2. A CDMO produces both upstream cell culture and downstream purification steps for clients. How should it differentiate between these two applications in its SUS qualification strategy?

5.3 Single-Use System Assembly

Original Text

SUSs include fittings, tubing, filters, connectors, bag material (film), and outer packaging. SU components are normally received and stored in areas with minimal environmental control. It is critical for the components to remain in their sealed packaging, and they should be handled with gloves to avoid contamination. Transport into the assembly room must follow standard clean-room practices. All the external packaging of the SU components should be wiped with a suitable antimicrobial or cleansing agent before moving them into the assembly room. The assembly rooms are typically ISO 7 or 8 with a combination of particle and microbial sampling. It is important to design manufacturing suites with HEPA-filtered air, low returns, smooth surfaces, and airlocks for entry and exit. Avoid introducing any sharp edges or corners into the assembly room because they may perforate the SUS bags. Personnel must be trained and wear appropriate clothing for an ISO 7 operation and the assembly room must be cleaned regularly. Where SUSs are preassembled, the supplier takes these steps.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

SUS assembly environment requirements: SUS components are usually received and stored in areas with lower environmental control requirements, but assembly must take place in a cleanroom of ISO 7 or ISO 8 class. This differs markedly from the manufacturing environment of traditional stainless steel equipment (usually a general factory environment).

Key controls: keep sealed packaging intact, handle with gloves, disinfect or wipe down outer packaging, use HEPA-filtered air, provide personnel training and gowning, and avoid sharp objects.

Analogy

SUS assembly is like assembling sterile instruments in an operating room: you would not open sterile packaging in a corridor. Even if the components are already packaged, the assembly environment must reach a certain cleanliness, because any contamination introduced during assembly can be sealed inside the final system. The specific mention of "avoiding sharp edges and corners" is like an operating room not allowing anything that could puncture a surgical glove.

Key Notes

CDMO practical considerations: If the CDMO assembles SUS in-house, it must maintain a dedicated assembly room that meets ISO 7/8, including an environmental monitoring program (particle counts + microbial sampling). If it chooses preassembled systems from the supplier, these responsibilities shift to the supplier, but the audit responsibility remains with the CDMO.

Practice Questions

  1. Why can SUS components be stored in a "minimal environmental control" area while assembly must take place in an ISO 7/8 cleanroom? What is the difference in risk between the two?
  2. If you were the head of quality at a CDMO, would you choose to assemble SUS in-house or purchase preassembled systems? What are the advantages and disadvantages of each?

5.4 Supplier Qualification of Single-Use Systems

Original Text

Since a typical SUS installation requires the acquisition of several components from various external suppliers, it is important for the end user to qualify suppliers to ensure that they can deliver SU components that meet or exceed regulatory and customer expectations.

5.4.1 Supplier Audits and Technical Diligence

It is important to audit the supplier's quality systems as part of the supplier qualification process to ensure delivery of consistent SU components to the end user. An understanding of the supplier's capabilities may lead to a better understanding of end-user expectations and can help identify any gaps in the capabilities of the supplier. Therefore, the auditing step must encompass manufacturing and technical aspects, and both parties must agree on corrective actions as appropriate. Microbiological control measures for SUSs must be evaluated as part of a supplier audit.

The auditor should review the procedure and records for quarterly dose audits where the SUS is subjected to gamma-irradiation sterilization.

Standard auditing practices, extending to a review of quality systems and complaint handling, should be followed. Unique to SUSs for inclusion in audits would be the supplier's definition of change and how the change-control process functions, including customer notification. The specific particulate control and reduction schemes in place at the supplier should also be evaluated.

Many SUS suppliers may either be ISO 9001 or medical device manufacturers under FDA and ISO registration. ISO 9001 registration means a company operates in a quality management framework that has been audited by a registrar and confirmed as adhering to ISO 9001 guidelines (20). The manufacturing facilities of registered medical devices are subject to inspection and follow 21 CFR Part 820 (cGMPs for Medical Devices) and ISO 13485 (21,22). SUSs, including bulk drug containers, are considered process equipment rather than medical devices; therefore, the manufacturing facilities of SUS suppliers are not subject to regulatory registration or inspection. Although SUS manufacturers tend to design their quality systems along GMP guidelines, the auditor should not expect these systems to adhere strictly to cGMPs for drug or medical device manufacturing (21,23). ISO 9001 is considered a minimum requirement.

Pertinent factors for a technical diligence assessment and an interpretation of its implementation over the clinical development lifecycle are reviewed in greater detail in Section 7.7.3.

5.4.2 Supplier Quality Agreements and Responsibilities

The purpose of a supplier quality agreement is to set quality expectations, identify and develop a mutual understanding of pertinent regulations and guidance documents, and provide advice on managing quality concerns such as audits, complaints, and change control. Change control can be a complex process for an SUS because of the diverse number of suppliers (e.g., compounders, film producers, and bag assemblers) and materials (e.g., resins and additives) involved in the supply chain. The supplier must provide full transparency on the full supply chain of the SUS and guarantee quality oversight of the supply chain.
