  • Sterilization methods: Gamma irradiation is the preferred SUS sterilization method; SIP is suitable for stainless steel interface connections; EtO is not recommended because of by-product risk; SUS are not designed to be resterilized.
  • Sensor technology: SU sensors undergo only one sterilization cycle and may be more reliable than reusable sensors, but limitations such as photo bleaching and drift must be noted.
  • Qualification and validation: The end user is responsible for process validation, which must cover material construction, test procedures and sterilization method, and SUS must be included in the media fill program.
  • Integrity and leaks: Integrity testing should be risk-tiered, from low risk (reliance on supplier data) to high risk (individual system testing). Establishing a standardized leak investigation procedure and trend analysis is the key to preventing leaks.
  • Leak prevention: The case study shows that improper handling is the main root cause of leaks. A standardized deployment process and operator training are key success factors for SUS implementation.
4

Section 4: Business Drivers

p61-p69

PDA Technical Report No. 66 (2014) — Single-Use Systems | p61 - p69

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the key business drivers and evaluation framework that lead companies to adopt Single-Use Systems (SUS)
  • Master the application of the Lifecycle Approach and Opportunity Cost in SUS decisions
  • Learn the structure of the Cost of Goods Manufactured (COGM) model, including the Process Model, Operational Model, Variable Cost and Fixed Cost submodels
  • Understand the components of Investment Costs, including equipment, facility, utilities and indirect costs
  • Recognize the impact of SUS implementation on QC/QA cost of quality and on fixed operating costs

Section Contents

6.0 Overview 6.1 Evaluation 6.2 Lifecycle 6.3 Opportunity Cost 6.4 Cost of Quality 6.5 Quantitative Evaluation 6.6 Operational Model 6.7 Total Cost Model 6.8 Investment Costs 6.9 QC/QA 6.10 Fixed Costs

6.0 Business Drivers for the Adoption of Single-Use Systems

Original Text

This section provides a detailed description of the key business drivers that inform the evaluation and implementation of single-use versus traditional MUSs. The business drivers for the introduction of an SUS are many, varied, and unique to each company's individual situation and business model. Several factors must be considered for a successful analysis. These factors include:

  • Supplier-demonstrated technical and quality capabilities
  • Product sustainability
  • Guarantee of business continuity
  • The business model of the company considering an SUS
  • Process design
  • The product and its potential market volume
  • Available facilities
  • Anticipated location

Each company will approach SUS implementation with a different set of objectives developed from business drivers that are unique to a specific business model.

SUS implementation confers advantages such as reduced capital investment, flexible operation, faster changeover, and the application of lean manufacturing principles. Since a company's individual situation and business model will affect the role of pertinent business drivers, it is critical to start the evaluation with an understanding of the business in question. For example, the main concern of a contract manufacturing company (CMO) may be the elimination of cross-contamination in a multiple-client, multiple-product facility. A stand-alone manufacturer with a facility dedicated to the production of a single product may consider a hybrid approach of an SUS and MUS to reduce SU variable costs yet allow for the expansion of production capacity at peak periods. An effective implementation of an SUS would help the company to meet important business goals in addition to cost savings.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Business Drivers: The various business considerations that lead a company to adopt SUS, including cost, flexibility, speed and regulatory compliance. Every company has a different mix of drivers, depending on its business model.

Hybrid Approach: A strategy that uses SUS together with traditional Multi-Use Systems (MUS), reducing variable cost while retaining flexibility to expand capacity.

Analogy

Imagine you run a restaurant. You could use porcelain tableware throughout (MUS), which requires a dishwasher and dedicated staff to wash up; or use disposable tableware throughout (SUS), which eliminates washing but costs more in consumables for every meal; or mix the two, with porcelain plates for main courses and paper cups for drinks (Hybrid). The best choice depends on what kind of restaurant you run: fine dining, fast food, or a catering service (CMO)?

Key Notes

TR66 emphasizes that the evaluation of SUS must start with an understanding of the company's own business model. A CMO places the most weight on preventing cross-contamination, a single-product plant may prefer a Hybrid approach, and a new facility may adopt an entirely SUS-based design. There is no "one size fits all" answer.

Note that the original text lists eight evaluation factors, covering everything from supplier capability to the intended plant location. This is not just a "cost comparison" but a full business strategy assessment.

Practice Questions

  1. If you were an operations head at a CMO, why might "eliminating cross-contamination" matter more than "reducing cost"?
  2. In what situations does a hybrid approach offer the greatest advantage? Give at least two specific scenarios.

6.1 Evaluation of Business Drivers

Original Text

The decision to implement an SUS tends to be driven by economic incentives such as 1) potential elimination of cleaning and sterilization processes; 2) reduction of dedicated equipment or changeover time; and 3) increased flexibility and capacity of the manufacturing facility. The end user must consider both tangible cost savings and less quantitative benefits such as low risks of cross contamination. A cost-of-goods-manufactured (COGM) analysis can be used to support claims of economic savings. Other tools, such as risk assessment, value-stream mapping, CO2 footprint, green manufacturing, and value-added analysis, can provide invaluable data to inform a business decision on whether to implement SUS or not.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

COGM (Cost of Goods Manufactured): A key metric for the total cost of manufacturing each batch of product. It divides all operating and capital costs by the number of batches to give a cost per batch, which is used to compare the economic viability of SUS and MUS.

Value-Stream Mapping: A lean manufacturing tool that visualizes the value-adding and non-value-adding steps across the whole process flow, helping to identify wasteful steps that SUS could replace.

Key Notes

An SUS evaluation cannot look only at "visible cost savings" (tangible savings); it must also consider "less quantitative benefits", such as reduced risk of cross-contamination. These soft benefits are often more decisive for regulatory review and product quality.

The combination of evaluation tools mentioned in the original text (COGM + Risk Assessment + Value-Stream Mapping + CO2 Footprint) reflects the modern pharmaceutical industry's expectation of a comprehensive approach to SUS decisions; it is no longer solely a finance department task.

Analogy

It is like buying a car: you cannot look only at the sticker price. You also have to consider fuel consumption, maintenance, insurance, depreciation and even the environmental impact. COGM analysis is the "total cost of ownership (TCO)" calculator for the SUS decision.

Practice Questions

  1. List three "quantifiable cost savings" and three "less quantitative benefits" that SUS implementation can bring.
  2. Why are CO2 footprint and green manufacturing also listed as evaluation tools? What does this mean for a pharmaceutical company's business decisions?

6.2 Lifecycle Approach

Original Text

The importance of specific business drivers will depend on the needs of each individual company. The scope of the project may be as narrow as using an SUS to boost the capacity of a single process step or to eliminate cleaning steps. Such a tactical comparison depends on technological, process compatibility, and economic factors. Conversely, the project scope could be a corporate-wide initiative to evaluate the feasibility of SUS implementation for an entire production process or part of an overall lifecycle evaluation for a product or facility. A lifecycle approach involves an evaluation of the costs incurred throughout the lifetime of the project in order to provide a complete economic basis for making decisions. Costs for evaluation may include the initial capital investments, production and quality overheads, and decommissioning. The lifecycle approach incorporates time and risk factors as relevant strategies are applied, and this leads to a more comprehensive economic evaluation.

For instance, the forecast for a new product may include an initial launch phase with a relatively uncertain production demand followed by a full-scale production phase with options for capacity expansion or additional products. In such a situation, operational flexibility becomes important to the business case because of the uncertainty in product demand. Such flexibility comes with an increase in capital investment for traditional MUSs, whereas the implementation of an SUS tends to reduce the cost of operational flexibility because it is less reliant on investment for utilities and equipment installation. An SUS is less costly to modify or expand to a new facility when a change in product lifecycle occurs.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Lifecycle Approach: A method that evaluates all costs of a project over its entire lifecycle, from construction to decommissioning. It looks beyond the initial investment to include operations, quality management overhead and the eventual cost of dismantling equipment.

Operational Flexibility: The ability of a manufacturing facility to adapt quickly to changes in volume, product changeovers or market demand fluctuations. Because SUS depends less on fixed equipment and utility installations, it inherently offers greater operational flexibility.

Analogy

The lifecycle approach is like the "rent vs. buy a home" decision: renting (SUS) means a low initial outlay and easy relocation, but higher monthly payments; buying (MUS) requires a large down payment and renovation costs, but long-term running costs may be lower. The best choice depends on how long you expect to stay, whether you may need to move, and the uncertainty about your future family size.

Key Notes

This passage reveals a core advantage of SUS: it lowers the "cost of flexibility". With traditional MUS, obtaining operational flexibility requires additional capital investment (spare equipment, a larger facility, etc.); SUS is inherently flexible and does not require large advance investment for a "possible expansion".

This matters especially for new drug launches. When demand is uncertain during clinical stages, SUS lets a company "scale to its means" and decide whether to expand only once the market is clear.

Practical Application

Scenario: Your company is developing a new biologic, and the Phase III results will be known in 18 months. If successful, you expect to need 2,000 batches per year; if only partially successful, perhaps only 500 batches. Under this uncertainty, the SUS lifecycle approach suggests starting production with an SUS design, avoiding a large investment in fixed assets while the outcome is unknown.

Practice Questions

  1. What items does "decommissioning cost" in the lifecycle approach include? Why might SUS have an advantage here?
  2. Under what circumstances might a company still choose SUS even when the lifecycle approach analysis shows that MUS has lower long-term cost?

6.3 Opportunity Cost

Original Text

Opportunity cost is defined as the value of the next-best choice available when choosing between several mutually exclusive options. These costs are often excluded from estimates of fixed operation costs because they can be difficult for comparative analyses in the overall decision process. For example, deciding to expand a facility may result in forgoing the opportunity to invest in other projects, contract batch production at a CMO, or maintain cash reserves. Opportunities can be difficult to compare; hence, opportunity cost calculations between competing projects may help identify the best path forward.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Opportunity Cost: The value of the "next-best alternative" given up when one of several mutually exclusive options is chosen. This is a basic economic concept, but it is often overlooked in facility investment decisions in the pharmaceutical industry because different projects are hard to compare directly.

Analogy

Suppose you have 10 million in capital. You could use it to expand the plant (A), invest in new drug R&D (B), or outsource to a CMO (C). If you choose A, the opportunity cost is the return from the better of B and C. Because SUS requires a lower initial investment, it lets you "keep more ammunition in reserve" for other opportunities.

Key Notes

Opportunity cost is "often excluded" from evaluations because it requires a hypothetical estimate for "the road not taken", which is especially difficult in large pharmaceutical companies because so many variables are involved. However, ignoring opportunity cost can lead a company to over-invest in fixed assets and miss more valuable business opportunities.

Practice Questions

  1. Why is opportunity cost especially important in a CMO's business decisions?
  2. If your company decides to use SUS instead of expanding the plant, what alternative investments could the saved funds be used for?

6.4 Cost of Quality

Original Text

The cost of quality can be defined as any cost that would not have been expended if quality were perfect. The inherent advantages of an SUS may lead to reductions in the cost of quality for the end user. In order for an SUS to be used in a manufacturing process, the end user must be able to demonstrate that it is fit for purpose and meets applicable quality and regulatory requirements. SUS suppliers may take advantage of automation, economies of scale, and lean manufacturing to improve consistency and reduce the cost of manufacturing. This arrangement makes the end user dependent on the supplier's quality system. Therefore, it is critical for the quality system of the supplier to be robust enough to manage subsuppliers (e.g., resin and component suppliers). In addition, the quality system of the supplier must be able to monitor and detect the unique failure modes associated with SUS (e.g., bag breakage, variations in the profile of extractables and leachables, and effects on cell growth). It is the responsibility of the end user to ensure that the SUS meets applicable quality standards. Any tests conducted by the end user to ascertain process compatibility due to changes made by the supplier may be included in cost-of-quality estimates.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Cost of Quality: All the costs that would not need to be spent if quality were perfect. It includes prevention costs, appraisal costs, internal failure costs and external failure costs. SUS can reduce some costs of quality (for example, eliminating CIP/SIP validation) but also introduces new ones (for example, E&L studies).

Extractables & Leachables: A quality risk unique to SUS: chemicals in plastic materials may leach into the process fluid, affecting product quality and safety.

Key Notes

This passage implies a key risk: SUS shifts part of the responsibility for quality assurance to the supplier. The end user relies on the supplier's quality system to manage sub-suppliers and to monitor SUS-specific failure modes. This means that:

  • The depth and frequency of supplier audits must be increased
  • The quality agreement must cover SUS-specific risks
  • The impact of supplier change control on the end user needs to be monitored continuously

Analogy

Cost of quality is like an insurance premium: you pay the premium (prevention cost) to avoid a huge loss (failure cost) if an accident occurs. Using SUS is like transferring part of the insurance responsibility to the supplier: your premium may fall, but if the supplier's "insurer" is unreliable, the loss could be even greater when something goes wrong.

Practice Questions

  1. Of the three SUS-specific failure modes (bag rupture, E&L variability, impact on cell growth), which is hardest for a supplier's quality system to detect, and why?
  2. When a supplier makes a change to an SUS component, what additional process compatibility testing does the end user need to perform?

6.5 Quantitative Evaluation of Business Drivers

Original Text

A quantitative evaluation of business drivers for an SUS project may be based on determining the average cost of a batch in order to examine the feasibility of the SUS project. An overall economic model for a given project may be obtained by combining parameters from a series of submodels. A list of submodels and their interactions for setting up the overall economic model is shown in Figure 6.5-1. The actual split between submodels and the amount of modeling work necessary depends on the project scope and level of accuracy necessary for the study. A more comprehensive comparative study may include all submodels, but in some cases, depending on the level of accuracy needed, an approach including only the Process, Capital Cost, and Variable Cost models may be sufficient for giving an estimate of the feasibility of the project.

Figure 6.5-1: Cost Comparison Studies Reference Model

Process Model
↓
Business Model
Operational Model
Facility Model
↓
Capital Cost Model
Variable Cost Model
Fixed Cost Model
↓
Total Cost Model

As shown in Figure 6.5-1, a process model is created first, followed by the generation of submodels according to the project scope. The resulting submodels for facility, investment, variable, and fixed costs may then be combined to form the total cost model. The factors to consider for the process model are summarized in Table 6.5-1.

6.5.1 Process Assessment

The process model is an assessment of the process with respect to an SUS. The process model forms the basis for any cost comparison exercise by defining process setup and deployment for either an SUS or an MUS. Data from the process model feeds into the facility, capital cost, and variable cost model to provide the basis for the fixed-cost model.

6.5.2 Batch Frequency

With a higher batch frequency, variable costs arising from using more consumables over time may become prohibitive for an SUS design. If the batch frequency is low, varies, or becomes difficult to predict, then an SUS may improve the business case by keeping fixed costs low. For batch sizes exceeding feasible SUS volumes, there may be adverse economic effects from frequent use of the SUS.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Economic Model: An analytical framework made up of several submodels, used to quantitatively compare the total cost of SUS and MUS. The submodels include the Process Model, Operational Model, Facility Model, Capital Cost Model, Variable Cost Model and Fixed Cost Model, which are finally integrated into the Total Cost Model.

Figure Analysis

Figure 6.5-1 shows a cost model architecture built up layer by layer:

  • Layer 1 (Process Model): The foundation of everything: first define the process steps, equipment requirements and batch size
  • Layer 2 (Business/Operational/Facility): Translates process information into operating scenarios and facility requirements
  • Layer 3 (Capital/Variable/Fixed Cost): Translates the scenarios into concrete cost data
  • Layer 4 (Total Cost Model): Integrates all costs and outputs the average cost per batch

Important: Not every project needs all six submodels. For a preliminary feasibility study, only three submodels are needed: Process + Capital Cost + Variable Cost.

Analogy

This economic model is like budget planning for building a house: first draw the design plans (Process Model), then estimate the building materials (Capital Cost), the monthly utilities (Variable Cost) and the property management fees (Fixed Cost), and finally add them up to get the monthly cost of living. For a preliminary assessment, you may only need rough figures for materials and utilities.

Key Notes

Batch Frequency is the key dividing line for the economic viability of SUS:

  • High batch frequency = consumable costs accumulate, so SUS may not be worthwhile
  • Low or irregular batch frequency = SUS has a clear advantage because fixed costs are low
  • Very large batches = may exceed the SUS bag volume limit, requiring multiple SUS assemblies and adding cost

This is why CMO and clinical-stage manufacturing are particularly well suited to SUS: batch frequency is low and hard to predict.

Formula

A simplified approach to cost comparison:

SUS cost per batch = (fixed cost / batches per year) + variable cost per batch
MUS cost per batch = (fixed cost / batches per year) + variable cost per batch

SUS: low fixed cost, high variable cost (consumables)
MUS: high fixed cost, low variable cost (cleaning/sterilization)

Break-even point = difference in fixed cost / difference in variable cost

Practice Questions

  1. The "Unit operation" factor in Table 6.5-1🔍 points out that large-volume processes may require multiple SUS bags. What does this mean for facility classification?
  2. If your batch frequency is 200 batches per year with a batch size of 500 L, which submodels should the SUS economic viability assessment focus on?

6.6 Operational Model

Original Text

Operational data about the facility is captured by the operation model and then added to the data from the process model in order to estimate utilization and batch output from the facility. When this information is combined with other cost models, the average batch cost for the various scenarios can then be calculated. The main factors that affect the selection of operation model are summarized in Table 6.6-1.

6.6.1 Single- or Multiple-Product Facility

The business case may be improved with an SUS because it may reduce downtime and changeover time. In a single-product facility, capacity may be increased via a reduction in the time required for equipment cleaning. In a multiple-product facility, improved capacity may be achieved through a decrease in both changeover and equipment-cleaning time. It is important to factor in differences in facility throughput when technologies are compared.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Downtime: The time between batches spent on cleaning, disassembly, sterilization, calibration, testing, installation and deployment. SUS can greatly reduce downtime because the CIP/SIP steps are eliminated.

Changeover Duration: The time required to switch between different products or batches. In a multi-product facility, this is often the capacity bottleneck. The "use once and discard" nature of SUS can significantly shorten changeover time.

Scrap Ratio: The proportion of batches lost due to contamination, loss of integrity or other problems. It includes the material and labor costs caused by scrapping.

Analogy

Changeover is like an F1 pit stop: a traditional MUS changeover is like pushing the whole car into the garage for a thorough overhaul (cleaning, sanitization, validation), which may take days or even weeks; an SUS changeover is like simply fitting a brand-new set of tires, getting back on track within hours. In a CMO, where "every race is with a different team", pit-stop speed decides who wins.

Key Notes

The "Batches per year" row in Table 6.6-1🔍 reveals a logic that is often overlooked: the real economic value of SUS lies not only in lowering the cost per batch, but also in allowing the same facility to produce more batches each year. When fixed costs are spread over more batches, the fixed cost allocated to each batch falls, which may completely offset the higher consumable cost of SUS.

Practical Application

Scenario: A CMO has a multi-product line that needs 12 product changeovers per year. Each MUS changeover takes 5 days (cleaning + validation), while SUS takes only 1 day. This means SUS frees up 48 additional working days per year, allowing roughly 15-20 additional batches to be produced. The revenue from these extra batches often far exceeds the increase in SUS consumable costs.

Practice Questions

  1. How does scrap ratio affect SUS and MUS differently? (Hint: the scrap cost for SUS is mainly consumables; what is the main scrap cost for MUS?)
  2. In a single-product facility, is the main value of SUS reducing downtime or changeover? Why?

6.7 Total Cost Model

Original Text

A project may involve either the construction of a new facility or the retrofitting of an existing one. The main points of consideration for both cases are summarized in Table 6.7-1🔍 (58).

6.7.1 Shortcut Cost Model Based on Cost of Goods

In a preliminary evaluation in which the speed of comparison is more important than accuracy, a yes-or-no type of feasibility study incorporating a minimum of factors would be sufficient. This approach may not be rigorous enough for a final binding decision where funds would be committed. In such a case, elements of the more comprehensive approach must be applied.

The minimum factors to consider in the shortcut model are:

  • Process equipment
  • Utility equipment
  • SU consumables
  • Cost of capital assumptions

6.7.2 Example of a Shortcut Cost Model

Use the following lists and the preceding paragraphs to estimate the following for each scenario:

Process and Operational Basis

  • Process equipment involved
  • Number of batches produced annually

Investment Costs

  • Equipment costs
  • Area needed
  • Area costs
  • Total investment costs

Cost of Capital

  • Annual depreciation on investment
  • Annual interest rate paid on capital investment
  • Total cost of capital

Operating Costs

  • Consumables costs
  • Materials costs
  • Labor costs

Evaluate the Following According to the Business Model:

  • Investment costs
  • Operating costs
  • Number of batches produced
  • COGM = (total operating costs + total cost of capital) / number of batches produced
  • Payback time = difference in investment cost / difference in COGM

6.7.3 Comprehensive Cost-of-Goods Model

A more comprehensive cost model may include factors selected from the preceding sections (Tables 6.5-1, 6.6-1, 6.7-1 and Section 6.7.2-1). The structure of the model must be flexible enough so that it can be improved for accuracy with additional data. A sensitivity analysis is the most effective way to use an economic model because it incorporates a range of input factors for arriving at a good understanding of the potential risks involved.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Retrofitting: Modifying an existing facility to introduce SUS technology, with different considerations from building a new facility. Retrofitting is constrained by existing facility conditions, but requires lower investment and allows production to continue.

Shortcut Cost Model: A method that uses a minimum number of factors for a quick feasibility judgment. It is suitable for preliminary assessment but not sufficient to support a final investment decision.

Sensitivity Analysis: Varying the range of the model's input variables to understand how much different assumptions affect the result, and to identify which variables are critical to the economic viability of SUS.

Key Notes

The comparison in Table 6.7-1🔍 is very practical:

  • New facility: Can be designed from scratch and optimized for SUS, greatly shortening time to market
  • Retrofitted facility: The focus is on overcoming existing capacity constraints and minimizing production interruption during the retrofit

For a CDMO, retrofitting is often the more common scenario, because the cost of shutting down production entirely to build a new plant is too high.

Formula

Core formulas of the Shortcut Cost Model:

COGM = (Total Operating Costs + Total Cost of Capital)
       / Number of Batches Produced

Payback Time = Difference in Investment Cost
             / Difference in COGM

Where:
  Total Cost of Capital = annual depreciation + annual interest
  Total Operating Costs = consumables + materials + labor

Example:
  SUS investment = $2M, COGM = $15K/batch
  MUS investment = $8M, COGM = $18K/batch
  Payback = ($8M - $2M) / ($18K - $15K)
          = $6M / $3K = 2,000 batches

Analogy

The Shortcut Model vs. the Comprehensive Model is like a medical visit: a rapid screening (shortcut) can quickly tell you "there may be a problem" or "it looks fine", but to confirm a diagnosis and plan treatment you need a full health check-up (comprehensive model). Sensitivity analysis is like a stress test: in the worst case, can your finances still hold up?

Practice Questions

  1. In the shortcut cost model, why are only four minimum factors (process equipment, utility equipment, SU consumables, capital cost assumptions) enough for a preliminary judgment?
  2. If sensitivity analysis shows that a 20% fluctuation in SUS consumable prices would push COGM above that of MUS, what would you recommend?
  3. When the payback time calculates to 2,000 batches, what does this mean for a company producing 100 batches per year compared with one producing 500 batches per year?

6.8 Investment Costs

Original Text

6.8.1 Process Equipment

The first step in evaluating an SU project is normally an equipment-to-equipment comparison of SUS versus MUS direct investment cost. Reduced demand for cleaning and sterilization allow for simplifications in piping and instrumentation, which leads to a reduction in the investment cost for SUSs. While this will give an estimate of whether the technology is economically feasible for the project, Table 6.8.1-1🔍 shows additional factors for evaluating investment costs that should be considered for an SUS project (59).

6.8.2 Utility Equipment

The effect of utility systems on the investment costs should be explored because it may be possible to remove entire system groups from the scope (e.g., clean steam, CIP stations) or achieve enough reduction in capacity to obtain cost reductions (WFI for rinsing, kill systems). The need to inactivate solid waste may increase the investment costs of an SUS, while the absence of a CIP in an SUS design may lead to a reduction in the generation of wastewater.

6.8.3 Indirect Equipment Costs

The estimates for engineering and installation costs (e.g., design, commissioning, and qualification and validation) are normally calculated as a percentage of the equipment costs. One of the advantages of an SUS is that reductions in piping and instrumentation may decrease the number of both installed hardware and communication I/O points in the automation system. However, an SUS may incur a higher engineering percentage cost compared to similar MUS designs because of lower initial investment costs and the cost of validating leachables and extractables for the SUS. The installation percentage is lower for an SUS than an MUS. A similar approach can be used to determine the percentage costs for construction and project management (60).

6.8.4 Facility

An SUS uses stackable rectangular containers, which results in a smaller footprint compared to MUS. The absence of a CIP and washing/sterilization function further reduces the space requirements of SUS. However, inefficient movement of containers, installation, and dismantling of bags can easily waste the space saved by an SUS. In addition, proper storage and handling of an SUS is very important, since any damage might result in a lost batch or contamination. The transport route of an SUS may increase compared to that of a hard-piped system, and an SUS requires space to store process waste prior to final disposal. Storage space for SUS is typically greater than that for traditional MUS because of the need to manage SUS inventory and associated reusable storage containers. The main contributions to variable operating costs are summarized in Table 6.8.4-1🔍 (61).

6.8.5 Materials

Often, input materials (e.g., SUS materials prefilled with hydrated or powdered media and buffer components and chromatography gels) may be independent of SUS or MUS technology (62).

6.8.6 Consumables

The best way to determine the component cost of consumables is from the specific SUS process design under consideration because there can be a significant discrepancy between the costs of a supplier's standard configuration and that of the specific assembly required for a particular process step. Whenever possible, using the supplier's standard configurations would be the most cost-effective way to determine the cost of consumables. Designing the SUS with backup systems such as redundant tubing, filters, and connectors will increase the costs per batch of that SUS. However, if these backup systems are necessary, they must be reflected in the final cost analysis of the project.

It is possible to minimize SUS overdesign via a thorough understanding of the real risks determined in a process risk assessment such as a failure mode and effects analysis (FMEA) (7). It is also important to get a feel for the actual cost of the SUS once specific connectors, filters, and tubing sets are added to the design. Since it may be possible to obtain discounts with large-volume orders from prospective suppliers, it is necessary to understand SUS configurations and how they can be customized to meet individual SUS process requirements. It would be advisable to incorporate a sensitivity analysis for the purchase cost of an SUS because final configurations and prices can be difficult to predict. In general, the sensitivity analysis should be extended to any variable with uncertain cost estimates (63).

6.8.7 Utilities

The reductions in cleaning and sterilization associated with an SUS leads to a decrease in the cost of utilities, especially for clean water generation, CIP, and SIP systems. It is important to use carefully verified cost data since large volumes may be involved in these processes. The cost of water for injection is significantly lower for large on-site systems than for commercially supplied WFI. It may be calculated from system depreciation, labor, and energy costs.

6.8.8 Waste

The cost of inactivating solid waste can be significant depending on the degree of containment required. The solid waste may be 1) inactivated on-site, which will lead to increased load on kill autoclaves; or 2) transported off-site for incineration or disposal at a landfill. Both approaches would incur transport and disposal fees. A third approach involves chemical inactivation. The disposal strategy for each SUS must comply with the laws and requirements of the municipal authority in charge (64–66).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Investment Costs: More than just the equipment purchase price, these include the full range of initial outlay: utilities, installation, engineering design, commissioning, validation, facility space and operators.

Indirect Equipment Costs: Usually estimated as a percentage of equipment cost, including design, commissioning, qualification and validation fees. SUS has a lower installation percentage, but its engineering percentage may be higher (because the base investment is lower and E&L validation is needed).

FMEA (Failure Mode and Effects Analysis): A systematic risk assessment tool used to identify the real risks of SUS and avoid the unnecessary cost of overdesign.

Key Notes

Table 6.8.1-1🔍 is a complete checklist for investment cost assessment. Pay particular attention to the following factors, which are often underestimated:

  • Commissioning: SUS can eliminate the I/O points associated with CIP/SIP, simplifying the automation system
  • Facility floor space: SUS affects not only the production area but also the cleanliness classification and storage requirements
  • Operators: The number of operators affects training costs, workstations and gowning room requirements

Key Notes: Facilities and Waste

The "space savings" SUS offers in a facility are not absolute:

  • Space for CIP/SIP equipment is eliminated
  • But storage space for SUS inventory increases
  • Temporary storage space for process waste increases
  • Transport route length increases (compared with hard-piped systems)

The three waste disposal methods (on-site inactivation, off-site incineration/landfill, chemical inactivation) each have different costs and regulatory requirements, and must comply with local authority regulations.

Analogy

Estimating SUS consumable costs is like renovating a house: the "standard package" price in the supplier's catalog and the "customized combination" you actually need can be worlds apart. You may see "basic renovation at 30,000 per ping", but once you add the imported tiles, concealed lighting and smart-home system you want, it may become 80,000 per ping. That is why TR66 recommends sensitivity analysis: to see how much price fluctuation affects the overall cost.

Practical Application

Scenario: You are evaluating converting an existing stainless steel buffer preparation line to SUS. Using Table 6.8.1-1🔍 as a checklist:

  • Process equipment: You need to purchase bag supports, welders and sealers
  • Utility equipment: The CIP station for this line can be eliminated, but changes in WFI demand need to be evaluated
  • Validation: E&L studies are needed, but CIP/SIP validation can be omitted
  • Facility: The production area may shrink, but an SUS warehouse needs to be added

Practice Questions

  1. Why might the engineering percentage cost of SUS be higher than that of MUS? Does this mean the total investment cost of SUS is higher?
  2. At which stage of the project should the SUS waste disposal strategy be planned, and why?
  3. Of the five variable costs in Table 6.8.4-1🔍, which increase with SUS and which decrease?

6.9 QC/QA and Cost of Quality

Original Text

Since some variable costs (e.g., analyses, preparing batch documentation, and batch release) are rarely affected by SUS implementation, the baseline costs of MU and SU systems tend to be similar. Batch review and investigation efforts may decrease and result in additional savings because SUS implementation results in reduced cleaning, SIP, and changeover activities. However, certain quality oversight activities (e.g., quality agreement, supplier audits, SUS documentation review, subsupplier audits, and QC-receiving inspection) may increase with an SUS implementation. The operation of several quality activities within the supplier's quality system rather than that of the end user may make process investigations more complex, and this additional effort should be taken into account.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Quality Oversight Activities: The additional quality management work required after SUS implementation, including: quality agreement, supplier audit, review of SUS documentation, subsupplier audit and QC receiving inspection. These are the quality management costs that must be borne when using "purchased ready-to-use components".

Key Notes

The "one up, one down" effect on QC/QA cost:

  • Reduced cost: Less batch review and investigation work (because deviation investigations related to cleaning, SIP and changeover steps are reduced)
  • Increased cost: More supplier quality oversight work (quality agreement, audits, documentation review, receiving inspection)
  • Increased complexity: When quality activities are split across two quality systems, those of the supplier and the end user, process investigations become more complex

The net benefit depends on the company's specific situation. If the supplier's quality system is mature and reliable, the overall cost of quality for SUS may be lower.

Analogy

It is like moving from "cooking at home" to "ordering takeaway lunch boxes": you save the time spent washing dishes and preparing ingredients (fewer cleaning/SIP-related deviations), but you need to check the food safety and hygiene of the lunch box supplier (supplier audit), confirm the source of the ingredients (subsupplier audit), and check the quality on receipt (receiving inspection). If something goes wrong with the lunch box, tracing the cause is harder than if you had cooked it yourself (process investigations more complex).

Practice Questions

  1. Why is the cost of "batch documentation preparation and batch release" not very different between SUS and MUS?
  2. If your SUS supplier is in another country, how do the challenges of a subsupplier audit increase? What does this mean for the cost of quality?

6.10 Fixed Operating Costs

Original Text

Fixed operating costs represent the expenditure on the facility regardless of the state of production. The main factors involved in evaluating fixed operating costs are summarized in Table 6.10-1.

(Table 6.10-1🔍 continues beyond the scope of pages covered in this section.)

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Fixed Operating Costs: Facility expenses that must be paid regardless of production status (in production, idle or under maintenance). Typical items include facility rent/depreciation, base staffing, maintenance, insurance and taxes. Because SUS needs less facility space and less equipment, it generally has lower fixed operating costs.

Key Notes

The nature of a fixed cost is that "you must pay it whether you produce 0 batches or 100". This is why SUS has the advantage when batch frequency is low: its fixed costs are lower, so even low output does not create excessive financial pressure. Conversely, the high fixed costs of MUS must be spread over a large number of batches, and idle equipment is "burning money".

Analogy

Fixed operating costs are like your monthly phone plan fee: you pay it every month no matter how many calls you make. MUS is like a high-fee plan (equipment depreciation, CIP system maintenance, maintenance of a large facility), and SUS is like a low-fee plan with higher call charges. When your call volume (number of batches) is uncertain, the low-fee plan carries less risk.

Practice Questions

  1. List three fixed operating costs that may fall after SUS implementation and explain why.
  2. Under what circumstances might the fixed operating costs of SUS actually be higher than those of MUS? (Hint: consider storage and inventory management.)

Section Summary

  • Business drivers are diverse: Adopting SUS is not only a cost consideration; it also involves eight factors, including supplier capability, product sustainability, business continuity, process design, market size, facility and location. Each company's evaluation focus differs.
  • Lifecycle Approach: The evaluation should cover the full lifecycle cost from construction to decommissioning. SUS is particularly advantageous when demand is uncertain and operational flexibility is needed.
  • The economic model is built up in layers: From the Process Model to the Total Cost Model, it consists of several submodels. A Shortcut Model can be used for a preliminary assessment; the final decision requires a Comprehensive Model + Sensitivity Analysis.
  • COGM and Payback Time: The core formulas are COGM = (operating costs + capital costs) / number of batches; Payback = difference in investment / difference in COGM. Batch frequency is the key variable determining the economic viability of SUS.
  • Investment cost assessment must be comprehensive: Do not compare equipment prices alone; also consider utilities, installation, engineering, commissioning, validation, facility space and personnel. SUS has a low installation percentage but its engineering percentage may be high.
  • Cost of quality: one up, one down: SUS reduces investigation costs related to cleaning/SIP but increases supplier oversight costs. When quality activities are split across two quality systems, process investigations become more complex.
  • Fixed cost advantage: SUS generally has lower fixed operating costs, which is particularly advantageous when batch frequency is low or uncertain.
5

Section 5: Implementation Framework

p70-p107

PDA Technical Report No. 66 (2014) - Single-Use Systems | p77-p85

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the stage-gate architecture of the SUS Implementation Road Map, and the logical flow of S1 to S7 and G1 to G8
  • Master the three Implementation Themes: stakeholder management, risk management, and process validation and verification (PVV)
  • Learn to apply Quality Risk Management (QRM) tools (FMEA, fishbone diagram, risk profile) to SUS implementation decisions
  • Understand how SUS implementation connects to the FDA Process Validation Lifecycle and ICH Q8/Q9/Q10
7.0 Implementation of a Single-Use System

Original Text

This section provides a framework for the implementation of an SUS. A successful implementation of an SUS takes into account the unique situation and requirements of the project and then communicates these needs effectively to stakeholders, suppliers, and sponsors. Although good project management skills are essential for any successful project implementation, SUS implementation tends to be enhanced by a stage-gate approach in which the details of the application are evaluated and the results communicated to the broader team. The approach presented in this section allows for important decisions to be taken at appropriate times to facilitate the success of the overall project.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Stage-Gate Approach: A structured project management method that divides the whole implementation into multiple "stages", with a "gateway" between each stage. The project can move to the next stage only after all objectives of the current stage have been met and reviewed.

The core value of this approach is that each gateway provides an opportunity to "pause, assess, and decide", so that resources are not over-invested in the wrong direction.

Analogy

Imagine building a house. You would not start the interior fit-out before the foundation is laid. Stage-Gate works like the inspection points of a construction project: only after the foundation is accepted (G1) do you move on to the structural work (S2); only after the structure passes inspection (G2) do you move on to plumbing and electrical (S3). Each gateway is the moment the site supervisor comes to sign off, making sure the previous step was done right before moving on.

Key Notes

TR66 stresses here that SUS implementation is not a simple equipment purchase but a systems engineering effort that requires cross-functional coordination and multi-stage verification. Even experienced engineers can miss critical steps if they ignore a structured process.

Practice Questions

  1. Why is the Stage-Gate approach particularly suitable for SUS implementation, rather than traditional linear project management?
  2. If your company is a small CDMO, which stages do you think could be combined? Which can absolutely not be omitted?
7.1 Implementation Road Map

Original Text

Figure 7.1-1🔍 shows an implementation road map based on a natural progression of workflows. These workflows use a manufacturing strategy that provides the foundation for the main SUS implementation process described in this section (see also Figures 3.0-1 and 3.0-2). Table 7.1-1🔍 provides a more detailed overview of each of the implementation stages together with key documentary outputs for the entire SUS implementation process.

Stages 3 to 7, as outlined in the figure below, can also be aligned with ICH Q8 and Q9 (Quality-by-Design and Quality Risk Management) processes/tools: stakeholder management, risk management, and process validation and verification.

The SUS implementation road map consists of a series of gateways (G1 to G8) and stages (S1 to S7). The stages (shown in yellow, blue, and green boxes) describe the natural progression of workflows in which information is gathered and documented. Specific goals (summarized in the drop-down boxes) must be attained before progressing to the next gateway. The process starts with a manufacturing strategy (S1 and S2); described in detail in Section 3.5) and proceeds into the main project-implementation, stage-gateway process via G3.

The implementation process is not necessarily linear: a more in-depth study of elements during S1 and S2 may negate the need for some of these activities at the later stages. For example, if a more elaborate technical assessment and definition of general principles in the SUS scope document are performed in S1 and S2, entry can occur at G4. A parallel approach from G1 to G3 and from G4 to G8 may facilitate or accelerate effective project completion. S3 and S4 may also be pursued in parallel. However, if one or more of these modifications are applied to the road map, care should be exercised that the implementation process does not lose structure and that the comprehensive treatment of all of the elements necessary remains in place.

Sections 4, 5, and 6 of this technical report provide additional information for reviewing the scope and activities of various workflows.

"The implementation process is not necessarily linear: a more in-depth study of elements during S1 and S2 may negate the need for some of these activities at the later stages."

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Implementation Road Map: This is the core framework of TR66, made up of 8 gateways (G1-G8) and 7 stages (S1-S7):

  • S1: Technology investigation and alignment with business objectives (Manufacturing Strategy Part 1)
  • S2: Impact assessment and implementation approach (Manufacturing Strategy Part 2)
  • S3: Scoping - develop the SUS strategy document
  • S4: Business Case - initiate the Project Execution Plan (PEP)
  • S5: Development - user requirements and procurement
  • S6: Testing & Validation - C&Q, PV, personnel training
  • S7: Launch - materials management and routine operations

Key Notes

Non-linear flexibility: TR66 specifically notes that this road map is not necessarily linear. If a more in-depth technical assessment was done in S1/S2, you can enter directly at G4 and skip part of the S3 work. S3 and S4 can also run in parallel.

However, TR66 also warns that if the sequence of the road map is modified, you must make sure the structure is not lost: all necessary elements still need to be fully addressed.

Analogy

The road map is like the "suggested route" on a GPS. The GPS suggests the highway (the standard linear process), but if you know the local roads well (S1/S2 done in depth), you can take a shortcut (enter directly at G4). Whichever route you take, the destination (successful SUS implementation) and the key intersections you must pass (gateway checks) cannot be skipped.

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: Suppose your CDMO has years of experience with SU bioreactors and now wants to introduce a new SU mixing system. Because you already have a mature supplier audit process and quality system, much of the S1/S2 work has already been done at the organizational level. You can enter directly at G4 and focus on the business case and technical development for this specific piece of equipment.

Practice Questions

  1. Under what circumstances is running S3 and S4 in parallel reasonable? When should they be executed strictly in sequence?
  2. For a new-build facility project that has never used SUS, which gateway (G1-G8) do you think is the most critical "point of no return" decision? Why?
Table 7.1-1🔍: Focus and Output for Each SUS Implementation Stage 🔍

Original Text

The end user should adjust the outputs from this table to match the desired level of project structure in the SUS implementation process. Strict adherence to this workflow provides a structured and practical approach; however, it may be equally beneficial to operate on an ad hoc basis by selecting specific documents and adding to them as the project lifecycle progresses. The time at which some of the documents are created may be adjusted as required by changes in the circumstances of the end user. A subject matter expert (SME) may implement a simplified SUS project plan whereas a fully established and large company may opt for a complete project implementation. Note that regardless of the approach the end user adopts, there is a minimum level of project organization and documentation necessary to provide structure and clear deliverables without which the implementation process may falter.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Core value of Table 7.1-1🔍: This table is the "document checklist overview" for the whole SUS implementation. It answers a key question: at each stage, which documents do I need to produce?

A few important observations:

  • CDA/NDA appears in S1, S2, S4 and S7 - reflecting that confidentiality management with suppliers runs through the whole lifecycle
  • PVV documents start to appear from S4 and continue to S7 - validation is not a one-time event but an ongoing activity
  • S4 (Business Case) has the most output documents - this is the key turning point from strategy to execution

Key Notes

Principle of appropriate scale: TR66 states explicitly that an SME can implement a simplified SUS project plan, while a large enterprise may need a full project implementation. The key point: regardless of size, there is a minimum level of project organization and documentation required, otherwise the implementation may fail.

This is consistent with the ICH Q9 principle of being "commensurate with risk".

Analogy

This table is like the "packing list" in a travel guide. If you are a backpacker (small company/SME), you only need the basic gear; if you are taking the whole family abroad on vacation (large pharma company), your list will be longer and more detailed. But either way, the passport (core documents), the plane ticket (project plan), and the insurance (risk management) are things nobody can leave out.

Practice Questions

  1. If you are a project manager at a CDMO, how would you prioritize the 16 documents to be produced in stage S4? Which documents could be combined?
  2. Why do CDA/NDAs need to appear repeatedly across multiple stages? What does this reflect about supplier relationship management?
7.2 Implementation Themes

Original Text

As shown in Figure 7.1-1🔍, three themes represent a series of concepts and principles that span SUS implementation, and each theme can be applied either as a tool in support or as part of the implementation method.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Three Implementation Themes: These three themes are not stage-specific activities but horizontal pillars that run through the entire implementation:

  1. Stakeholder Management - ensuring the expectations of all relevant parties are understood and managed
  2. Risk Management - identifying, assessing, and mitigating risks
  3. Process Validation and Verification, PVV - ensuring the process continues to meet requirements

These three themes are like the three pillars of a building, supporting the work of every stage.

7.2.1 Stakeholder Management

Original Text

Stakeholder management is important throughout the SUS implementation process. It helps an organization to achieve strategic objectives by 1) interpreting and influencing both the external and internal environments, and 2) creating positive relationships with stakeholders by appropriately managing their expectations and agreed objectives.

A structured approach is important and beneficial to every stage of implementation (Table 7.1-1🔍). Identifying stakeholders who are involved across multiple functional areas would encourage teamwork and facilitate effective communication. Deficient stakeholder management may lead to adverse conditions such as diverging implementation and missed opportunities for standardization and process streamlining (e.g., the parallel pursuit of several different types of sterile connectors or storage bags, when multiple SUS technologies or suppliers are engaged). It may be helpful to create a "power" grid that classifies stakeholders according to their influence and their interest in the project. Figure 7.2.1-1 shows an example for preparing user requirements and managing stakeholders.

Figure 7.2.1-1: An Example of a Stakeholder Power Grid

The power grid allows the project team to identify, gather input from, and manage internal or external stakeholders. It is also useful for preparing SUS user requirements, and it fosters effective communication among project team members.

Effective stakeholder management may be achieved by 1) evaluating company governance and management processes in order to identify functional areas, and 2) using a responsible, accountable, consulted, and informed (RACI) template to clearly define roles and responsibilities within functional areas and between stakeholders (71).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Stakeholder Power Grid: This is a 2x2 matrix that classifies stakeholders along two dimensions, "influence/power" and "interest/motivation":

  • High power + high interest = Manage Carefully: shareholders, parent company, regulatory authorities, HSE staff. These people have both decision-making power and a strong interest in the outcome, so they are the group that most needs proactive communication and management.
  • High power + low interest = Manage Satisfaction: senior management, the quality unit. They have the authority to veto decisions but may not be deeply involved in day-to-day details, so you need to make sure they are satisfied with the overall direction.
  • Low power + high interest = Keep Informed: the project team, suppliers, customers. They care a great deal but have limited influence, so they should receive regular updates.
  • Low power + low interest = Be Informed: internal memos, industry events, and similar channels are sufficient.

Key Notes

Consequences of a lack of stakeholder management: TR66 mentions a common pitfall - when multiple SUS technologies or suppliers are introduced at the same time without coordination, different departments may each choose different types of sterile connectors or storage bags, leading to failed standardization and a fragmented process.

This is especially common in a CDMO, because the products of different clients may drive different technology choices.

Analogy

Stakeholder management is like planning a big wedding. The couple's parents (high power + high interest) need to be "managed carefully", with every decision fully communicated; the company boss (high power + low interest) only needs to know when you will be taking leave; friends (low power + high interest) need to be told the date and venue; and the other colleagues (low power + low interest) only need to receive a notice email.

Understanding the RACI Matrix

RACI Template: A tool for defining roles and responsibilities:

  • R (Responsible): the person who actually does the work
  • A (Accountable): the person ultimately answerable for the result (only one A per task)
  • C (Consulted): experts who provide input
  • I (Informed): people who need to be told the outcome

Practice Questions

  1. In your organization, which stakeholders would fall in the "Manage Carefully" quadrant of an SUS implementation project? Should the regulatory affairs department be in this quadrant?
  2. If a supplier (such as Sartorius or Pall) moves from "Keep Informed" to "Manage Carefully" in your Power Grid, what does that mean?
7.2.2 Risk Management

Original Text

As shown in Figure 7.1-1🔍, risk management is important throughout the lifecycle of a project (specifically from Sections 7.4 to 7.7). Application of a quality risk management (QRM) program during SUS project implementation confers benefits on the manufacturer, patients, quality auditors, and regulators. These benefits come from an understanding of product and process risks, individual assessments, mitigation or acceptance, communication, and review as outlined in Section 3.3.1 (4,7,72).

It is critical for an organization to use a QRM program to underpin product quality. An effective QRM program must be organized as a team effort with regular review meetings involving stakeholders. A company charter should provide policies and guidelines for when and how to apply risk management, along with the necessary implementation level and control. The risk assessment and risk mitigation tools used should be commensurate with the objectives of the project and the quality impact of the SUS being addressed.

Risk Assessment Tools

  • Information gathering (data mining) and data evaluation
  • Ad hoc applications (e.g., using simple tables that delineate some risks associated with predefined implementation scenarios)
  • Directional-risk profiles
  • Fishbone analysis
  • More robust applications such as failure mode and effects analysis (FMEA), in which events are assessed in terms of impact, severity, probability, and detectability

Risk Mitigation Tools

  • Identification and ranking of risks, their impact, and acceptance or mitigation (i.e., applying and comparing current and future states)
  • At a minor level, mitigation can range from the requirement for further data mining to simple process adjustments
  • For more elaborate subjects, quality audits that employ an efficient document management system are appropriate
  • A specified risk-review period commensurate with the risk in question

In-depth reviews of common risk-assessment techniques and their applications in risk management are available in specialized literature (4,7,72,73). A more detailed evaluation of the risks associated with SUS implementation and the necessary qualification and validation studies is reviewed in Section 5.2. Some examples of QRM application in SUS implementation are shown in Tables 7.2.3-1 to 7.2.3-3.

Once the severity of the risk and the likelihood of its detection have been assessed, the most suitable detection tool can be selected.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Quality Risk Management, QRM: Based on the ICH Q9 framework, QRM is a systematic process that includes:

  • Risk Identification: finding what could go wrong
  • Risk Analysis: estimating the probability of occurrence and the degree of impact
  • Risk Evaluation: deciding whether the risk is acceptable
  • Risk Control: mitigating or accepting the risk
  • Risk Communication: sharing the results with stakeholders
  • Risk Review: reviewing and updating periodically

Hierarchy of Risk Assessment Tools

The tools listed in TR66 run from simple to complex, forming a progressive toolkit:

  • Simplest: Data Mining - collecting historical data and supplier information
  • Simple: Ad Hoc risk tables - using a simple table to list the risks of predefined scenarios
  • Intermediate: Directional Risk Profile - using a matrix to rank risk priorities
  • Intermediate: Fishbone (Ishikawa) diagram - finding root causes
  • Most rigorous: FMEA - systematically assessing impact, severity, probability of occurrence, and detectability

Which tool to choose depends on the severity of the risk and the complexity of the project - this is the principle of being "commensurate".

Analogy

Choosing a risk assessment tool is like seeing a doctor. For a minor cold (low risk), taking your temperature and some medicine is enough (data mining + ad hoc); for recurring headaches (medium risk), you need an X-ray or CT (fishbone analysis); if a serious illness is suspected (high risk, impact on product quality), you need a full health check including MRI and blood analysis (FMEA).

Key Notes

Teamwork is key: TR66 specifically emphasizes that QRM must be a "team effort", with regular review meetings that involve stakeholders. In practice, many SUS risk assessments fail not because the wrong tool was chosen, but because the right people were not included. For example, if the supplier's technical representative is not invited to the risk assessment meeting, important material compatibility risks may be missed.

Practice Questions

  1. In an SUS implementation project, what types of risk are suited to a simple ad hoc risk table? What types require a full FMEA?
  2. TR66 mentions a "risk-review period commensurate with the risk". For extractables/leachables risk, how often do you think it should be reviewed? Why?
Risk Management Tables (7.2.3-1 to 7.2.3-3)

Original Text

Table 7.2.3-1🔍: Guidelines for the Application of Risk Management During SUS Implementation

Table 7.2.3-2🔍: Directional Risk Profile of SUS Items and Applications

Table 7.2.3-3🔍: Risk Management and Mitigation in Current and Future SUS Implementation

Tutorial Commentary

Table 7.2.3-1🔍 Explained: Risk Level and Tool Selection

This table establishes a clear decision logic:

  • Red zone (impact on product quality): FMEA must be used, whether or not the risk is detectable. This is the strictest requirement because patient safety is involved.
  • Orange zone (possible impact on quality/yield): use a combination of tools of intermediate complexity (data mining + fishbone + risk profiling).
  • Green zone (no impact): continuous verification is all that is needed.

Note: Detectability is another key dimension in choosing the tool. Even where severity is the same, a risk that is not easily detected calls for a more rigorous tool.

Table 7.2.3-2🔍 Explained: Directional Risk Profile

This matrix is a practical decision tool for SUS supplier qualification:

  • Highest risk (red): Cell & virus culture, Drug product formulation, Sterile connectors. These come into direct contact with the product or involve aseptic operations.
  • Lowest risk (green): Tubing & connectors, and externally sourced manifolds. These are relatively simple and highly standardized.
  • Worth noting: in-house assembled manifolds carry a higher risk than externally sourced ones, because assembly quality depends on operator skill and the internal QC system.

Table 7.2.3-3🔍 Explained: Risk Evolution from Current State to Future State

This table depicts the "maturity evolution" of risk management during SUS implementation:

  • Current State: everything is "does not exist" or "unknown" - the typical state before a project starts.
  • Future State: all systems are established and operating continuously - the ideal state after S7 (Launch).

The transition from Current to Future is achieved through the work of stages S1-S7.

Analogy

The evolution described in Table 7.2.3-3🔍 is like opening a new restaurant: before opening, your ingredient suppliers (unknown), food safety plan (does not exist), and hygiene inspection process (does not exist) are all blank. As you build the supply chain step by step, draw up the HACCP plan, and pass the health department inspection, you gradually evolve from "nothing in place" to "everything ready and continuously improving". SUS implementation follows the same logic.

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: Suppose your CDMO is introducing an SU bioreactor for cell culture. According to Table 7.2.3-2🔍, this falls under "High Complexity + High Risk of Application" = the highest risk level (red). This means:

  • A full risk assessment using FMEA is required
  • Supplier qualification must be at the strictest level
  • Full extractables/leachables testing is needed
  • No Stage-Gate can be skipped

Practice Questions

  1. In Table 7.2.3-2🔍, why are "in-house assembled manifolds" riskier than "externally sourced manifolds"? What does this imply for a CDMO's make-vs-buy decision?
  2. If a company's Table 7.2.3-3🔍 current state shows most items still at "Current" but S6 (Testing & Validation) has already started, what problem does this indicate?
7.2.3 Process Validation and Verification (PVV)

Original Text

In pharmaceutical manufacturing, process validation (PV) is the collection and evaluation of data from the process design stage through commercial production that establishes scientific evidence that a process is capable of consistently delivering quality product (8). The U.S FDA and EMA consider PV a requirement in both general and specific terms in Good Manufacturing Practice (74,75). Verification is a systematic approach for ensuring that manufacturing systems (acting either singly or in combination) are suitable for their intended use, have been properly installed, and are operating correctly. This is an umbrella term that encompasses all types of activities to assuring systems are fit for use, such as qualification, commissioning and qualification, verification, system validation, and others (18). Verification (or process verification) is an important concept for SUSs and is a primary control strategy for sourcing from suppliers. This remains active throughout the lifecycle of the project.

PVV is a tool that requires an integrated project management approach that involves all pertinent stakeholders (e.g., a cross-company project team) and full support from management. A science- and risk-based approach to PVV tends to generate a thorough understanding of the manufacturing process, which in turn enhances product quality and patient safety (8,74). ICH guidance documents, such as ICH Q8, Q9, and Q10, provide useful guidelines for development, risk analysis, and product control (3-5). A draft guideline on process validation for the manufacture of biotechnology-derived active substances and a guideline on process validation for finished products has been published by EMA (75,76).

SUS process validation and verification is normally performed using a lifecycle approach. The FDA 2011 process validation guidance and PDA Technical Report 60: Process Validation - A Lifecycle Approach provide useful models (8,74). Within that model, SUS implementation becomes part of process validation stages 1 and 2 (S1 and S2). Productive use of SUS is part of process validation S3. Additional technical guidance is available for this and for a phased approach to quality systems and the application of GMP as well (63,77).

Continuous quality verification can also be used. The knowledge obtained as the product lifecycle progresses will allow a framework to be established in order to attain mitigation of risks, reduction of process variability, enhancement of process capability, definition of enhancement of process design space, and improvements in product quality. These may lead to other benefits that address both compliance and operational goals (e.g., continuous process improvement) (78). Figure 7.2.3-1🔍 illustrates two component elements—validation prerequisites and conformance batches—for process validation S2 that confer greater clarity on SUS implementation.

"SUS process validation and verification is normally performed using a lifecycle approach. Within that model, SUS implementation becomes part of process validation stages 1 and 2 (S1 and S2). Productive use of SUS is part of process validation S3."

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Process Validation vs Verification: It is essential to understand the difference between these two terms:

  • Process Validation (PV): the collection and evaluation of data, from process design through commercial production, to establish scientific evidence that the process is capable of "consistently" delivering a quality product. This is an FDA/EMA GMP requirement.
  • Verification: a broader umbrella term covering all activities that ensure a manufacturing system is fit for its intended use. It includes qualification, commissioning, system validation, and so on.

In the SUS context, verification is especially important because it is the primary control strategy for supplier quality. You cannot perform a full PV at the supplier's site, but you can confirm through verification activities that the SUS delivered by the supplier meets requirements.

The FDA Process Validation Lifecycle Model

The FDA 2011 process validation guidance divides PV into three stages, and TR66 maps SUS implementation onto this model:

  • Stage 1 - Process Design: corresponds to the early work of SUS implementation - defining process parameters, CQAs, and the design space
  • Stage 2 - Process Qualification: corresponds to the SUS validation prerequisites + conformance batches - demonstrating that the process is reproducible at commercial scale
  • Stage 3 - Continued Process Verification: corresponds to routine SUS production operations - ongoing monitoring to ensure the process remains in a validated state

Analogy

The relationship between PV and verification is like that between "taking a driving test" and "routine vehicle checks". Process Validation is like passing the driving test - you must prove you are able to drive safely (consistently deliver quality). Verification is like checking tire pressure, oil, and brakes before every trip - making sure the car (the system) is still fit for use. For SUS, because "new car parts" (single-use components) are used every time, verification before each use is especially important.

Key Notes

Continuous Quality Verification: TR66 describes this as an advanced concept in which, as the product lifecycle progresses, the accumulated knowledge can be used to:

  • mitigate risks
  • reduce process variability
  • enhance process capability
  • define/expand the design space
  • improve product quality

This is fully consistent with the ICH Q10 concept of continual improvement.

Regulatory Alignment

The relationship between ICH Q8/Q9/Q10 and SUS implementation:

  • ICH Q8 (Pharmaceutical Development): provides the QbD framework and defines the design space and CQAs
  • ICH Q9 (Quality Risk Management): provides the risk management methodology
  • ICH Q10 (Pharmaceutical Quality System): provides the framework for the quality system and continual improvement

Stages S3-S7 of TR66 can be mapped directly to the requirements of these three ICH guidelines, giving a clear bridge between SUS implementation and regulatory expectations.

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: When preparing the PV documentation package (validation package) for delivery to a client, you need to be clear about which FDA PV stage each SUS-related validation activity belongs to. For example:

  • PV Stage 1: material compatibility studies, extractables assessment, and confirmation of process parameter ranges for the SU bioreactor
  • PV Stage 2: conformance batches run with the SU bioreactor (usually 3 batches), including leachables analysis
  • PV Stage 3: incoming inspection of SUS materials, supplier audits, and ongoing trend monitoring in routine production

Practice Questions

  1. Why does TR66 say that verification is the "primary control strategy" for SUS supplier management? How does this differ from the control strategy for traditional stainless steel equipment (MUS)?
  2. If your SUS supplier suddenly announces a change in the film material of the SU bag, which stage of the FDA PV lifecycle model would this trigger activities in? What would you need to redo?
  3. How can Continuous Quality Verification help a CDMO demonstrate the maturity of its SUS process to the client when the contract comes up for renewal?
PDA Technical Report No. 66 (2014): Single-Use Systems | Sections 7.4-7.5 | p88-p99

Learning Objectives for This Section

  • Understand the objectives and key deliverable of the Implementation S4 (Business Case) stage: the Project Execution Plan (PEP)
  • Master how to plan the facility-level, equipment-level and operational-level integration plans
  • Learn the risk-management approach to supplier selection and supply-chain review
  • Understand the user requirements, layout and design, specification and standardization strategy of Implementation S5 (Development)
  • Recognize the key considerations for building an Extractables & Leachables (E&L) database and for the procurement process
7.4 Implementation S4: Business Case

Original Text

This stage of implementation concerns the different business and technical scenarios that can influence user requirements and the implementation process. Examples of key business drivers include:

  • Whether the project concerns drug substance or drug product
  • Whether the project concerns the manufacture of preclinical material, investigational medicinal products (IMPs), or licensed product
  • Whether the product is a biological or chemical entity
  • Containment requirements

The main documentary output from S4 is the project execution plan (PEP). Important parameters for preparing the PEP (such as facility and process integration, technological survey, supplier selection, and supply chain) are discussed in the paragraphs that follow. Further details on the evaluation of business drivers can be found in Section 5.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Implementation S4: Business Case - After the scope has been defined in S3, S4 focuses on turning the SUS strategy into a concrete business and technical solution. The core output of this stage is the Project Execution Plan (PEP), the main roadmap document for the whole implementation project.

Business Drivers - The key business factors that shape the direction of SUS implementation, including product type (active pharmaceutical ingredient vs. drug product), product life-cycle stage (preclinical vs. GMP manufacturing), product nature (biologic vs. chemical entity) and containment needs.

Analogy

The S4 stage is like the business plan you write before building a new plant. Imagine opening a restaurant: before any fit-out begins, you must decide what to serve (product type), who the target customers are (clinical stage vs. commercial production), whether it is Western or Chinese cuisine (biologic vs. chemical entity), and whether you need special ventilation equipment (containment needs). The PEP is that complete business plan for opening the restaurant.

Key Notes

The PEP is the core output of S4. It defines not only the budget, schedule and quality objectives, but also integrates every aspect, including facility, process and supply chain. A PEP with an incomplete structure will directly cause confusion and delays in later implementation stages. For a CDMO, each client project should have its own PEP to ensure SUS implementation is systematic.

7.4.1 Project Execution Plan

Original Text

The purpose of the PEP is to ensure that the goals of the project (i.e., budget, time, and quality) are met. It defines and describes:

  • The project parameters
  • Execution strategy
  • Management
  • Compliance

A detailed and structured PEP is critical to a well-organized implementation project. Other project documents such as a validation master plan (VMP) and user requirements may be used in PEP preparation or execution. Typically, the PEP would address the headings shown in Table 7.4.1-1. Materials management (Section 7.7.1) contains some additional points that may need to be addressed in the PEP. An example of a PEP for an SUS facility can be found in Section 9, Appendix 2. Additional examples of checklists, implementation experiences, and points to consider can be found in the literature (83–89).

Table 7.4.1-1🔍: Typical Contents of the SUS Project Execution Plan

The PEP, sometimes called a project implementation plan (PIP), provides a thorough and detailed description of the project. It complements other project documents such as the VMP, user requirements, and other non-GxP documentation, including environmental safety, waste-handling requirements, and inspections and building permits. Since not all projects involve the same level of complexity, the PEP may need to be modified accordingly. Therefore, this table is for illustrative purposes.
