The final content of the PEP will depend on the approach to the project, risk-management level, and business drivers such as the nature of the product, lifecycle requirements, engineering policies, and project scale.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

PEP (Project Execution Plan) - This is the "master document" for SUS implementation. It covers 12 main topics, from project introduction, scope definition, contract documents, quality strategy and risk management through to HSE (health, safety and environment), procurement and installation, training, and handover and support.

VMP (Validation Master Plan) - The validation planning document that runs in parallel with the PEP and defines the validation strategy, scope and schedule for the entire SUS.

Key Notes

The 12-chapter structure in Table 7.4.1-1🔍 provides a complete blueprint for the PEP. Note a few points that are easily overlooked:

  • Chapter 6, Organization: includes a conflict-resolution mechanism, which is essential for SUS projects involving many departments
  • Chapter 8, Risk Management: this is not only risk assessment; it also needs concrete mitigation strategies and plans
  • Chapter 11, Handover: includes Materials Management, which is key to keeping the SUS operating over time

The depth of PEP content should be adjusted to project complexity: a small SUS project can use a lean PEP, while a large facility build requires a fully developed one.

Analogy

The 12 chapters of the PEP are like the "architectural design specification" for building a house. Chapters 1-4 are "what we are going to build"; chapters 5-6 are "who builds it and how"; chapters 7-8 are "how quality and risk are managed"; chapter 9 is "safety and environment"; chapters 10-11 are "actual construction through to acceptance and handover"; and chapter 12 is "appendices and references". If any part is missing, the house can go wrong.

Practice Questions

  1. If your CDMO is building SUS production lines for biologic drug products for three different clients at the same time, which chapters of the PEP most need to be customized?
  2. Why does the PEP need to include a Conflict Resolution mechanism? Give an example of a cross-department conflict that could occur during SUS implementation.
7.4.2 Process and Facility Requirements: Basis of Approach

Original Text

Process and facility requirements are key business drivers, and their consideration forms an important part of the PEP preparation. SUSs can be integrated and deployed within existing processes and manufacturing facilities. However, when a high level of SUS integration is necessary, new facilities may be required because of the radical structural differences between SU and MU systems. For PEP preparation, SUS integration must be considered from the point of view of the engineering service, suppliers, and the end user. The outputs are three relatively simple documents which outline the implementation strategy: facility-, implementation-, and operational-level plans.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

SUS integration needs to be planned from three different perspectives, producing three documents:

  • Facility-Level Plan - how the SUS fits into the facility design space
  • Implementation-Level Plan - the parameters for suppliers and end users in producing, marketing and supplying SUS
  • Operational-Level Plan - the parameters for assembling, connecting and deploying the SUS

Key Notes

When the degree of SUS integration is high, a completely new facility design may be needed. This is because SU and MU systems differ fundamentally in structure: an SU system needs no CIP/SIP piping and no extensive utility connections, but it needs more storage space, waste-handling areas and personnel operating flow. This decision point has a major impact on a CDMO's capital expenditure.

7.4.3-7.4.5 Three-Level Integration Plans

Original Text

7.4.3 Facility-Level Plan

This plan shows how the SUS fits into the facility design space together with other process equipment, and it summarizes different functional situations.

7.4.4 Implementation-Level Plan

This contains the parameters that affect the supplier during the production, marketing, and supply of SUS for off-the-shelf or customized systems. In addition, it describes the parameters that influence the end user during the creation of self-made or in-house SUSs.

7.4.5 Operational-Level Plan

This describes the parameters for SUS integration (i.e., assembly, connection, and deployment) in readiness for manufacturing. It is essential to review each of these factors thoroughly because they provide the necessary content to complete the PEP and subsequent documents. The operational-level plan contains elements from manufacturing and SUS strategies. The input and output documents for SUS integration are illustrated in Figure 7.4.5-1.

[Figure 7.4.5-1🔍: SUS Process and Facility Integration]

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The three levels of integration plans form a planning framework running from the macro to the micro:

  • Facility-Level - answers "Where does the SUS sit in the plant?"
  • Implementation-Level - answers "Where does the SUS equipment come from? Is it bought in or made in-house?"
  • Operational-Level - answers "How is the SUS assembled, connected and deployed until it is ready to start production?"

Analogy

The three levels of plans are like planning a large banquet: Facility-Level is choosing the venue and space layout (how the tables and chairs in the banquet hall are arranged); Implementation-Level is deciding where the food and tableware come from (cook it yourself or hire a caterer? buy standard tableware or order custom?); Operational-Level is the preparation workflow on the day (how dishes are served, how tableware is set, how the service staff move around).

Key Notes

The Implementation-Level Plan mentions two sources of SUS: off-the-shelf and self-made. This choice has a major impact on risk management: self-made SUS offers high flexibility, but you must take on all the quality responsibilities yourself, including specification setting, batch records, qualification and stability studies.

7.4.6 Facility-Level Integration Plan

Original Text

This is normally a concise document that describes the integration of the SUS into the facility design space (87). Robust process knowledge is a prerequisite for defining the various operational situations of the facility. It is critical to adopt a holistic approach because several factors (e.g., compliance, environment, operational requirements, and the compatibility of processes and equipment) may affect the successful operation of the SUS. These factors depend on the nature and properties of the product (e.g., biological or chemical entity) and the requirements for containment. Figure 7.4.6-1🔍 provides a conceptual representation of how the successive steps in facility-level integration may be achieved. Some of the most relevant parameters to consider include:

  • Product type
  • Sterile or nonsterile processing
  • Product volume
  • Lifecycle
  • Available surface footprint
  • Dependence on utilities
  • Flexibility of facility design
  • Avoiding cross-contamination
  • Level of segregation and dedication
  • Environment (e.g., room air grade, containment)
  • Personnel interactions
  • Process validation (may be necessary when the equipment used in scale-up changes in terms of volume, aspect ratio, or between MUS and SUS)

[Figure 7.4.6-1🔍: Process and Facility Considerations for SUS Implementation]

The chart on the left side of the figure depicts a holistic vision of a facility from the top-level processes that created it (outside rings) to the process design space (inside ring). The key points to address during implementation are illustrated as a stakeholder pathway. The triangle and the down-facing arrow on the right show how the risk from biological or chemical potency may affect the amount of dedication/segregation and containment necessary at all levels within the manufacturing site.

A structured risk-based approach should be used to prepare the facility plan, starting with the manufacturing strategy prepared in S1 and S2, and the SUS implementation strategy prepared in S3.

Process mapping and regulatory assessment tables (RATs) are additional tools for defining facility requirements. Process mapping identifies operating scenarios and potential CQAs, while a RAT enables cross-referencing to key GMP factors and other regulatory requirements where it is appropriate for the user to document their policy regarding those regulations (i.e., how they intend to practically address those requirements) (87). The use of good engineering practice (GEP) also complements this strategy by providing a framework for the application of key concepts (e.g., project engineering, common practices, and operations and maintenance) (90). Examples include non-GxP compliance, design reviews, change management, equipment maintenance, and lifecycle management.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Facility Design Space - the physical and functional space in the facility available for SUS integration. This is not only a matter of floor area; it also involves the overall planning of cleanliness classification, utility layout, personnel flow and material flow.

RAT (Regulatory Assessment Table) - a practical tool for cross-referencing regulatory requirements against the user's specific response. For example, for a given clause of ICH Q7A, the RAT records the original text of the clause together with how the user plans to meet that requirement.

GEP (Good Engineering Practice) - a methodology that provides a systematic framework for engineering projects, covering project engineering, common practices, operations and maintenance.

Key Notes

The list of 12 facility-level integration parameters is very important. Pay particular attention to the following:

  • Cross-contamination avoidance - SUS is itself an effective means of reducing cross-contamination risk, but facility design must still consider segregation of personnel flow and material flow
  • Process validation - when moving from MUS to SUS, even if the process is the same, the change in equipment type (such as volume ratio and mixing method) may require process validation to be repeated
  • Dependence on utilities - SUS facilities are generally less dependent on utilities such as WFI and pure steam, but need more storage and waste handling

Analogy

Facility-level integration is like planning a new building in an existing neighborhood. You cannot look only at the design of the building itself (the SUS equipment); you must also consider the surrounding roads (material flow), water and power supply (utilities), parking (storage space), fire access (safety regulations), the impact on neighbors (cross-contamination risk) and municipal regulations (GMP requirements). The RAT is the checklist you use to verify, clause by clause, that the building complies with the code.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does TR66 stress that facility integration requires a "holistic approach"? If you focus on only a single factor (such as equipment size), what risks might be missed?
  2. Explain how a RAT helps a CDMO quickly demonstrate its compliance strategy during a regulatory inspection.
7.4.6 Regulatory Assessment Table (RAT) Example

Table 7.4.6-1🔍: Example of a Regulatory Assessment Table

ICH Q7A - Basic Requirements for Active Substances Used as Starting Materials: Specific Guidance for APIs Manufactured by Cell Culture/Fermentation

This table is an example based on Chapter 18 of ICH Q7A (EU-GMP Part II) (2). Other regulatory texts can also be exploited in this manner. A RAT is used to cull the sections of a regulatory text that are most applicable to the project and to document the company's position with regard to each chosen paragraph. This helps to identify the key strategies or policies that may be required. A RAT can be a useful supplement as part of a risk-management-based approach to developing situations for facility-operating scenarios.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

How the RAT (Regulatory Assessment Table) works in practice:

  • Left column: lists the relevant clause numbers and text of the regulation
  • Right column: records the user's (company's) specific policy for each clause

This "regulatory text vs. company policy" format lets both inspectors and internal teams see clearly how each regulatory requirement is met.

Key Notes

Note several important policy statements in Table 7.4.6-1🔍:

  • Policy for 18.30: all sterile additions are made in closed systems, which is a core advantage of SUS
  • Policy for 18.31: open connections must be made in an EU-GMP Grade A environment with a Grade B background, a typical risk control for mixed SUS-MUS operations
  • Policy for 18.34: upstream process equipment uses SUS, used only once, which directly eliminates the need for cleaning validation
  • Policy for 18.38: MUS parts in a hybrid system (such as steel sampling valves) require a validated CIP procedure

Practical Application

CDMOs are advised to build their own RAT for every new SUS implementation project. In addition to ICH Q7A, RATs can also be built for the following regulations:

  • EU GMP Annex 1 (Manufacture of Sterile Medicinal Products)
  • FDA 21 CFR 211（cGMP）
  • PIC/S GMP Guidelines
  • ICH Q8-Q12 (quality by design and lifecycle management)

A RAT can serve as a "preventive preparation tool" for regulatory inspections, and also as a common language for communicating regulatory requirements across departments.

7.4.7 Equipment-Level Integration Plan

Original Text

This brief document describes factors influencing the supplier or end user during the construction, marketing, and supply of the SUS as an off-the-shelf, customized, or self-made system. Some of the parameters for verification are:

  • Position in the product lifecycle and making sure that it is compatible with future GMP and quality systems
  • Working volumes
  • Transport conditions
  • Handling or moving the system, and protecting it during this process
  • Physical support (e.g., whether the system can support itself or requires a supporting structure)
  • Physical parameters
  • Flow rates for gases and liquids
  • Temperature range (e.g., cold storage to process conditions)
  • Heating and cooling requirements
  • Agitation and mixing
  • Pressure
  • Sterilization or chemical sanitation cycles
  • Chemical compatibilities (e.g., organic solvents, gas permeability)
  • Direct-impact phenomena (e.g., cellular growth kinetics)
  • Biocontainment capabilities
  • Standardization
  • Control systems and their validation
  • Autonomy [i.e., does the system require an uninterrupted power supply (UPS) in case of power failure?]

At S4, it is generally assumed that 1) sufficient process knowledge exists to enable a coherent review of the design factors that must be built into the SU, and 2) this would be performed from a risk-based perspective. Some of the most relevant design factors are:

  • Construction materials
  • Physical properties
  • Microbial control methods
  • Size and volume
  • Physical parameters
  • Direct-impact phenomena (e.g., contact time, E&L, biocompatibility)

For many end users, establishing a close partnership with major SUS suppliers allows them to leverage their extensive experience in the process field. Adopting customized SUS or using inexperienced suppliers would increase risk, although this may be mitigated by on-site SUS testing. The onus is on the end user to ascertain the compatibility of process, equipment, and facility design space from a risk-based perspective (Sections 7.4.10 and 7.7).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The Equipment-Level Integration Plan covers 18 validation parameters, which can be grouped into several broad categories:

  • Physical and mechanical: working volume, pressure, temperature range, agitation and mixing, physical support
  • Chemical compatibility: organic solvents, gas permeability, chemical sanitization cycles
  • Biological impact: cell growth kinetics, biocontainment capability
  • Logistics: transport conditions, handling protection
  • System integration: control system validation, uninterruptible power supply (UPS), standardization

Key Notes

The S4 stage rests on two key assumptions: (1) sufficient process knowledge is already available, and (2) design review is based on a risk-based approach. This means that entering S4 without adequate process knowledge can lead to incorrect equipment specifications, and later changes are extremely costly.

Another important point: building partnerships with major SUS suppliers can reduce risk, because they have extensive application experience. Using customized SUS or inexperienced suppliers increases risk, but this can be mitigated through on-site testing. This bears directly on a CDMO's supplier management strategy.

Analogy

Equipment-level integration is like choosing a car engine. You cannot look at horsepower (working volume) alone; you must also consider fuel consumption (utility dependence), exhaust output (waste generation), cooling efficiency (heating/cooling needs), durability (chemical compatibility), airbags (biocontainment), self-driving features (control system) and a backup battery (UPS). Neglecting any one parameter can keep the whole car from running properly.

Practice Questions

  1. Of the 18 validation parameters at equipment level, which are especially critical for SUS used with biologics, and which are more important for SUS used with chemical drugs?
  2. Why does TR66 recommend building partnerships with "major SUS suppliers" rather than using many small suppliers? Analyze this from a risk-management perspective.
7.4.8 Operational-Level Integration Plan

Original Text

This describes the parameters for SUS integration (i.e., assembly, connection, and deployment) in readiness for manufacturing. A risk-based approach should be used because of the structural and functional diversity of SUSs (91). Some of the important parameters to consider during deployment include:

  • Packaging and container integrity — It must be determined whether preuse leak testing of SUS is feasible. Manufacturing, shipping, handling, and operational procedures should be qualified to maintain SUS integrity. This can be achieved by implementing control strategies within the end user's organization and between supplier and end user.
  • SUS testing and release — Testing and release vary with use. The same off-the-shelf system may have different test and release criteria depending on process-, product-, regulatory-, and user-imposed requirements.
  • Reuse of components — In general, reuse of SUSs is not recommended. The policy for this should have been established in the SUS strategy (Section 7.2.1.7). However, there are circumstances in which the reuse of SUS may be acceptable (e.g., use of a container as a mixing vessel or reservoir for buffer concentrates in which there are no broken connections). A risk analysis should be performed to determine if the risks are acceptable.
  • Training — Appropriate personnel training is essential because the collapse of a bag may lead to stress cracks that could result in a loss of structural and functional integrity. Training topics might include the use of different sterile connection and disconnection devices or techniques for avoiding bag creases or holes during deployment.
  • Shelf life — A coherent expiration and shelf-life policy should be implemented, especially in situations in which the end user has a large number of different SUSs from multiple suppliers or local, self-made assemblies.
  • Quality assurance — Ensuring quality during deployment should be addressed at an early stage of implementation. Technical diligence may enhance this goal (Section 7.4.10 and 7.7.3).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Operational-Level Integration is the key transition at which the SUS changes from "static equipment" into a "usable system". Six key parameters cover the whole flow from delivery to use online:

  • Preuse leak testing - confirms that the SUS was not damaged during transport and storage
  • Reuse policy - reuse is not recommended in principle, but may be acceptable under specific conditions (such as a buffer container that has not been disconnected)
  • Technical diligence - an in-depth understanding of the SUS product, the supplier and process compatibility

Key Notes

The aspect of operational-level integration that is most easily overlooked but has the greatest impact is personnel training. Collapse of a bag can cause stress cracks and in turn damage structural and functional integrity, which is a very real operational risk. Training topics should include:

  • Use of the different types of aseptic connection and disconnection devices
  • Techniques for avoiding creases or holes in the bag during deployment
  • Correct handling posture and methods

For a CDMO, the quality of operators' SUS-specific training directly determines product quality and batch success rate.

Analogy

Operational-level integration is like a pilot's pre-flight checklist. However well the aircraft is designed (equipment level) and however perfect the runway is (facility level), flight safety cannot be guaranteed if the pre-flight instrument check (leak testing), crew training (Training) and fuel expiry (Shelf life) are not in place. Every seemingly trivial check item can decide the success or failure of the mission.

Practice Questions

  1. Under what circumstances might reuse of an SUS be acceptable? What risk analysis does the user need to perform?
  2. Why might the same off-the-shelf SUS have different testing and release criteria in different applications? Give an example.
7.4.9 Technological Survey

Original Text

The aim of this document is to complement the information gathered as part of the manufacturing strategy (Sections 3.0, 7.0, and 7.1). Depending on when the manufacturing strategy is written, an update to the document may be necessary. However, if a greater level of information is obtained in S1 and entry into the full implementation process takes place at G4, then this survey can be omitted. Adaptations of the implementation road map are addressed in Section 7.1. However, if the organization of the road map is changed, care should be exercised that the implementation process does not lose structure and that the comprehensive treatment of all of the elements necessary remains in place.

The components for SUS implementation often come from a range of suppliers, and a SWOT-type assessment (i.e., strengths, weaknesses, opportunities, and threats) may be used to evaluate and compare solutions. The aim is to make sure that the best available technology is selected while also obtaining pertinent information for including any further requirements in the specification process. Sources of information may include:

  • Suppliers
  • In-house know-how
  • Industry networks and associations
  • Journals
  • Conferences
  • Industry guidelines

Suppliers may be considered as stakeholders.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The Technological Survey is a systematic investigation of the SUS technology solutions available on the market. Its purpose is to ensure that the Best Available Technology (BAT) is selected.

SWOT Assessment is used here to evaluate and compare different SUS solutions:

  • S (Strengths): the supplier's technical capability, product maturity
  • W (Weaknesses): product limitations, chemical compatibility issues
  • O (Opportunities): new technologies, cost-saving potential
  • T (Threats): supply-chain risks, patent restrictions

Key Notes

The technological survey can be omitted under certain conditions, if sufficient technical information was already obtained in S1. However, if the organizational structure of the implementation roadmap changes, you must ensure that the implementation process does not lose its structure and that all necessary elements are still fully addressed. This warning appears several times in TR66, showing that quality omissions caused by organizational restructuring are a common problem in practice.

Analogy

A technological survey is like market research before buying a house. You look at the reputation of different developers (Suppliers), ask friends who have lived there about their experience (In-house know-how), attend property fairs (Conferences), read architecture magazines (Journals) and join home-buyer communities (Industry networks). A SWOT assessment is then the decision tool you use to compare several candidate properties.

7.4.10 Supplier Selection and Supply-Chain Review

Original Text

The aim of this review is to determine a policy for the identification and selection of suppliers in order to complete the PEP and project planning.

The selection of appropriate SUS suppliers can often be an arduous task that can be made less cumbersome with a risk-management approach. For a successful SUS implementation, it is essential to have a detailed and transparent relationship between suppliers, vendors, and the end user. This requires good communication and understanding of stakeholder management in order to garner the expectations of both the suppliers and the end users. Being able to share pertinent information with suppliers at the early stages of the project may speed up the design process. Note that in order to obtain full benefits from this approach, the exchange of uni- or bilateral confidential disclosure agreements (CDA) may be required. The implementation and management of an end user–supplier relationship via technical diligence is described in Section 7.7.3. Suppliers provide material data, advice on applying typical designs, and the following documents:

  • Process flow diagrams (PFDs)
  • Piping and Instrumentation Diagrams (P&IDs)
  • Component lists
  • Physical layout proposals
  • Operation manuals
  • Pricing
  • Qualification packages, including guidelines on E&L, for example

For both large companies and SMEs, it is critical to prevent the selection of equipment and suppliers from becoming an ad hoc process because that can lead to unnecessary and costly redundancies. In addition, working with too many suppliers may divert limited resources that could otherwise be gainfully employed in other parts of the SUS implementation process. Since the degree of business integration of a particular SUS supplier may depend on the size, location, and SUS assembly concerned, it is important to adopt a risk-mitigation approach to SUS supplier qualification and validation (Section 7.7.3).

Starting materials such as resins and films should be treated as critical raw materials as part of the verification process due to the complexity of SUS implementation (18). It is important to begin the supplier technical diligence (i.e., obtaining a thorough understanding of the SUS product, supplier, and compatibility of the SUS with the end user's process and facility) at this stage. These principles are based on established procedures for critical raw materials as recommended by the Association for Professionals in Infection Control and Epidemiology (APIC) due diligence process, ASTM E2500-07 vendor technical capability as part of the PVV process (Figure 7.2.7.1-1), and quality by design (QbD)-inspired raw materials qualification processes (18,92–94). Full details on how to implement the technical diligence process are described in Section 7.7.3. Note that this task may require the exchange of uni- or bilateral CDAs or nondisclosure agreements (NDA).

The quality of the entire supply chain should be assessed, qualified, and implemented via a structured risk-management process (72). All parties in the supply chain should make sure that their activities maintain business continuity and support the health of patients. SUS equipment that is purchased occasionally may be done on a contractual basis, but a quality and supply agreement must be used to buy routine SUS equipment (Sections 7.5.7 and 7.7.12).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Technical Diligence - one of the core concepts in TR66. It is more than a supplier audit: it is a comprehensive, in-depth understanding of the SUS product itself, the supplier's technical capability, and the compatibility of the SUS with the user's process and facility. The underlying concept derives from the APIC due diligence process and ASTM E2500-07.

CDA/NDA (confidentiality agreement) - to allow transparent exchange of information between supplier and user, a unilateral or bilateral confidentiality agreement may need to be signed. This is especially important in the early project stages and can speed up the design process.

Quality and Supply Agreement - the formal contract for routine purchase of SUS equipment, covering quality standards, supply terms, change notification and so on. A general contract may be used for ad hoc purchases, but routine purchases must have a quality and supply agreement.

Key Notes

Two core risks in supplier selection:

  • Ad hoc selection - the selection of equipment and suppliers must not be an improvised process. It leads to unnecessary duplicate purchases and wasted cost, and can happen regardless of company size.
  • Too many suppliers - working with too many suppliers spreads limited resources thin. Supplier management itself requires a great deal of time and manpower, and too many suppliers squeeze the resources available for other parts of SUS implementation.

TR66 stresses that "starting materials (such as resins and films) should be considered critical raw materials", which means they need the same level of control and review as API starting materials.

Analogy

Supplier selection and management is like putting together an F1 racing team. You need to choose carefully an engine supplier (core SUS supplier), a tire supplier (film and accessory suppliers) and a fuel supplier (raw materials such as resins). Working with too many suppliers is like a race car with parts from too many different brands: integration cost soars and troubleshooting becomes difficult. Technical Diligence is the thorough test drive and technical evaluation you carry out on each supplier before signing. The CDA is the agreement that protects technical secrets between racing teams.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does TR66 classify SUS resins and films as "critical raw materials"? What does this mean for supplier management strategy?
  2. How do contract requirements differ between ad hoc SUS purchases and routine SUS purchases? Why must routine purchases have a Quality and Supply Agreement?
  3. In a CDMO environment, how can the tension between "reducing the number of suppliers" and "maintaining supply-chain resilience" be balanced?
7.4.11 Extractables and Leachables Database

Original Text

It may be convenient for the end user to begin collecting E&L guidance and testing data. Note that this can also be implemented in the next stage, Implementation S5: Development. At a minimum, however, the end user should collect extractables data at this stage.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

E&L Database - E&L is one of the most important safety considerations in SUS implementation:

  • Extractables - compounds extracted from SUS materials under exaggerated (aggressive) conditions
  • Leachables - compounds that migrate from SUS materials into the product under normal process conditions

TR66 recommends that extractables data collection begin no later than the S4 stage, while leachables data can be collected later, in the S5 stage.

Key Notes

Although this subsection is short, the importance of E&L should not be underestimated. Building an E&L database early has the following benefits: (1) unsuitable materials can be excluded already at the supplier selection stage; (2) it provides baseline data for later specification setting and validation; (3) complete E&L data are needed for regulatory submissions. For a CDMO, a systematic E&L database is an important asset across client projects.

7.5 Implementation S5: Development

Original Text

During this stage, the SUS user requirements are developed, including layout and design, specifications, and standardization. Complete user requirements should precede the procurement process (e.g., commercial negotiations between strategic project partners or raising a purchase order).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Implementation S5: Development - After the business case has been established in S4, S5 focuses on turning the strategy into concrete user requirements documents. The key outputs of this stage include user requirements, layout and design, specifications and the standardization policy. Importantly, complete user requirements must be finished before the procurement process.

Key Notes

The core principle of S5 is define requirements first, then start procurement. In practice, many organizations skip complete requirements definition under schedule pressure and go straight to procurement, which often results in mismatched specifications, more change orders and project delays. This order must not be reversed.

7.5.1 User Requirements for Implementation

Original Text

User requirements should be prepared at the start of the development process. The principal user and stakeholders must be identified and their requirements obtained. The user requirements are based on product and process knowledge, critical quality attributes, and any relevant regulatory requirements. The principal user should also provide the justifications for the resources required to design, purchase, qualify, and implement any new process, system, or equipment. These justifications can be developed prior to selecting an SUS or supplier, or they may be developed for a particular system. Some or all of the requirements can be based on a QbD approach. In this case, the product and process-control strategy performed earlier in the QbD process will become a prerequisite for defining the SUS requirements.

User requirements and functional specifications may be recorded together in a single document for low-risk, small-scale SUS. However, multiple user requirements may be needed for complex systems or processes. In this case, it would be critical to avoid conflicts across multiple documents. Any necessary conflicts must be identified and documented. The user requirements are created by the end user or on the end user's behalf; however, some supplier documentation may be necessary to supplement this. Supplier documentation is acceptable if the supplier follows GEP (90), has an appropriate quality system, and is technically capable according to ASTM E2500-07 and PDA Technical Report No. 60: Process Validation — A Lifecycle Approach (8,18). In some cases, the supplier documentation could replace the company document. The user requirements are kept current throughout the system's lifecycle and should be reevaluated after any change in intended use, user needs, or altered materials specifications (e.g., the films used, functional specifications, regulatory assessments or supplier quality audits, technical diligence). An example of a user requirements document is included in Section 8, Appendix 1.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

User Requirements - the foundation document of SUS implementation, based on three pillars:

  • Product and process knowledge - understanding of product characteristics and process parameters
  • CQA (critical quality attributes) - quality characteristics that affect product safety and efficacy
  • Regulatory requirements - applicable GMP and regulatory standards

User requirements can follow a QbD (quality by design) approach, using the product and process control strategy as the premise for defining SUS requirements.

Key Notes

Several important principles apply to managing user requirements documents:

  • Document complexity should match the risk level: a low-risk, small-scale SUS can combine requirements and functional specifications in a single document; complex systems need multiple documents, but conflicts between documents must be avoided
  • Supplier documents can supplement or even replace company documents: provided the supplier follows GEP, has an appropriate quality system and meets the technical capability requirements of ASTM E2500-07
  • User requirements are a living document: they must be kept up to date throughout the system life cycle, and any change of use, change in user requirements or change in material specification requires reassessment

Analogy

A user requirements document is like the measurements taken before a bespoke suit is made. You must first take the body measurements (product and process knowledge), understand the occasion of wear (CQA) and confirm the dress code (regulatory requirements), and only then let the tailor (supplier) start making it. If you have three tailors make the jacket, trousers and waistcoat separately, you must make sure the measurements do not contradict one another (avoiding conflicts between documents). And if you gain or lose weight (process change), the measurements need to be updated.

Practice Questions

  1. Under what conditions can supplier documents replace user requirements documents written by the end user?
  2. If a CDMO's client changes the product formulation (for example, changes the buffer composition), which parts of the user requirements document need to be reassessed?
7.5.2 Preparing User Requirements

Original Text

Brainstorming sessions, workshops, interviews, assessments, and ad hoc discussions may be used to gather pertinent information about the needs of the end user. Other useful information may be obtained from suppliers, case histories, and workflow analyses. These requirements may not be fully defined initially but may be developed further during subsequent phases of the project. It is crucial to determine the level of interest of each stakeholder has in the project and ensure that stakeholders receive adequate and regular communications throughout the SUS process lifecycle. User requirements statements must be written to a level of specificity that:

  • Describes the SUS function
  • Allows end users to verify that the system meets their functional requirements
  • Addresses the compatibility of processes and materials (e.g., polymer films)

However, they must be general enough to avoid constraining the system design. If a supplier will provide specific functionality, then the user requirements must be specific enough to determine that functionality.

Any particular safety concerns and training needs resulting from the requirements should be noted for later implementation. Finally, make sure that a change-control process is in place for this process.

A functional risk-assessment approach may be used for further evaluation of critical requirements for the quality and function of the SUS. For instance, if an integrated SUS is composed of components from multiple sources, it may be necessary to identify the CQA of each component. An example would be a bioreactor system in which the hardware and SUS bags are sourced from different suppliers. Requirements that are not deemed to satisfy CQA may be further evaluated to determine whether they express a regulatory requirement or not. The project team may apply other criteria such as priority, clinical development stage, importance to the business, and other factors that may influence company decisions.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Writing user requirements is an art of balance between two extremes:

  • Specific enough: to describe SUS functions, to allow verification that the requirement is met, and to cover process and material compatibility
  • General enough: so as not to restrict flexibility in system design

Functional Risk Assessment can be used to evaluate critical requirements further. For example, when an integrated SUS consists of components from multiple sources (such as bioreactor hardware and SUS bags from different suppliers), the CQAs of each component need to be identified.

Key Notes

There are many methods for gathering requirements: brainstorming, workshops, interviews, assessments and informal discussions. TR66 specifically notes that requirements may be "not fully defined initially" and developed further in later stages, which reflects the iterative nature of SUS implementation.

Two further key reminders:

  • A change control process must be in place to manage changes to user requirements
  • It must be ensured that stakeholders receive adequate and regular communication throughout the SUS life cycle

Analogy

Writing user requirements is like ordering a pizza: you need to say "I want a 12-inch Margherita, no onions" (specific enough to verify), but you do not need to say "the dough must use 00 Italian flour, water at 38 degrees, fermented for 24 hours" (too specific restricts the chef's professional judgment). If you have a special functional need that depends on a particular supplier (such as gluten-free), you must state it explicitly.

Practice Questions

  1. How can user requirements balance being "specific enough" and "general enough"? Use an SUS mixing bag as an example.
  2. When an integrated SUS has components supplied by multiple suppliers, how should the functional risk assessment be carried out?
7.5.3 Layout and Design

Original Text

The layout and design of the SUS can often be achieved using a standard design format; however, in certain cases, process-specific requirements may result in the need for a unique design process (refer to Section 4). A structured approach like the one from the S88 batch-automation standard may be used to attain completeness of design and control over interfaces because it focuses on the actual batch operations (95). This approach examines three key elements:

  • Operations
  • Equipment (reusable parts)
  • Consumable materials (SU parts)

Start the design process with the functional requirements of the SUS in mind. Figure 7.5.3-1🔍 provides an example of a scope drawing, and the accompanying listing below shows typical considerations for designing a generic SUS within this context.

[Figure 7.5.3-1🔍: Example of SUS Installation Scope Drawing]

This schematic representation of the flow path of an SUS facility shows that design meets both spatial requirements and container staging.

Typical Considerations:

Operations

  • Transportation, delivery, and installation
  • Process operations
  • Ergonomics
  • Sufficient space for operations
  • Manufacturing operations, including the following staging processes: Setting up, Charging, Processing, Feeding, Discharging, Storing, Dismantling

Equipment (Reusable Parts)

  • Bag support
  • Control system
  • Heating/cooling device
  • Pumps
  • Scales
  • Tables
  • Layout (i.e., the physical arrangement)

Materials (SU Parts)

  • Process-containing part (e.g., bag, cartridge, capsule, membrane)
  • Volume
  • Agitation
  • Footprint
  • Sampling
  • Sensors
  • Inlets and outlets
  • Films
  • Tubing
  • Connectors

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

S88 Batch-Automation Standard - an ISA (International Society of Automation) standard that provides a structured approach to designing and controlling batch manufacturing processes. TR66 borrows the framework of this standard to ensure the completeness of SUS design, splitting the design into three key elements:

  • Operations - the complete flow from transport and installation to manufacturing operations
  • Equipment (reusable parts) - bag supports, control systems, pumps and so on
  • Materials (single-use parts) - bags, tubing, connectors and so on

Key Notes

The "Typical Considerations" list in Layout and Design is a practical checklist for SUS design. Pay particular attention to:

  • Ergonomics - SUS operation usually requires more manual handling than MUS (such as bag installation and tubing connections), so ergonomic design directly affects operating efficiency and error rate
  • Staging processes - the seven steps from Setting up to Dismantling define the complete usage cycle of an SUS
  • Sensors in SU parts - single-use sensors (such as pH and DO probes) are the technically more challenging part of an SUS

Analogy

SUS layout design is like designing a modular mobile kitchen (for a large outdoor banquet). Operations is the whole cooking process (from moving ingredients to serving and clearing dishes); Equipment is the equipment that can be used repeatedly (gas stove, work table, refrigerator); Materials is what is discarded after each use (aluminum foil, plastic wrap, baking paper). The S88 methodology ensures that you do not design only the kitchen and forget to plan the material flow from the parking lot to the kitchen.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does TR66 recommend using the framework of the S88 batch-automation standard for SUS design? How does this standard ensure "design completeness"?
  2. Why is ergonomics especially important in SUS Layout and Design? Give an example of a potential scenario in which poor ergonomic design leads to an operational failure.
7.5.4 Specification

Original Text

The design process produces a series of specifications for the proposed SUS. The user requirements cover both MU and SU parts and can be communicated through the following means:

  • Descriptive text
  • Calculation
  • Diagrams, drawings, and PFDs
  • Reference to existing equipment

Implementation of a periodic design review procedure is critical for success. The delivery and installation process must be examined carefully to ensure that the SUS will be delivered in a form that will integrate into the facility.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Specifications are the concrete output of the design process, translating user requirements into measurable, verifiable technical requirements. Specifications can be expressed through descriptive text, calculation results, diagrams and flowcharts, and reference comparisons with existing equipment.

Periodic Design Review is the key mechanism for ensuring that the design continues to meet requirements. Pay special attention to the delivery and installation of the SUS: it must be ensured that the SUS is delivered in a form that can be integrated into the facility.

Key Notes

Specifications cover both the MU and SU parts. This matters because an SUS is usually a hybrid system (MU hardware + SU components), and the specification must consider both. The purpose of periodic design review is to correct problems early when they are found, avoiding the discovery after manufacture that the design does not meet requirements, which leads to expensive rework.

7.5.5 Standardization Policy

Original Text

Although the inherent flexibility at the core of SUS design is an advantage, it could degenerate into a disadvantage by introducing unwelcome variations in the supply chain if left unchecked. Therefore, introducing a standard design policy across the project may reduce the incidence of variations, especially as the scale of operations becomes more complex. Implement a standardization policy as part of the user requirements according to the following guidelines:

  • Working volumes may be chosen so that bag ranges cover a series of application ranges.
  • Connections and material on bags and tubing may be chosen so that several possibilities for connecting are maintained (e.g., providing weldable tubing and a typical connector together).
  • Setting up an SU toolbox consisting of Y pieces, sample manifolds, and lengths of tubing for special or infrequent applications will allow a more specific design element to be attached to a more standardized bag design.
  • An active standardization policy may have been defined as part of the SUS strategy (Section 7.2.1). If so, it may not be necessary to implement it at this stage.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The Standardization Policy is the key strategy for controlling the supply-chain variability that comes with SUS design flexibility. TR66 proposes three practical standardization approaches:

  • Bag ranges - select standardized working volumes so that a series of bag sizes can cover a variety of application needs
  • Connection standardization - unify the way bags and tubing are connected while still retaining multiple connection possibilities
  • SU Toolbox - build a standardized library of accessories (Y-connectors, sampling manifolds, tubing sections) so that special needs can be met by attaching accessories to standardized bag designs

Analogy

A standardization policy is like the design philosophy behind LEGO bricks. The brilliance of LEGO lies in achieving unlimited combinations from a limited number of standardized parts (standard stud spacing and dimensions). The SU Toolbox is your "special parts box": when standard parts cannot meet the need, you can take out a Y-connector or sampling manifold to extend functionality. This is far more efficient than ordering a completely new design for every application.

Key Notes

The flexibility of SUS is a double-edged sword: the advantage is rapid customization; the disadvantage is that, without control, each operator or each project may create a different design, causing supply-chain variability to surge, inventory management to become difficult and training costs to rise. The core objective of a standardization policy is "to maximize repeatability and predictability while retaining the necessary flexibility".

Practice Questions

  1. If a CDMO uses 15 different SUS bag designs across different client projects, what management challenges would this create? How can a standardization policy help solve them?
  2. How does the "SU Toolbox" concept meet special application needs while maintaining standardization?
7.5.6 Extractables and Leachables

Original Text

In some cases, an E&L database collection may have begun in S4 (Sections 5.6 and 7.4.11). If not, E&L data should be compiled now. Although there is no specific regulatory guidance document for E&L in SU systems, the USP, FDA, and EMA have extensive E&L guidance documents for SU technology (96–100). In addition, a number of cross-industry initiatives are in place to standardize the approach for SUS and achieve industry and regulatory consensus or provide a cross-industry database of current knowledge (100–102).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

As of 2014 (when TR66 was published), there was no SUS-specific regulatory guidance document on the E&L regulatory environment, but USP, FDA and EMA had extensive E&L guidance relevant to single-use technologies. The industry also had several cross-industry standardization initiatives working to:

  • Harmonize the E&L assessment methods for SUS
  • Reach industry and regulatory consensus
  • Build a cross-industry knowledge database

Key Notes

If the E&L database has not been started in S4, S5 is the last opportunity to build it. Collecting E&L data takes time and may affect material selection and design decisions. The earlier collection starts, the lower the risk of discovering late that a material is unsuitable and the design must be redone.

7.5.7 Procurement

Original Text

Although procurement procedures will differ from company to company, it is critical to make sure that the end user requirements and other relevant specifications are correctly used and that the SUS-based equipment adheres to those specifications. It is important to maintain a close partnership between suppliers and end users in order to facilitate the procurement process. Some of the key points to consider are as follows:

  • The main procurement process will be outlined and planned in the PEP.
  • Key stakeholders must be identified and their roles and responsibilities must be clearly defined. This is extremely important in cases where procurement precedes the implementation of a quality and supply agreement.
  • Contractual issues must be clearly defined. The end user's legal team should have reviewed and, if necessary, negotiated any modification(s) to the supplier's general terms and conditions of sale to ensure that the user's business, financial, and operational expectations are met (e.g., compliance with Incoterms 2011) (103).
  • Good project planning and management principles must be carefully followed so that the project remains on track throughout its life.
  • A change-notification process should be in place between the equipment supplier and the end user in order to monitor any design changes that take place at this point.
  • Appropriate verifications must be conducted and the acceptance criteria met on a timely basis in order to avoid unnecessary delays.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Procurement is the key step that turns the user requirements and specifications into actual orders. TR66 lists six key considerations:

  • Procurement planning in the PEP - the procurement process should be planned in advance in the PEP
  • Roles and responsibilities of stakeholders - especially when a quality and supply agreement has not yet been established
  • Contract terms - including compliance with Incoterms (international commercial terms)
  • Project management - ensuring the project schedule does not slip
  • Change-Notification Process - monitoring design changes on the supplier side
  • Timely verification of acceptance criteria - avoiding unnecessary delays

Key Notes

The most critical risk-control point in the procurement process is the change-notification process. An SUS supplier may change materials, design or process during the procurement period; without a formal change-notification mechanism, the end user may receive a product that differs from the original specification without knowing it. This has a major impact on product quality and regulatory compliance.

Another easily overlooked point: the Incoterms (international commercial terms) in the contract terms define how risk and cost are shared during transport. SUS are especially vulnerable in transit (film materials are easily damaged), and choosing the right Incoterms clause can make clear where responsibility lies.

Analogy

SUS procurement is like ordering precision medical devices. You cannot simply sign an order and wait for delivery; you need to keep monitoring whether the manufacturer is producing to your specification (acceptance verification), whether raw materials have been quietly swapped (change-notification process), whether the goods are properly protected in transit (Incoterms), and how disputes will be handled (contract terms).

Practical Application

A CDMO's procurement department should establish an SUS-specific procurement SOP covering:

  • Requirements confirmation and specification review process before purchase
  • A template change-notification agreement with suppliers
  • A standard checklist for incoming-goods acceptance
  • Procedures for handling nonconformance with specifications (return, acceptance by concession, etc.)
  • Authorization and a simplified process for urgent purchases

Practice Questions

  1. What consequences could result if an SUS supplier changes the film material during manufacture without notifying the end user?
  2. Why does TR66 stress that defining the roles and responsibilities of stakeholders before a quality and supply agreement is established is "extremely important"?

  • This section (Section 5b) covers the complete content of Implementation S4 (Business Case) and S5 (Development) (7.4-7.5, p88-p99)
  • This section will be merged with Section 5c, which continues with the later implementation stages, including Implementation S6-S7
  • The concepts of the PEP, user requirements and the procurement process will continue to be referenced and deepened in later chapters
PDA Technical Report No. 66 — Single-Use Systems (2014) | p100 - p107

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the complete S6 (Testing and Validation) workflow for SUS implementation: delivery, installation, qualification, training and safety
  • Grasp the key elements of S7 (Launch): supplier management, routine procurement and Technical Diligence
  • Learn how to develop and execute the HSE (Health, Safety and Environment) plan in an SUS project
  • Understand the role of quality and technical agreements and of logistics and storage strategy in sustaining SUS operations
  • Distinguish the purpose and scope of a Quality Audit from those of Technical Diligence

7.6 Implementation S6: Testing and Validation

Original Text

During this stage, methods and documentation must be developed for equipment SOPs, maintenance, qualification and process validation, HSE processes, and personnel training. Some documents (e.g., the validation master plan) may require updates at this stage to maintain compliance.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Implementation S6: This is the critical turning point where an SUS project moves from design to actual operation. In this stage, all equipment SOPs, maintenance plans, qualification and validation documents, HSE (health, safety and environment) procedures and personnel training must be put in place.

Validation Master Plan: This document governs all validation activities. In S6 it may need to be updated to reflect new SUS equipment information, so that regulatory compliance is maintained.

Analogy

S6 is like the soft-opening period before a new restaurant opens. The menu (SOPs) has been drafted and the kitchen equipment (SUS hardware) is in place; now you test whether each dish's workflow runs smoothly, whether the cooks are familiar with the operations, and whether the fire safety equipment passes inspection. Every detail must be confirmed before the grand opening (S7 Launch).

7.6.1 Delivery

Original Text

The PEP must cover the delivery process, including responsibilities of the supplier and the end user. Transport logistics are sometimes complex, and the procedure by which the equipment will be safely shipped (transported) and unloaded from its delivery vehicle must be considered. Some suppliers do not take unloading into account, and the end user may need to take particular precautions here or ensure that the supplier contractually takes responsibility. There may be specific non-GxP issues to resolve such as documentation (delivery notes), inspection for inventory or damage, and insurance or the removal and appropriate disposal of crates and other packaging materials.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

PEP (Process Engineering Plan): The PEP is the master document that runs through the entire SUS implementation and must cover the respective responsibilities of the supplier and the end user in the delivery process.

Delivery looks simple but hides many risks: transport damage, unclear responsibility for unloading, disposal of packaging waste, and so on. Although these fall under non-GxP, poor handling can delay the entire project schedule.

Key Notes

Many suppliers' base quotations do not include unloading services! The end user must explicitly agree on unloading responsibility in the contract; otherwise it may face additional costs and delays. Be sure to add a delivery acceptance checklist to the PEP: delivery note verification, visual damage inspection, inventory registration and insurance claim procedures.

Practice Questions

  1. If a supplier's quotation does not include unloading services, how would you protect the end user's interests in the contract?
  2. What logistics challenges might arise when transporting large SUS equipment? How would you plan for them in advance?

7.6.2 Installation

Original Text

The PEP will also cover all aspects of the startup process, including post-delivery storage, handling prior to installation unpacking, and the responsibilities of the supplier and the end user in a more general manner. Transferring the equipment to its place of use can be problematic if equipment dimensions or facility access has not been reviewed in advance. Preparing the area, particularly in cases where the SUS equipment will be used in manufacturing operations, may require specific training. The overall strategy and approach will depend on the nature of the SUS and the level of risk associated with this process (Section 7.4.1). In some cases, non-GxP documentation may be generated or supplied and work permits (e.g., fire safety) secured during the installation processes (e.g., removals).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Installation: Installation is more than placing the equipment in position. Every step needs to be planned, from staging after delivery and handling before unpacking to transport to the point of use. In particular, equipment dimensions and facility access routes must be assessed in advance; if this step is neglected, the equipment may turn out to be unable to enter the building.

Analogy

It is like buying a large refrigerator and having it delivered to your new home, only to find that the elevator is too small and the door frames too narrow to get it into the kitchen. In a pharmaceutical plant, the delay and cost from such a situation are far more serious than for household equipment. Measuring the equipment dimensions and the width of all access routes in advance is the most basic yet most frequently overlooked step.

Key Notes

Installation in a GMP manufacturing area may require: (1) specific personnel training; (2) work permits such as a hot work permit; (3) non-GxP documentation of the removal of old equipment. The installation strategy should be based on the results of the risk assessment (Section 7.4.1).

7.6.3 Qualification

Original Text

Qualification of an SUS should include lifecycle testing that reproduces the most impactful steps of the system's lifecycle (e.g., sterilization, transport, freezing/thawing) followed by a verification of the key functionalities and performance attributes (e.g., integrity testing). The lifecycle testing must be designed to cover the intended working space. Please refer to Sections 5 and 7.7 for a discussion of the SUS qualification process and supplier qualification, respectively.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Lifecycle Testing: This does not mean testing the SUS only under "perfect conditions". Instead, the most impactful steps of its entire use lifecycle, such as sterilization, transport shock and freeze-thaw cycles, are simulated, and critical functions (such as integrity testing) are then verified to still be acceptable.

Integrity Testing: This confirms that the sealing and barrier functions of the system remain intact after the SUS has been subjected to lifecycle stresses. It is a key step in ensuring product sterility assurance.

Key Notes

Qualification testing must cover the "intended working space". In other words, testing cannot be limited to optimal conditions; it must also cover boundary conditions and worst-case scenarios. This echoes the concept of Design Space in ICH Q8.

Practice Questions

  1. Why must SUS qualification include lifecycle testing rather than static functional testing alone?
  2. List three of the "most impactful" steps in the SUS lifecycle and describe the risks they may pose.

7.6.4 Training

Original Text

Training is a GMP requirement and an integral part of SUS technical implementation. The goal of any training program should be to provide specific knowledge and understanding that will support successful operations. Although, the specific training needs are normally described in the user requirements, other training requirements (e.g., for project management and risk assessment) may be critical for the successful implementation of the project. Training programs for SUS should include concepts and principles of operation that might be unique to SUS and unfamiliar to MUS operators.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Training: Training is a firm GMP requirement and an indispensable part of implementing SUS technology. The focus is not only on "how to operate", but also on soft skills such as project management and risk assessment.

MUS (Multi-Use System): Traditional stainless steel equipment. The operating concepts of SUS differ fundamentally from those of MUS, so operators accustomed to MUS need to "relearn" the unique principles of SUS.

Analogy

Moving from MUS to SUS is like switching from a manual-transmission car to an electric vehicle. Both are "driving", but braking feedback, acceleration characteristics and charging (rather than refueling) are completely different. If you simply tell an electric vehicle driver to "drive the way you used to", problems are certain. Likewise, MUS operators must receive SUS-specific training.

7.6.5 Training Workflow

Original Text

Guidelines for a training workflow are as follows:

  • Examine each part of the entire SUS PEP individually and identify the functional areas concerned.
  • Identify the training needs in each functional area and assign stakeholders.
  • Assemble training requirements in a training matrix (example in Section 10, Appendix 3).
  • Determine the scope of each training module. Some subjects may require more than one level of presentation and may require periodic retraining.
  • Make sure that a trainee assessment or evaluation process is included at the end of the training module; this is a GMP requirement.
  • Take the resources as well as the subject and complexity of the training requirement into account when planning training procedures.
  • Some training modules may be delivered by competent team members or operators who are familiar with SUS implementation. Other training modules may be delivered by suppliers.
  • Add completed training modules to the individual training records of each stakeholder.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Training Matrix: A tabular tool that maps the training needs of each functional area to the relevant stakeholders, ensuring that no one is missed and no topic is overlooked.

Key Notes

Core principles of the training workflow:

  • Completeness: Review each part of the PEP one by one to identify all functional areas that require training
  • Tiering: Some topics require multi-level training and periodic retraining
  • Assessment: Each training module must end with an assessment; this is a mandatory GMP requirement
  • Recordkeeping: Completed training must be recorded in the individual training file

Practical Application

In a CDMO environment, operators may handle different SUS platforms for several clients at the same time. The training matrix must therefore be designed to cover: (1) general SUS fundamentals; (2) client- or product-specific operating procedures; (3) awareness training on cross-platform differences. Training modules provided by suppliers can save internal resources, but you must ensure that their content meets the company's GMP standards.

7.6.6 An Example of SUS Training

Original Text

Factors that may contribute to the leakage of bags are:

  • Poor handling during deployment
  • Mechanical stress on films or welds
  • Accidental puncture or stressing of the SUS during handling and setup

Incidences of bag leakage may be reduced through the following interventions:

  • Changing storage bin and bag design, if appropriate
  • Optimizing the method of deployment (i.e., installation)
  • Providing operational training
  • Providing bag-handling awareness training
  • Implementing a control strategy (e.g., remedial procedures by the supplier)
  • Monitoring location and type of bag damage for trending

The in-house training program should be based on operational parameters and operator feedback. Operators should be educated and engaged about:

  • Working areas and surfaces
  • Bag installation and filling
  • Prior root causes for failure
  • Leakage history and trends
  • Root-cause analyses

Additional details on training are provided in Section 6.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Bag Leakage: One of the most common and troublesome problems with SUS. Leakage not only causes product loss but may also jeopardize sterility assurance. This section provides a complete training framework, from "cause analysis" to "preventive intervention".

Analogy

An SUS bag is like a sterile glove in an operating room: the material itself is durable, but if it is handled improperly (scratched by a fingernail, pulled too hard), it will tear. Training operators in correct "bag handling awareness" is as important as training surgeons to put on gloves correctly.

Key Notes

Among the six interventions for preventing bag leakage, "operator training" and "bag handling awareness training" are the most cost-effective. Based on industry experience, most bag damage is caused by improper handling by people rather than by material defects. It is therefore essential to establish a continuous improvement loop of "operator feedback → root cause analysis → trend monitoring".

Practice Questions

  1. If your plant has experienced five bag leakage events in the past three months, how would you design a training improvement program?
  2. Why is "awareness training" for operators more important than simple "operating step training"?

7.6.7 Safety

Original Text

Health and safety issues involving the control of both recognized and unidentified hazards in order to achieve an acceptable level of risk in the workplace are the responsibility of all stakeholders. Ensuring safety in the workplace as required by local laws can be best addressed by preparing an HSE plan.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

HSE Plan (Health, Safety and Environment Plan): A systematic document covering health, safety and environmental concerns throughout the project. The HSE plan is the shared responsibility of all stakeholders, and it must address not only "known risks" but also "unidentified risks".

Key Notes

Safety is not the responsibility of a single department; it is "the responsibility of all stakeholders". Because SUS projects involve a great deal of manual work (handling, installation, disassembly), the safety risks can be more complex and variable than with traditional MUS.

7.6.8 Preparation of a Health, Safety, and Environment Plan

Original Text

The HSE plan addresses health, safety, and environmental concerns throughout the SUS project. It should be prepared at an early stage and incorporated into the user requirements. Published examples of an HSE plan (although not related to SU technology) can be found in the literature. The HSE plan lists stakeholders and those responsible for performing specific HSE activities. These activities may range from mapping and analyzing the impact of HSE to developing and implementing process improvements. A workflow for developing an HSE plan may consist of the following steps:

  • Screen — Quick clarification of any HSE aspects related to the project
  • Define — Overall evaluation of significant HSE concerns during the project lifecycle
  • Plan — Final HSE plan with stakeholders, activities, responsibilities, and deadlines

The screening and defining activities involve the complete evaluation of the project from an HSE point of view in order to determine HSE concerns that require immediate action. Some of the HSE concerns for a typical SUS project are presented here.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The three-step HSE workflow:

  • Screen: Quickly take stock of the HSE-related aspects of the project
  • Define: Comprehensively assess the significant HSE concerns across the project lifecycle
  • Plan: Develop the final HSE plan, clearly listing stakeholders, activities, responsibilities and timelines

Analogy

An HSE plan is like a hospital's Infection Control Plan: it is not written after a problem occurs, but is built into the user requirements specification early in the project. The three-step process is like a hospital's "risk screening → detailed assessment → plan development", narrowing the focus step by step from the macro to the micro level.

Key Notes

The environmental aspects of an SUS project deserve particular attention: single use means large volumes of plastic waste, packaging material and transport carbon footprint. This contrasts sharply with traditional MUS (which uses CIP/SIP but generates less solid waste). The company's sustainable manufacturing policy must be included in the HSE assessment for SUS.

Practice Questions

  1. What are the fundamental differences between SUS and MUS in terms of environmental impact?
  2. Why should the HSE plan be developed early in the project rather than waiting until the installation stage?

7.6.9 Health and Safety Issues Related to SUS

Original Text

SUSs often consist of plastic components that have low resistance to physical stress, pressure, and temperature extremes. A typical setup includes a range of support equipment (e.g., pumps, agitators, scales) that requires manual transportation, installation, and dismantling. This generates a unique set of HSE concerns, as presented in the following lists.

Safety

  • Tubing ruptures at eye level
  • Bag ruptures at eye level
  • Spills and slippage
  • Emergency stops for moving parts
  • Automatic restarts after power cuts
  • Tubing on floor that may result in tripping
  • Loose parts (e.g., media containers, peristaltic pumps) that can fall
  • Mobile containers over feet or hands, mobile containers squeezed in doorways
  • Mobile containers' finish, corners, and handles
  • Connections failing for compressed air and other gases (e.g., CO2 and nitrogen)
  • Retention of infectious material
  • Failure in connecting to CIP/SIP equipment
  • Position of emergency stop during filling operations may be out of sight from dispensing vessel

Health

  • Ergonomics for installation and removal of SUS components (e.g., installing bags, filters)
  • Ergonomics for handling bags that contain liquid
  • Stress of communicating during filling operations that may be out of sight from dispensing vessel
  • Lifting heavy or cumbersome objects
  • Insufficient work space and surfaces

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The safety risks of SUS are quite different from those of MUS. MUS is fixed stainless steel equipment that rarely moves once installed; SUS consists of plastic components with lower pressure and temperature resistance, and requires frequent manual handling, installation and disassembly. This creates a unique set of safety hazards.

Key Notes

High-risk scenarios requiring particular attention:

  • Tubing/bag rupture at eye level: If biologics or hazardous substances are being handled, the consequences can be very serious
  • Automatic restart after power failure: Uncontrolled equipment restart can lead to serious safety incidents
  • Trip hazards from floor-level tubing: Temporary SUS tubing runs often create obstacles on the floor
  • Location of the emergency stop button: During filling operations, the emergency stop button may not be within the operator's line of sight

Analogy

Consider the difference in kitchen safety: one is a professional kitchen with fixed stainless steel stoves (MUS), the other is a mobile kitchen using temporary gas lines and portable equipment (SUS). The safety risks of the latter are more complex: lines can trip people, equipment can slide, and connections can come loose. A completely different safety mindset is required.

Practice Questions

  1. What preventive measures would you establish for the risk of "tubing rupturing at eye level"?
  2. Why are ergonomic issues more serious in SUS operations than in MUS? Please give examples.

7.7 Implementation S7: Launch

Original Text

The PEP leads to an initial procurement of equipment for the SUS project in addition to setting out a secure supply chain for continuous operation of the SUS. Initial supplier evaluations are part of the PEP preparation in S4 (Section 7.4.10). In the following paragraphs, these first steps are consolidated with a detailed evaluation of the appropriate tools for the routine management of suppliers.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Implementation S7 - Launch: S7 marks the critical moment when the SUS moves from "project mode" to "operations mode". The PEP not only guides the initial equipment procurement but must also establish a safe and reliable supply chain to support the ongoing operation of the SUS. The core task of this stage is to deepen the preliminary supplier assessment from S4 into a routine supplier management system.

Analogy

S7 is like a restaurant moving from its "soft opening" to "regular business". During the soft opening you can test with small quantities of ingredients, but once in regular business you must build a stable ingredient supply chain: fixed suppliers, quality agreements, backup suppliers, inventory management strategies and so on. Without a stable supply chain, even the best cooking cannot keep dishes coming out.

7.7.1 Management of Materials

Original Text

Since continuous sourcing of supplies is critical to keeping the SUS in a productive state, it is important to maintain an efficient routine supplier management (e.g., technical diligence, quality audits, quality and supply agreements, and purchasing, logistical, and waste-handling strategies) to create a robust support for SUS manufacturing operations.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Routine Supplier Management: This covers multiple aspects, including technical diligence, quality audits, quality and supply agreements, procurement strategy, logistics strategy and waste disposal strategy. The fundamental difference between SUS and MUS is that SUS follows a "consumable model": any interruption of continuous supply directly means a production stoppage.

Key Notes

For a CDMO, the importance of materials management is self-evident. Because SUS components cannot be reused, any supply interruption directly affects the client's product delivery timeline. Establishing a "robust supplier management system" is the cornerstone of successful SUS operations.

7.7.2 Routine or Operational Procurement

Original Text

Routine procurement for SUS (e.g., single-use bioreactors or chromatography systems) requires a supplier qualification program for each of the SUS suppliers included in the PEP. Whenever the risk and complexity of the component in question is considered significant, the qualification program should be implemented using a risk-management approach. Some of the factors to consider are as follows:

  • The end user may have to adopt a different approach to the procurement of integrated SUS equipment versus routine SUS components (Sections 5.2, 5.4, 5.5, 6.12.1, 6.14, 7.5.7, and 7.6.2).
  • A backup policy of sourcing the same component from at least two different vendors should be adopted, and the suitability for the backup to be interchangeable with the original must be defined.
  • The end user must evaluate and audit the supplier's manufacturing capabilities and supply chain to determine the supplier's ability to increase output on demand.
  • The relationship between the end user and the supplier should be as transparent as possible in order to enhance the unimpeded operation of the SUS.
  • The quality systems of the supplier should be examined as part of a quality audit process, and quality and supply agreements should be implemented (Section 7.7.4).
  • The supplier should be provided with a detailed description of the manufacturing process of the SUS, film quality, biocompatibility, and testing (e.g., bioburden monitoring of the SUS), particulates, extractables and leachables, and testing of leakages.
  • A contract (supply or manufacturing agreement) describing the commercial conditions that relate to the supply chain, guarantee of delivery, and transport quality should be implemented.
  • Both the supplier and the end user must institute robust change-control processes in order to prevent ad hoc modifications to the SUS.
