  • Time lines from the design to arrival of both MUS and SUS parts should be monitored.
  • Evaluation models (preferably at the end user's site) that use mock-ups of preliminary SUS designs (available for training and simulations) should be tested. Testing is an important risk-mitigating procedure. This process may have been started as part of the SUS strategy (Section 7.2.3).
  • The end user should adhere to green and sustainable manufacturing policies throughout the entire supply chain and evaluate waste disposal for carbon footprint reduction.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Supplier Qualification Program: A systematic assessment program implemented for each SUS supplier in the PEP. When components are of higher risk and complexity, a risk management approach should be used to implement it.

Dual Sourcing: A backup strategy of purchasing the same component from at least two different suppliers, with a definition of whether the backup supplier's product is interchangeable with the original.

Change Control: Both the supplier and the end user must establish a rigorous change control process to prevent ad hoc modifications to the SUS.

Key Notes

Of the eleven considerations for routine procurement, the three most critical are:

  • Dual sourcing strategy: It avoids single-supplier risk, but defining and verifying "interchangeability" is a major technical challenge
  • Two-way change control: Not only must the user not make arbitrary changes; any process change by the supplier must also be notified to the user and assessed
  • Sustainable manufacturing: The environmental impact of SUS (large volumes of plastic waste) is receiving increasing regulatory attention

Practical Application

As a CDMO, your client may specify the use of SUS components from a particular supplier. But what if that supplier runs into insufficient capacity or quality problems? We recommend proactively negotiating a backup supplier plan during the client's technology transfer, and clearly defining the procedure and responsibilities for "supplier change" in the quality agreement. Mock-up evaluation (simulation testing using a mock-up) is an effective means of reducing risk.

Practice Questions

  1. Why is supplier management for SUS more complex than for MUS? Analyze this from the nature of "consumables".
  2. If a supplier unilaterally changes the film formulation, what impact might this have on your process? How would you guard against it through change control?
  3. What special challenges does "green sustainable manufacturing" face in the SUS supply chain?

7.7.3 Technical Diligence

Original Text

A QbD approach is the best way to qualify suppliers for SUSs. It is based on ICH principles (ICH Q8) and it embraces the complementary concepts of quality-risk management and pharmaceutical quality-system management (as detailed in ICH Q9 and Q10), process validation and verification, and stakeholder management (Sections 7.2.1, 7.2.2 and 7.2.3). These approaches lay the foundation for quality-driven processes built on a science- and risk-based approach and understanding. This is the case not just for the process for which the SUS will be used but also for the SU technology that will be deployed, thus supporting patient safety and product availability. The QbD approach allows for the identification and management of critical sources of variability in which control strategies can be used to maintain a state of control and facilitate the application of continuous improvement throughout the SUS product lifecycle.

Technical diligence can be started at S4 (Section 7.4). Based on established principles, it involves a carefully documented visit to the supplier's site. The outcome should be an appropriate and measured understanding of all the technical issues pertaining to the SUS manufacturing process, readiness for installation, and operation at the end user's facility. Technical diligence should be considered an important step in the supplier qualification process and a complement to the quality audit process. The principal differences between technical diligence and a quality audit are summarized in Table 7.7.3-1.

Table 7.7.3-1🔍: Principal Differences Between a Quality Audit and a Technical Diligence Assessment

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Technical Diligence: Unlike a quality audit, which focuses only on "compliance", the purpose of technical diligence is to gain an in-depth understanding of the supplier's design, manufacturing, suitability and control strategy. It is a supplier assessment method based on QbD (Quality by Design) principles.

QbD Approach (Quality by Design): Based on ICH Q8/Q9/Q10 principles, it uses scientific and risk-based methods to identify and manage critical sources of variability and to establish a control strategy that maintains a state of control.

Analogy

A quality audit is like checking whether a restaurant has a business license, a hygiene permit and a food safety system (compliance); technical diligence is like sending a head chef to visit the ingredient supplier's farm in person to understand the soil quality, irrigation methods, fertilization strategy and harvesting process, and to judge whether the ingredients truly suit his or her style of cooking. The two are complementary, and neither can be omitted.

Table Analysis

Table 7.7.3-1🔍 clearly presents three major differences between a quality audit and technical diligence:

  • Different purpose: An audit focuses on "compliance"; technical diligence focuses on "technical understanding"
  • Different participants: An audit is led by the quality team; technical diligence requires the deep involvement of scientists, engineers and SMEs
  • Different scope: An audit has a clear and focused scope; technical diligence is open-ended and may need to trace back to sub-suppliers

Table 7.7.3-2🔍: Some Pertinent Factors for a Technical Diligence Assessment Checklist

Determine the supplier's degree of integration:

  • Vertical: The supplier manufactures practically everything on-site, from the film to the finished and packaged SUS
  • Horizontal: These activities are spread over more than one site
  • Mixed model: A combination of vertical and horizontal integration

The mixed model has become increasingly common. Resin is nearly always sourced as a bulk from larger industrial companies, and the SUS supplier company may be specialized in the production of SUS for pharmaceutical operations and have facilities on more than one continent. In some cases, it may require more than one technical diligence visit to the supplier's sites to address all the relevant technical concerns (e.g., the film's blow-extrusion may be performed at a different site than that used for SUS assembly and testing). The end user should pay careful attention to supply chain issues in this context. Depending on sourcing from another continent or from multiple continents could easily be disrupted by many factors beyond the end user's control and should be assessed from a risk-based approach.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Supplier Integration Models:

  • Vertical: Everything from film to finished product is manufactured at a single site; this gives the simplest supply chain but limited supplier choice
  • Horizontal: Activities are spread across multiple sites; this requires multiple technical diligence visits
  • Mixed: The most common form; resin is usually purchased in bulk from large industrial companies, while SUS assembly and testing may take place at different locations

Key Notes

Table 7.7.3-2🔍 provides a checklist framework for technical diligence covering three main areas: supplier information, technical capability, and suitability for the end user. Points of particular note:

  • CDA (Confidentiality Disclosure Agreement): Technical diligence involves the exchange of in-depth technical information, so a confidentiality agreement is a prerequisite
  • Contingency Planning: It is essential to know whether the supplier has a backup plan
  • Cross-continent supply chain risk: Dependence on supply from different continents can easily be broken by geopolitics, natural disasters, logistics disruptions and other factors

Practice Questions

  1. If your SUS supplier uses a mixed integration model, with film blowing in Europe, assembly in Asia and testing in North America, how would you plan the itinerary and focus of the technical diligence?
  2. Why must a CDA be signed before technical diligence begins? What are the risks of not signing one?

Table 7.7.3-3🔍: An Interpretation of the SUS Implementation Process

A quality audit may be executed either following or in parallel with the technical diligence process (e.g., an audit questionnaire can be part of the technical diligence process for phase I IMPs). A practical example of the technical diligence process can be found in Brown's presentation on Implementation and Technical Diligence.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Implementation matrix for the lifecycle approach: Table 7.7.3-3🔍 is one of the most important tables in this section. It aligns the product's clinical development phase, the SUS implementation stage, the FDA process validation (PV) stage, supplier qualification activities and quality agreement requirements.

Table Analysis

This table reveals an important trend:

  • Risk decreases over time: It falls gradually from "high risk" in the preclinical phase to "low risk" for marketed products
  • Depth of supplier management increases: From "collecting information" → "technical diligence" → "technical diligence + audit" → "ongoing verification"
  • Quality agreements move from optional to mandatory: A quality agreement is optional in the preclinical phase but becomes necessary once clinical trials begin
  • Phase-appropriate approach: Supplier management requirements differ by development phase, reflecting the ICH principle of phase-appropriateness

Analogy

It is like the growth of a student into a professional: during the internship (preclinical) the company only needs basic information; during the probation period (Phase I) the person must demonstrate real ability; for formal employment (Phase II/III) they must pass a performance review and background check; and a long-term employee (marketed product) enters a mode of continuous supervision and annual appraisal. The depth and formality of management increase at each stage.

7.7.4 Quality and Technical Agreements

Original Text

Quality and technical agreements clarify the functional aspects of a business relationship by delineating the responsibilities of quality, regulatory, and production personnel within each company in order to enhance communication and prevent ambiguity as referred to in Section 6. A quality agreement should not be confused with a business, supply, or manufacturing agreement. Several templates for APIs are available for chemical and biological products, and the end user may adapt them to an SUS from the Bulk Pharmaceuticals Task Force (Society of Chemical Manufacturers and Affiliates or SOCMA) or the International Pharmaceutical Excipients Council (IPEC).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Quality Agreement: A document that clearly defines the quality, regulatory and manufacturing responsibilities of both parties. Note: a quality agreement is not a commercial agreement or a supply agreement; it focuses on the allocation of quality-related responsibilities.

SOCMA / IPEC templates: Existing quality agreement templates for APIs (active pharmaceutical ingredients) can be adapted for SUS use, but must be adjusted for the special nature of SUS (such as extractables/leachables, film quality and biocompatibility).

Key Notes

The core function of a quality agreement is to "prevent ambiguity". In the SUS supply chain, the line of responsibility between supplier and user is often blurrier than in API supply. For example: who is responsible for film extractables testing? Who provides the biocompatibility data? What is the time limit for change notification? These questions must all be answered clearly in the quality agreement.

Practice Questions

  1. Why should a quality agreement not be conflated with a commercial/supply agreement? How do their core purposes differ?
  2. When adapting an API quality agreement template into an SUS quality agreement, what specific clauses do you think need to be added?

7.7.5 Logistics and Storage

Original Text

The storage and warehouse facilities required for the efficient operation of the SUS should be defined in the PEP. In general, the amount of pallet space required may be calculated as a function of consumption and the dimensions of the shipping container. A risk-based approach may be used to allocate storage space for critical consumables with limited or no alternative suppliers, while standard consumables can be sourced as and when they are needed. Value-stream mapping (a lean manufacturing technique used to analyze the flow of materials and information required to bring a product or service to the user) can be used to identify improvements in lead time or to identify inefficient operations. For example, a value-stream map can show where excess materials are accumulating. Some of the factors to consider are:

  • High-risk consumables
  • Standard consumables
  • Storage policy
  • Pallet space
  • Frequency of delivery to consumers
  • Environmental storage conditions monitoring and control (a GMP requirement)

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Value-Stream Mapping (VSM): One of the core tools of lean manufacturing, used to analyze the flow of materials and information from raw materials to the end user. In SUS warehouse management, VSM can help identify opportunities to improve lead times and inefficient steps.

Risk-Based Storage: Critical consumables (especially those without an alternative supplier) should be allocated more safety stock space; general consumables can be purchased on demand.

Analogy

An SUS storage strategy is like a supermarket's inventory management: best-selling, exclusively distributed products (high-risk consumables) need more shelf space and safety stock, while everyday goods that are easy to obtain from multiple suppliers (standard consumables) are replenished just in time. A value stream map is the supermarket's "traffic flow analysis", finding where queues form (bottlenecks) and where stock piles up (waste).

Formula and Calculation

Pallet space requirement = f (consumption rate, container size)

Safety stock strategy:
  High-risk consumables → higher safety stock (2-4 weeks)
  Standard consumables  → purchase on demand (JIT)

GMP requirements:
  Environmental storage conditions → must be monitored and controlled
  (temperature, humidity, etc.)

Key Notes

SUS poses a unique storage challenge compared with MUS: SUS items are consumables, and every production batch requires brand-new components, so the storage space required is much greater than for MUS. Monitoring and controlling environmental storage conditions is a mandatory GMP requirement; some SUS components (such as film bags) are sensitive to temperature and humidity, and improper storage may shorten shelf life or degrade the material.

Practice Questions

  1. If your SUS usage suddenly increases by 50% because of a new client order, what adjustments would your storage strategy need?
  2. How can a value stream map help you discover "hidden waste" in the SUS supply chain? Please give a specific example.
  3. Why is SUS storage management more complex than MUS and more space-consuming?

Section Summary

  • The S6 Testing and Validation stage covers five major areas: delivery, installation, qualification, training and safety. Each area must be planned in detail in the PEP, with the responsibilities of the supplier and the end user clearly divided.
  • SUS qualification must include Lifecycle Testing, simulating the most impactful steps such as sterilization, transport and freeze-thaw, to ensure functional performance within the intended working space.
  • Training is a firm GMP requirement. SUS operators need dedicated training because the operating principles of SUS differ fundamentally from those of MUS. Bag handling awareness training is the most effective means of reducing leakage.
  • The HSE plan should be developed early in the project. SUS creates unique safety risks because of the large amount of manual work (rupture at eye level, floor-level tubing trip hazards, ergonomic challenges and so on), and its single-use nature also brings a significant environmental impact.
  • The core of S7 Launch is to establish a supplier management system that moves from project procurement to routine operational procurement, including dual sourcing, change control and quality agreements.
  • Technical diligence and quality audits are complementary: the former focuses on technical understanding (led by scientists and engineers), and the latter on compliance (led by the quality team). The supplier's integration model (vertical/horizontal/mixed) determines the complexity of the investigation.
  • The quality agreement is the key document for preventing ambiguity of responsibility and must be managed separately from the commercial agreement. SOCMA and IPEC provide templates that can be adapted.
  • Storage strategy should take a risk-based approach: safety stock for high-risk consumables, on-demand purchasing for standard items. Value-stream mapping (VSM) is a lean tool for optimizing logistics efficiency. Environmental monitoring is a mandatory GMP requirement.
A1

Section A1: Appendix: URS Example

p108-p120

PDA Technical Report No. 66 (2014) Single-Use Systems | p108 - p120

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the role and structure of the User Requirement Specification (URS) in an SUS facility project
  • Grasp the main areas covered by the URS example: facility description, process, material flow, personnel flow, regulatory requirements, utilities, cleanrooms and control systems
  • Learn how to write user requirements for an SUS manufacturing facility for gene therapy viral vectors
  • Recognize how BSL2 biosafety requirements affect facility design and HVAC system planning
  • Understand how the URS lays the foundation for Design Qualification (DQ) and subsequent qualification/validation activities

8.0 / 8.1 Description of Document

Original Text

8.0 Appendix I: Overall User Requirement Specification Example

8.1 Description of Document

This appendix contains an example of user requirements (UR) written for an SUS facility. This material is referenced in Section 7.5.1, User Requirements, and should be used as an example for how this type of document is structured. The complexity and size of the document will change according to the type of SUS and complexity of the project. These types of documents should be considered as living (i.e., they can be updated as appropriate should the project or UR change). The document can be prepared by the end user or a sponsor of the end user such as an engineering company or supplier.

This document describes the UR concerning the new production suite, GMP, SUS (shown in figure below). This is an overall UR specification and detailed UR will be written. The requirements stated in this document may not be qualified but will form the basis for design.

8.1.1 Abbreviations

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

User Requirement Specification, URS: The URS is one of the most important foundation documents in a pharmaceutical engineering project. It is written by the user (the pharmaceutical company) and defines clearly "what I need", not "how to achieve it". The URS is the starting point for all subsequent design (concept design, basic design, detailed design) and is the basis for Design Qualification (DQ).

Living Document: A URS is not a document that is finalized once and never changed. As the project progresses, regulations change or process needs are adjusted, the URS can and should be updated, but only through a formal Change Control procedure.

Analogy

A URS is like the "requirements list" when building a house: you tell the architect "I need three bedrooms, two bathrooms and an open kitchen", but you do not tell them which brand of cement or what size of rebar to use. The URS describes the "What" (what is needed), not the "How" (how it is achieved). The architect produces drawings based on your requirements, just as the engineering company produces a Design Specification based on the URS.

Key Notes

This appendix provides an overall URS example. In actual projects, more detailed URSs are also needed for individual systems (such as the HVAC system, WFI system, automation system, etc.). The overall URS defines the big framework, while the detailed URSs go deep into each subsystem.

Note: the requirements in this URS example "may not be qualified". This means that not every requirement necessarily needs formal qualification, but they form the basis of the design. Which requirements need qualification is decided in the subsequent risk assessment.

Practice Questions

  1. Why is the URS called a "living document"? In what situations does the URS need to be updated?
  2. A URS can be written by "the end user's sponsor (such as an engineering company or supplier)". What does this mean in a CDMO context?
  3. What is the difference between an "Overall URS" and a "Detailed URS"? Please give an example.

8.2 Introduction

8.2.1 Description of Facility

Original Text

Company X needs to equip a new facility GMP, SUS for the manufacture of injectable products for viral particle gene therapy. This document outlines the user requirements of the facility. In this project and UR document, abbreviation T4 stands for the main building site and GMP, SUS for the cleanroom area. The new production suite should be designed for biosafety level 2 (BSL2) and comply with EU GMP and FDA cGMP.

The products of this facility shall be based on modified live viral particle technologies (gene therapy). The manufacturing process shall cover production of biological bulk and final filled products for sterile, as parenteral applications. The initial adenovirus production portfolio covers in the first instance two bulk and two final products, which are known as Product 1 and Product 2.

Product 1 is a novel gene-based product that is given as a suicide therapy for tumors. Product 2 is a novel product being developed to deliver a growth factor for use in surgery.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

BSL2 (Biosafety Level 2): BSL2 applies to work with microorganisms that pose a moderate potential hazard to personnel. This case uses a live viral vector (adenovirus), so BSL2-level biosafety containment is required. This directly affects the facility HVAC design (negative pressure or directional airflow is needed), waste handling (inactivation is needed) and personnel protection.

Gene Therapy: Uses viral vectors to deliver therapeutic genes into a patient's cells. Adenovirus is used here as the vector, which is a common gene therapy technology platform.

Dual regulatory compliance: The facility must comply with both EU GMP and FDA cGMP, indicating that the product will be marketed in the EU and the US. Dual compliance increases design complexity, because the two sets of regulations differ slightly in cleanroom classification, environmental monitoring and other areas.

Analogy

A BSL2 facility is like a "laboratory with access control": compared with an ordinary office, it has strict entry and exit control, a special ventilation system (ensuring air flows only inward and does not leak outward) and a dedicated waste-handling route. Imagine a hospital ward handling infectious material: the air is under negative pressure control, waste must be sterilized before disposal, and personnel must change clothing when entering and leaving. An SUS facility adds the GMP cleanliness requirements on top of this.

Key Notes

This URS example is particularly valuable because it shows the application of SUS technology in the manufacture of gene therapy products, an advanced therapy medicinal product (ATMP) category. Because gene therapy batch sizes are usually small and product types change quickly, the flexibility and cleaning-free nature of SUS are especially well suited to such applications.

Pay attention to the language used in the URS: "shall" indicates a mandatory requirement; "should" indicates a recommended requirement. In URS writing, the choice of wording directly affects the scope of subsequent qualification/validation.

8.2.2 Environment / 8.2.3 Capacity

Original Text

8.2.2 Environment

As a design basis for the HVAC in Company X, an upper limit of +30°C at 50% RH (63 kJ/kg) in summertime and lower limit of −37°C in wintertime for the outside climate will be used.

8.2.3 Capacity

The target capacities to fulfill the expected need of the market for the facility are summarized in the following list. Total number of vials will be about 130,000.

The process capabilities are limited by the drug substance manufacture. The drug substance manufacture will need 24-hour, 7-day-week control. However, the filling operation will be run on an 8-hour, 5-day-week basis.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

HVAC Design Basis: The HVAC system design must consider the most extreme local climate conditions. This case sets a summer maximum of +30°C/50% RH and a winter minimum of −37°C, suggesting that the facility may be located in Northern Europe (such as Finland). These figures determine the cooling and heating capacity design of the HVAC system.

Capacity planning: An annual output of about 130,000 vials is medium scale in the gene therapy field. Drug Substance manufacturing requires 24/7 monitoring (because cell culture cannot be interrupted), but filling needs only 8 hours/day, 5 days/week, reflecting the different operating rhythms of the two process stages.

Key Notes

It is essential to define the environmental design basis clearly in the URS. If the HVAC design basis is set incorrectly (for example, the summer maximum temperature is underestimated), the cleanroom may be unable to maintain the required temperature and humidity conditions in extreme weather, affecting product quality and GMP compliance.

8.2.4 Process

Original Text

8.2.4.1 Production Process

The planned process for large-scale manufacturing of Product 1 and Product 2 vectors for the commercial scale process is shown below.

Thawing and expansion of master cell banks (API clean process)

  • T-flask, roller bottles, and rocker bags

Infection of expanded cells with working virus seed stock (WVSS) (API clean process)

  • Infection of the cell suspension with WVSS
  • Full CPE monitored with analytical and process tools

Cell lysis and clarification of crude viral lysate (cCVL) (API clean process)

  • Benzonase® and surfactant treatments to lyse the cells
  • Depth filtration to remove cell debris

Buffer conditioning by crossflow ultrafiltration (API clean process)

  • Diafiltration to reduce the amount of host cell– and growth medium–originated impurities
  • Buffer conditioning suitable for chromatography

Chromatography (API clean process)

  • Capture and polishing of recombinant adenoviruses

Crossflow ultrafiltration (API clean process)

  • Concentration
  • Diafiltration to remove the chromatography elution components (salts)
  • Change to final formulation buffer standardization of concentration
  • Freezing and holding in −70°C

Fill and finish (aseptic process)

  • Thawing final sterilization filtration prior to filling
  • Washing and sterilization of vials
  • Filling, stoppering, and crimping

Inspection, packaging, and storage

  • Checking of container seals and visual inspection
  • Labeling of primary containers and packaging
  • Freezing and storage of the drug product

8.2.4.2 Assumptions for planning

  • Disposable equipment is used during processing of the product.
  • The production suite should be equipped with its own WFI facility and solution manufacture.
  • Glass vials for sterile filling are brought into the facility ready to be washed and depyrogenated. Stoppers and aluminum crimps for filling are bought from suppliers presterilized.

8.2.4.3 Storage in GMP, SUS

There shall be storage areas for incoming raw materials and packaging material within T4. The GMP, SUS area shall provide storage of a batch material between process steps in the production.

8.2.4.4 Inactivation

All floor and process drains, such as product-containing waste waters, elution buffers, and so on, from the manufacturing areas shall be led to the collection tanks in the inactivation system. The connection to the collection tank shall be a closed system. Inactivation shall be carried out chemically or thermally in the balance tanks. After inactivation and neutralization, the drain liquid is transferred to the common drainage for transportation to the municipal wastewater treatment plant.

Floor and process drains from other areas shall be led directly to the common drainage or, when appropriate, to existing T4 balancing tank.

8.2.4.5 Waste Handling

Used equipment from the aseptic area and reusable equipment from the API areas are placed and sealed into plastic bags and decontaminated in a decontamination area (BSL2). The risk assessment will show the need for decontamination in the autoclave.

8.2.4.6 CIP

No CIP station is necessary due to the use of disposables. Elution solutions are used in order to clean columns and filter systems. A washing machine is used for washing filling-machine parts and filter holders.

8.2.4.7 Washing and Sterilization

Sterilization of reusable equipment shall be performed in an autoclave. Stoppers and aluminum crimps are bought from suppliers presterilized. Vials are to be washed and depyrogenized locally on entering the filling area.

8.2.4.8 SIP

No sterilization-in-place (SIP) is necessary due to the use of disposables and the supply of media, buffer, and elution solutions.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Process flow overview: This is a typical adenovirus vector production process, comprising the following key stages:

  • Upstream: cell thaw → cell expansion → viral infection → cell lysis
  • Downstream: depth filtration → ultrafiltration/diafiltration → chromatography purification → concentration/buffer exchange
  • Fill & Finish: sterile filtration → filling → stoppering → capping

Note that all upstream and downstream steps are labeled "API clean process", while filling is labeled "aseptic process", reflecting the different sterility requirement levels of the different stages.

Analogy

The role of SUS in this process is like "disposable tableware" at a catered event: every use involves brand-new single-use equipment (rocker bags, tubing, filters, chromatography columns, etc.) that is discarded after use, with no need for cleaning and sterilization. This not only saves the cost of building CIP/SIP systems but, more importantly, eliminates the risk of cross-contamination, just as disposable tableware never carries residue from the previous guest.

Key Notes

How SUS simplifies facility design:

  • No CIP station needed - greatly reduces utility demand and equipment footprint
  • No SIP system needed - no need for the complex piping layout of a clean steam system
  • But an autoclave is still needed - for sterilizing reusable equipment and inactivating BSL2 waste

This is the core value proposition of SUS: simplify the facility, speed up construction and reduce capital expenditure, but not eliminate all traditional equipment entirely.

Biosafety Inactivation

Because live viral vectors (BSL2) are used, all effluent and discharges that have contacted process materials must undergo chemical or thermal inactivation before being discharged to the public sewer. This is a hard requirement of biosafety regulations, and the specification of the inactivation system must be clearly defined in the URS.

Practice Questions

  1. Why is an autoclave still needed even when fully disposable equipment is used?
  2. If this facility were downgraded from BSL2 to BSL1, which requirements in the URS could be simplified or omitted?
  3. What is the difference between an "API clean process" and an "aseptic process" in terms of cleanroom grade requirements?

8.2.5 Material Flows

Original Text

The risk of cross-contamination shall be minimized in the layout of the plant area and in the manufacturing areas. Special attention shall be put on straight flows so that possibility for mix-ups between products from different process phases or different products is minimized. Line clearance shall be performed between production runs of different products. The facility shall be designed for sequential batches of cells to be expanded in cell culture at the same time. Attention shall also be put on keeping the BSL2 requirements in the layout.

8.2.5.1 Traffic to and from T4

The incoming raw materials shall be transported to the goods-receiving area of T4. In the same way, the final products are removed for transportation from the same T4 building. Rejected material is transported to incineration.

The following shall be delivered to or brought out of T4:

Incoming:

  • Raw material for the process
  • Utensils, disposables, and components for production
  • Clothing for cleanrooms
  • Packaging materials
  • Material for cleaning of cleanrooms

Outgoing:

  • Final products in cardboard boxes
  • Clothing from cleanrooms
  • Production waste
  • Additional waste

Material flows for the production shall be separated from the other material flows.

8.2.5.2 Storage areas in GMP, SUS and T4

The main function of the storage areas in T4 is to receive and store the raw material needed in production and daily work and, when approved, to distribute it for use. Released final products shall be stored and dispatched to the customer in pallets or smaller packages. The storage areas also work as storage for hazardous and medical material and collection points for waste. The storage areas shall be designed for storage of at least two months' needs in production and consumption. The storage for finished products shall be dimensioned for six months' production.

There shall be storage areas for the following materials and products:

  • Incoming raw material, packaging material, and pallets (existing)
  • Raw material in cold storages (MCB in liquid nitrogen and MVSS in freezer)
  • Intermediates during process (cold storages)
  • Quarantined material and packaging material (existing)
  • Approved bulk material and packaging material (existing)
  • Rejected raw material and packaging material (existing)
  • Finished products (freezers)
  • Quarantined finished products (existing freezers)
  • Approved finished products (existing freezers)
  • Rejected finished products
  • Reference samples (existing)
  • Spare parts (existing)
  • Samples for stability studies (existing)
  • Lining materials and used boxes (existing)

8.2.5.3 Material Airlocks

Transport of material or products between unclassified and classified rooms and rooms with different classifications shall be done through extracted airlocks or pass-throughs. The airlocks shall be of the one-way type because personnel and the material flow shall be unidirectional. The material or product shall be unpacked and sprayed with ethanol inside the airlock when transporting to a room with class C classification. Filling components passed into the class B area shall be washed and depyrogenated, or packages of presterilized components shall be subject to a vaporized hydrogen peroxide (VHP) cycle.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Unidirectional Flow: Materials and personnel may move in one direction only, avoiding crossover between clean materials and waste materials. This is one of the basic principles of GMP facility design and is key to preventing cross-contamination.

Line Clearance: The cleaning and inspection procedure carried out between different product batches to ensure that materials from the previous batch do not remain on the line. In an SUS facility, because disposable equipment is used, the scope and complexity of line clearance are greatly reduced.

Material Airlock: A transition space connecting areas of different cleanliness grades, used for material transfer. A pressure differential is maintained in the airlock to prevent air from the lower-grade area from entering the higher-grade area.

Analogy

Material flow management is like an airport baggage handling system: outbound and inbound luggage use different routes and never cross. An "airlock" is like the airport security checkpoint: everything entering the controlled area must be inspected and processed (unpacking, alcohol spraying or VHP sterilization) before entering a cleaner area.

Key Notes

Storage requirement design: The URS requires raw material storage of at least two months of production demand and finished product storage of six months of output. The finished product storage requirement is larger because gene therapy products must be stored frozen at −70°C and release testing takes longer. These figures directly determine the size of the freezer and liquid nitrogen storage facilities.

Special impact of SUS on storage: Using disposable equipment means that a large amount of storage space is needed for single-use consumables (bags, tubing, filters, etc.), which a traditional stainless steel facility does not need to consider.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does the URS require the material airlock to be a "one-way type"? What risks would arise if two-way passage were allowed?
  2. Materials entering the grade C area need "alcohol spraying", while filling components entering the grade B area need "washing and depyrogenation" or a "VHP cycle". Why are the requirements different?

8.2.6 Personnel Flows / 8.2.7 Training

Original Text

8.2.6 Personnel Flows

The risk of cross-contamination shall also be minimized in the personnel flows. The main office building area has existing entrance, lunchroom, offices, and other social rooms.

There shall be emergency exits from the T4 and GMP, SUS buildings. If possible, the use of emergency exits should not compromise contained operation.

Personnel lockers and changing rooms for factory suit shall also be situated in T4. There shall be authorization control at the entrance to the manufacturing area, allowing only authorized personnel to enter. The working areas should be visible by inspectors and QA personnel for observation of the activities.

8.2.6.1 Gowning

Personnel working in the production areas and in laboratories shall wear suitable protective clothes for classified rooms and working in a BSL2 containment area. Personnel working in office areas, such as the central facility control room, may wear common clothes.

8.2.6.2 Personnel Airlocks

The first main objective with airlocks is to maintain a positive pressure in the classified area and, by that, ensure a proper separation of the individual rooms or areas when only cleanliness grades are to be considered. The second main objective with airlocks is to prevent contaminants from BSL2 active rooms from spreading to nonactive cleaner areas. The personnel entering or leaving the cleanrooms shall go through airlocks where gowning is performed to suit the classified area gowns. The airlocks can be of the one-way type. There should be unidirectional personnel flow in the active areas.

8.2.6.3 Estimated Number of Personnel

About 8–11: cell culture rooms, 2–3 persons; downstream, 3–4 persons; filling, 3–4.

8.2.7 Training

In order to assure that the personnel have updated information of their working area, all personnel shall be trained in GMP procedures and in using SOPs and instructions concerning them.

Sufficient training for the personnel shall be provided by the supplier of every process equipment and utility and automation system. The education level shall be maintained with additional training during the whole lifecycle of the plant.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The dual purpose of the personnel airlock:

  • GMP purpose: maintain positive pressure differential in the clean area and prevent particles and microorganisms in outside air from entering
  • BSL2 purpose: prevent biological contaminants from Active Areas (areas where live virus is handled) from spreading to non-active areas

These two purposes sometimes conflict: GMP requires positive pressure in the clean area (air flows from the clean area to the dirty area), while BSL2 requires negative pressure in the active area (air flows from outside inward, preventing the release of biohazards). The facility design must balance these two requirements skillfully.

Analogy

A personnel airlock is like the "airlock chamber of a space station": before astronauts enter the station (clean area) from outer space (uncontrolled area), they must change clothing and pressurize in the airlock. Similarly, before entering the cleanroom from the office area, operators must put on cleanroom garments layer by layer in the gowning area. The difference is that the opposite BSL2 requirement must also be considered here: in some areas the air must flow "inward" rather than "outward".

Key Notes

Balancing emergency exits and biosafety: The URS specifically mentions "emergency exits should not compromise contained operation", meaning that biosafety containment should be maintained as far as possible even during emergency evacuation. This is a challenge in practical design and requires special airlock designs or interlock control systems.

Manpower planning: Only 8-11 people are needed to run the whole facility, reflecting the manpower efficiency advantage of SUS facilities: a large workforce is not needed for equipment cleaning, assembly, sterilization validation and similar work.

Practice Questions

  1. How can the GMP positive pressure requirement and the BSL2 negative pressure requirement coexist in the same facility? Consider possible design solutions.
  2. Why does the URS require that "work areas should allow inspectors and QA personnel to observe"? How does this affect facility design?

8.3 Regulatory Requirements

Original Text

The production facilities shall be designed to meet applicable requirements in the US CFR, EU GMP, PIC/S, and applicable EU directives and Finnish legislation. All components and systems shall be designed and commissioned according to GEP, GAMP, and ASME standards and shall have a CE mark and a declaration of conformity.

The substances used and produced at the site shall meet the effective versions of monographs in the Ph. Eur., USP, or registered specifications. The compressed air and gases shall meet the demands in ISO 8573-1.

The cleanrooms shall be designed, constructed, and commissioned according to ISO 14644 and 14698, to meet cGMPs above.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Multiple regulatory compliance framework:

  • US CFR: US federal regulations, in particular 21 CFR Part 211 (cGMP for drug products) and Part 600 (biological products)
  • EU GMP: EU GMP Guidelines, including Annex 1 (manufacture of sterile medicinal products)
  • PIC/S: Pharmaceutical Inspection Co-operation Scheme, providing globally consistent GMP inspection standards
  • Finnish Legislation: confirms that the facility is located in Finland and must comply with local regulations

Key standards:

  • ISO 14644: standard for classification and testing of cleanrooms and associated controlled environments
  • ISO 14698: standard for biocontamination control in cleanrooms
  • ISO 8573-1: compressed air quality standard
  • GAMP: Good Automated Manufacturing Practice, used for computerized system validation

Key Notes

It is very important to list all applicable regulations and standards in the URS: they form the regulatory boundary conditions of the design. If an applicable regulation is omitted, the facility may be unable to pass regulatory inspection after it is built.

The CE mark requirement confirms that equipment must comply with EU safety directives, which is a legal requirement for using equipment within the EU.

Practice Questions

  1. If this facility's product were also to be sold in the Japanese market, what would need to be added to the regulatory requirements section of the URS?
  2. What are the differences between ISO 14644 and EU GMP in cleanroom classification? Why does the URS cite both?

8.4 User Requirements

8.4.1 Functional Area Requirements

Original Text

The following areas shall be available in the manufacturing suite:

  1. Material receipt and product dispatch
  2. Storage for incoming materials (room temp and cold)
  3. Quarantine area
  4. QC laboratory control area
  5. Released and rejected material storages
  6. Weighing room (optional)
  7. Master cell bank storage
  8. Master virus seed stock storage
  9. Airlocks for personnel and materials going between areas of different classification
  10. Personnel locker room/changing room
  11. Column packing room
  12. Cell culture room, clean
  13. Cell culture room, infected (upstream)/harvest
  14. Purification room (downstream)
  15. Filter integrity testing
  16. Filling
  17. Visual inspection, labeling, and packaging
  18. Washing area
  19. Decontamination
  20. Clean area
  21. Intermediate storage
  22. Media/buffer preparation area
  23. Storage for the final drug product (−70°C)
  24. Area for production utilities
  25. Area for waste inactivation
  26. Technical space for building HVAC and cleanroom HVAC
  27. Area for cleanroom clothes
  28. Area for maintenance equipment
  29. Storage for cleanroom cleaning equipment
  30. Control room/IT (CNC—external to change room routine)

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Functional Area List: This is one of the core contents of the URS: it lists all functional areas that the facility must include. This list directly drives the facility layout design.

Several special areas worth noting:

  • #12 vs #13: Cell culture is divided into "clean" and "infected" rooms. This is a special requirement of BSL2 and virus production: pre-infection and post-infection operations must be carried out in different rooms
  • #19 Decontamination: Essential in a BSL2 facility, used for inactivation of live virus waste
  • #25 Waste Inactivation: Works together with #19 to handle liquid biological waste
  • #30 Control Room (CNC): The control room is located outside the gowning flow, so operators can monitor production without wearing cleanroom garments

Key Notes

The list contains 30 functional areas, showing that even for a facility using SUS, space planning remains quite complex. Although SUS eliminates CIP/SIP equipment rooms, it adds storage space for disposable consumables and waste handling areas.

Note #6 "Weighing room (optional)": in an SUS facility, many solutions are prepared in advance by suppliers and supplied directly in bags, so a weighing room may not be necessary.

Practical Application

In a CDMO context, if gene therapy products for several clients are to be manufactured in the same facility, the functional area list needs to consider additionally: the split between dedicated and shared areas, storage segregation of different clients' products, and a more flexible changeover strategy. The advantage of SUS is even more obvious here: changing products requires only replacing the single-use consumables, not cleaning an entire stainless steel system.

8.4.2 Utilities and Services

Original Text

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Utility categories:

  • Process gases: CO2, O2, process air, used for the rocker bag cell culture system
  • Water systems: WFI (water for injection), purified water (PW), tap water, chilled water, each with different uses and quality grades
  • Steam: plant steam (for heating) and clean steam (for sterilization). An SUS facility still needs clean steam for the autoclave
  • Waste water: divided into "Waste active" and "Waste inactive", a key distinction in a BSL2 facility

Key Notes

SUS does not eliminate utility requirements entirely: The table shows that although the CIP system is eliminated, WFI (for cleaning), clean steam (for the autoclave), liquid nitrogen (for frozen storage) and others are still needed. This is a reminder that SUS simplifies the cleaning and sterilization of process-contact surfaces, but the facility's basic utilities are still required.

VHP "Has to be considered": Vaporized hydrogen peroxide (VHP) is used for surface decontamination and airlock disinfection. It is marked here as "to be considered" rather than definitely needed, meaning that whether to adopt it will be decided at the later detailed design stage.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does an SUS facility still need clean steam? Where is it used?
  2. Why is the segregated handling of "Waste active" and "Waste inactive" important? What would be the consequences of mixing them?

8.4.3 Cleanroom and HVAC

Original Text

8.4.3.1 Room Classification

The rooms are classified according to the needs of the process and BSL2 containment requirements. The cleanrooms are designed according to ISO 14644-1 but validated, operated, and monitored according to EU GMP and PIC/S. The protection classes of electrical equipment in the cleanrooms shall be IP54 and in other areas according to room activity.

Table 8.4.3.1-1🔍 Sample Room Classifications

ISO classes are, unless separately mentioned, defined as during operation.

Airlocks shall be clean-room classified according to cleaner classification when at rest.

8.4.3.2 Ventilation Equipment

The ventilation system is divided into separate systems, according to use (one for cleanrooms and one for technical basement). Space will be reserved for future expansion of ventilation systems, which will serve cleanrooms on the first floor.

The cleanroom ventilation systems consist of fresh-air-handling units to obtain the air changes specified for each room or classified area. The ventilation system shall be designed as a "full fresh air" system in order to ensure the BSL2 and prevent cross-contamination in the production suite. The cleanrooms and technical spaces with high heat generation shall be equipped with separate circulation air-handling units to maintain room temperature within acceptable limits. Fan filter units shall be used in nonactive (BSL2) cleanrooms. There shall be a redundancy in the HVAC for the primary fan motors at a minimum. Due to the fact that operators will be in the cleanroom only eight hours a day, there shall be a possibility to have a lower airflow at night.

8.4.3.3 Air Filtration

The air for basement ventilation is filtered in three steps: G4, F7, and F9 filter, according to EN 779, within the air-handling unit.

Cleanroom air is prefiltered in four steps: G4, F7, F9, and H12, according to EN 779 and EN 1822, within the air-handling units. The terminal filter is minimum H13.

Exhaust air from the area where live virus is processed is filtered in two steps: F7 and H14 filter. Exhaust air from the basement is F7 filtered.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

ISO vs EU GMP cleanroom classification comparison:

  • ISO 5 / Grade A: core zone of aseptic operations (under a UDAF unidirectional airflow hood), with the strictest particle limits
  • ISO 7 / Grade B: background environment for aseptic operations
  • ISO 8 / Grade C: general operating area for aseptic manufacturing
  • ISO 8 at rest / Grade D: lower-risk operating area (ISO 8 achieved at rest)

Full Fresh Air System: All air supplied to the cleanroom is filtered fresh air and is not recirculated. This is a BSL2 facility requirement: if air were recirculated, live virus could be carried from one room to another, causing cross-contamination.

H14 exhaust filtration: Exhaust from areas where live virus is handled must pass through HEPA H14 filtration (efficiency ≥ 99.995%) to prevent live virus particles from being released to the atmosphere.

Analogy

A "full fresh air system" for HVAC is like the ventilation of a hospital negative pressure isolation ward: every breath of air supplied is fresh, and every breath of air exhausted has passed through HEPA filtration. Unlike ordinary office air conditioning, it does not recirculate indoor air. This ensures that "the air going in is clean and the air coming out is safe".

Key Notes

Night-time air volume reduction: The URS allows the supply air volume to be reduced during non-operating periods, which is an important energy-saving design consideration. A full fresh air system consumes a great deal of energy (because all the air must be heated/cooled and filtered), and reducing air volume at night can significantly lower operating costs. However, it must be ensured that the necessary pressure differentials and cleanliness (at-rest state) can still be maintained after the reduction.

Fan redundancy: The main fans must have backups. If a cleanroom HVAC fan fails, pressure differentials will be lost, which may endanger product quality and BSL2 safety.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does exhaust from live virus handling areas need H14 (99.995%) rather than H13 (99.95%)? What does the 0.045% difference in efficiency mean in practical terms?
  2. What are the main disadvantages of a "full fresh air system" compared with a "recirculation system"? Under what circumstances could partial recirculation be considered?

8.4.4 Control and Automation System

Original Text

8.4.4.1 Process Control System

The process equipment is of the stand-alone type, which means that every unit has its own control system. The system shall consist of a local PLC/PC and an HMI and it shall be possible to handle at least three user levels. Every user shall have an individualized user name and password to restrict the access and maintain the audit trail. The ability of a control system to communicate with a SCADA system shall be stated in the UR. There shall be electronic records from the process but no electronic batch records.

All utility systems and appropriate equipment shall have separate control systems, which control production, distribution, and quality of the utility. It shall be possible to connect critical parameters to a SCADA system.

There should be an overall data collection and viewing system for the entire production plant.

8.4.4.2 Facility Monitoring System

The cleanrooms shall have a facility monitoring system (FMS) consisting of a PLC and an HMI. The FMS shall monitor the critical parameters for room conditions [e.g. temperature, pressure, viable and nonviable particles, microbial presence, and moisture (some areas)] in every classified room. The critical refrigeration devices also have to be connected to the FMS. The whole GMP, SUS suite is controlled by the building monitoring system (BMS). Cleanrooms in grades A and B will be equipped with a continuous online particle monitoring system. The operational C grade areas are also equipped with online particle monitoring (e.g., manifold system).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Stand-alone Control: Each piece of equipment has its own PLC/PC and HMI, rather than being controlled by a central system. This is common in SUS facilities because disposable equipment suppliers usually provide integrated equipment control systems.

Three-level user access: Usually Operator, Supervisor and Administrator, each with different operating permissions. This is a basic requirement of 21 CFR Part 11 / EU GMP Annex 11 for electronic records.

Audit Trail: The system must record the time stamp and operator identity of all operations, modifications and data access. This is a core element of data integrity.

FMS vs BMS: The FMS (facility monitoring system) focuses on GMP-critical parameters (cleanroom pressure differentials, particles, temperature, etc.), while the BMS (building management system) handles facility control of the building as a whole (lighting, power, general HVAC, etc.). The two are usually independent systems but can exchange data.

Key Notes

Continuous online particle monitoring: Grade A and B areas must be equipped with continuous online particle monitoring, as required by EU GMP Annex 1. Grade C areas use a manifold system, in which one particle counter samples multiple sampling points in turn, a cost-effective solution.

The URS states "electronic records but no electronic batch records", meaning that although electronic data records exist, batch records are still mainly paper-based. This was common practice in 2014, but today the trend is toward electronic batch records (EBR).

Practice Questions

  1. Why does the URS require equipment control systems to be able to communicate with SCADA, even though SCADA is not currently used?
  2. What are the advantages and disadvantages of "stand-alone" versus "centralized" equipment control? Why is the former preferred in SUS facilities?

8.4.5 Health, Safety, and Environmental Requirements

Original Text

8.4.5.1 General

An HSE risk analysis shall be performed for the plant. Special attention shall be put on taking care of the environment. Contribution to the sustainable development shall be made by using natural resources rationally and by preventing health and environmental damage caused by waste. The generated waste shall primarily be reused, secondarily used as energy, and only as a final solution be transported to the dump. Special attention shall also be put on biosafety containment.

8.4.5.2 Air

Air from the production areas shall be filtered before leaving the building.

8.4.5.3 Drain

After inactivation, the drain liquid is processed further and neutralized in the municipal effluent plant together with all other drain systems.

8.4.5.4 Material

Waste material (e.g., biowaste, cardboard, plastics, paper, glass, and other waste) shall be sorted and reused if possible. If not, the material shall be transported to a dump or burned.

Hazardous and dangerous waste shall be gathered separately and moved to a storage area for hazardous waste. The waste shall be transported to incineration. Hazardous waste can be, for example, medical waste from production and equipment or returned medicines.

8.4.5.5 Emergency Systems

8.4.5.6 Noise

A risk assessment regarding noise levels shall be performed. Both internal and external noise shall be analyzed.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

HSE (Health, Safety, Environment) risk analysis: This is an aspect of facility design that is as important as GMP but often overlooked. The URS requires a systematic HSE risk assessment covering:

  • Health: risk of personnel exposure to biohazardous materials
  • Safety: liquid nitrogen asphyxiation risk, high-pressure steam scald risk, etc.
  • Environment: exhaust filtration, waste water inactivation, waste segregation and treatment

Waste handling priority: reuse → energy recovery → landfill only as a last resort. This reflects EU sustainable development regulatory requirements and is especially important for SUS facilities, because disposable equipment generates large amounts of plastic waste.

Key Notes

Tiered design of emergency systems:

  • UPS (uninterruptible power supply): provides short-term backup power (usually 15-30 minutes), allowing critical equipment (UDAF hoods, HVAC, monitoring systems) to shut down in an orderly way or switch to the diesel generator
  • Diesel generator: provides long-term backup power with wider coverage (including freezers and cold rooms)
  • Together they form "seamless power supply": the UPS handles the instantaneous switchover, and the diesel generator handles long-term supply

Note: no sprinkler system is required (Sprinkler = No). This may be because sprinkler discharge in a cleanroom would cause severe particulate and microbial contamination, and water in BSL2 areas could become biologically contaminated. An inert gas fire suppression system may be the alternative.

Analogy

An emergency power system is like a hospital's power backup: power to the operating room cannot be interrupted, so even if the outside power fails, the UPS takes over immediately while the diesel generator starts within seconds to provide long-term power. The UDAF hoods and HVAC of an SUS facility are like the surgical lights and laminar flow system of an operating room: once power is lost, the sterile environment is compromised.

Practice Questions

  1. How can the large amount of plastic waste generated by an SUS facility be balanced against "sustainable development" goals? What feasible solutions are there?
  2. Why is an "oxygen sensor" needed? Under what circumstances is liquid nitrogen dangerous to personnel?
  3. If a cleanroom does not use a sprinkler system, what alternative fire protection scheme should be adopted?

8.4.6 Maintenance and Service

Original Text

A maintenance program for the whole suite shall be developed before start of production. The program shall include the facility and technical equipment within, the ventilation system and equipment, the production equipment, and all laboratory equipment. The maintenance system shall produce, in advance, weekly reports of equipment that needs service.

All equipment in the area shall be assigned an item number, which shall be fed to the maintenance system. The maintenance personnel are responsible for service of all equipment. Two times a year (summer and winter) there shall be production stops for maintenance of the production equipment.

Spare parts for process equipment for two years of continuous production shall be stored.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Preventive Maintenance: The URS requires a complete maintenance plan to be established before production starts. Each piece of equipment has a unique Item Number to ensure traceability. The system can automatically generate weekly reports to give advance notice of equipment needing maintenance.

Planned Shutdown: One maintenance shutdown each summer and winter. Summer and winter were probably chosen so that seasonal maintenance of the HVAC system can be carried out at the same time.

Key Notes

Spare parts strategy: Two years of spare parts are stored. This is especially important for SUS facilities, because a supply chain interruption for single-use consumables directly affects production. The spare parts plan must cover not only equipment parts but also safety stock of disposable components.

In an SUS facility, maintenance needs are very different from those of a traditional stainless steel facility: no periodic re-passivation of piping and vessels, no periodic replacement of gaskets, no periodic inspection of welded joints, but it must be ensured that supporting equipment such as rocking platforms and peristaltic pump drives operate properly.

8.5 Documentation Requirements / 8.6 Validation and Testing

Original Text

8.5 Documentation Requirements

8.5.1 General

8.5.1.1 Version Handling

8.5.2 Risk Assessment

8.5.3 SOPs

8.6 Validation and Testing

(Note: Sections 8.5 and 8.6 are listed in the table of contents on p108 as part of the URS structure, with content on p120. These sections serve as placeholders indicating that the URS document must address documentation requirements, version control, risk assessment, SOPs, and validation/testing plans.)

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Documentation Requirements: The last sections of the URS remind us that a facility project is not only about hardware and equipment: the documentation system is also part of the URS. The URS should define:

  • Version Handling: version control rules for all documents
  • Risk Assessment: which risk assessments are required at the design stage
  • SOPs: which standard operating procedures must be completed before production
  • Validation & Testing: the basic strategy and scope of IQ/OQ/PQ

Key Notes

These "placeholder" sections remind URS authors that even if not all details can be fully defined in the initial version, a place must be reserved in the URS structure for these topics. As the project advances, these sections will be gradually filled in. This reflects the nature of the URS as a "living document".

Practice Questions

  1. Why does the validation strategy need to be planned at the URS stage, rather than waiting until equipment installation is complete?
  2. How does the risk assessment requirement in the URS relate to subsequent FMEA or HACCP analysis?

Section Summary

  • Role of the URS: The User Requirement Specification is the foundation document of an SUS facility project. It defines "what is needed" rather than "how to achieve it" and is the starting point for all subsequent design and qualification/validation activities
  • Example scenario: This URS example describes a BSL2 SUS facility for the production of gene therapy adenovirus vectors, which must comply with both EU GMP and FDA cGMP
  • Impact of SUS on facility design: CIP/SIP systems are eliminated, but basic utilities such as the autoclave, WFI system and clean steam are still needed; storage requirements for disposable consumables increase
  • BSL2 design challenges: A full fresh air HVAC system, H14 HEPA filtration of exhaust, waste water/waste inactivation systems, and balancing of pressure differentials between GMP positive pressure and BSL2 negative pressure
  • Cleanroom classification: From Grade A (ISO 5) to Unclassified, the cleanliness grade of each functional area must be clearly defined, with reference to both ISO 14644 and EU GMP standards
  • Control and monitoring: Stand-alone equipment control + SCADA integration capability + FMS/BMS environmental monitoring make up the complete automation architecture
  • HSE and emergency systems: Tiered power supply with UPS + diesel generator, waste segregation and inactivation, and safety measures such as oxygen detectors are essential elements of a BSL2 SUS facility
  • Living document concept: The URS is continuously updated as the project evolves; the initial version may contain placeholder sections that are gradually filled in later
A2

Section A2: Appendix: PEP Example

p121-p134

PDA Technical Report No. 66 (2014): Single-Use Systems | p121 – p134

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the purpose and structure of the Project Execution Plan (PEP) and its role in a single-use systems (SUS) project
  • Master the core elements of the PEP: project scope, execution approach, and quality strategy
  • Learn how to plan the Commissioning and Qualification (C&Q) process, including how FAT, SAT, IQ, and OQ are executed
  • Understand the division of roles and responsibilities between the Supervising Engineering Contractor (SEC) and the owner
  • Recognize the practical application of change management, risk management, and document control in a project

Table of Contents

9.1 Introduction 9.2 Project Overview 9.3 Contract Documents 9.4 Scope 9.5 Execution Approach 9.6 Organization 9.7 Quality 9.8 Risk Management 9.9 HSE 9.10 C&Q 9.11 Handover 9.12 Abbreviations 9.13 References

9.1 Introduction

9.1.1 Purpose

This appendix contains an example project execution plan (PEP). This material is referenced in Section 7.4.1, Project Execution Plan, and should be used as an example for how this type of document is structured. The complexity and size of the document will change according to the type of SUS and complexity of the project. These types of documents should be considered as living (i.e., they can be updated as appropriate should the project change). The document can be prepared by the end user or by a sponsor of the end user such as an engineering company or supplier.

The purpose of this PEP is to define and describe the project execution strategy as well as how the project will be managed and how quality control is applied in order to ensure that the project meets its goals regarding cost, time, and quality.

9.1.2 Context of this Document

This document provides a road map for the work to be completed. The PEP is a "fit-for-purpose" and living document that defines the supervising engineering contractor's (SEC's) scope of services and responsibilities for the project.

This plan describes and defines the project scope, the execution strategy, quality control systems, and management plan.

9.1.3 Revision of the PEP

All information included in this PEP is current as of the latest issue date of each document referred to. If changes are made that significantly affect the execution strategy, project management, and/or quality control activities as defined herein, a revision shall be made with rationale for the change explained in the new revision. The approval of the new revisions follows the same procedure as the approval of the original PEP. If there is a change to an appendix or a referred document to this PEP, only the document will be revised.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Project Execution Plan / PEP: The PEP is the "battle plan" for an entire SUS facility build or retrofit project, covering scope definition, execution approach, quality control, and schedule management. It is not a document that is written and then shelved; it is a "living document" that is updated continuously as the project progresses.

Supervising Engineering Contractor / SEC: In large pharmaceutical engineering projects, the owner usually appoints an SEC to coordinate design, procurement, construction, and validation. The SEC plays the central project management role.

Analogy

A PEP is like the "construction drawings plus the construction management plan" for building a house. Imagine fitting out a new restaurant: you need a document that explains what is to be done (scope), how it will be done (approach), who is responsible for what (organization), how quality will be controlled, and how the schedule will run. The PEP integrates all of this in one master document so that every stakeholder works from the same basis.

Key Notes

The "living document" nature of the PEP is very important. In SUS projects, the supplier ecosystem for single-use technology is still evolving quickly, so design or supplier choices often need to be adjusted during execution. The PEP must have a clear revision control mechanism so that every change goes through formal review and approval.

Note that the PEP may be written by the end user or by an engineering firm/supplier appointed by the end user, but whoever writes it, the owner must review and approve it.

Practice Questions

  1. Why is the PEP defined as a "living document"? In an SUS project, which factors are most likely to trigger a revision of the PEP?
  2. If a reference document in a PEP appendix is revised, does the main PEP also need to be revised? Why?

9.2 Project Overview

9.2.1 General

Company X wishes to design and equip a new production suite, GMPX, for manufacture of injectable products for viral particle gene therapy.

The products manufactured in the new production suite shall be based on modified live viral particle technologies (gene therapies). The manufacturing process shall cover production of biological bulk and final filled products for sterile, liquid drug applications. The viral developments are based on adenovirus. The production portfolio covers in the first place two bulk and two final products, which are known as Product 1 and Product 2.

9.2.2 Client

The client for the GMPX project is Company X.

9.2.3 Project History

A basic design was earlier developed for a traditional non-SUS approach.

The building for GMPX is already constructed alongside building T4, and all basic utility installations are available there.

9.2.4 Project Scope

The overall purpose of the project is:

  • To design and equip a new manufacturing suite, to be integrated into the T4 complex, for manufacture of injectable products for viral particle gene therapy with disposable technology
  • To finalize OQ for the manufacturing suite within 18 months from start of basic design within agreed project budget

9.2.5 Project Objectives, Success Criteria, and Focus Areas

(Refer to the original document for the detailed table of objectives, success criteria, and focus areas.)

9.2.6 Steering Committee

The SEC and Company X will implement a common steering committee for the project. The purpose and responsibilities of the steering committee are to:

  • Assure that the project goals are met
  • Create a common picture of questions, problems, and limitations within each organization
  • Create a common picture of roles and responsibilities between parties
  • Create a common picture of how project risks should be handled
  • Approve project execution plan, schedules, economy, and planning
  • Supervise project progress
  • Approve monthly project reporting

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Gene therapy and SUS: This example shows very typically why SUS is particularly suited to gene therapy products. Viral vector manufacturing involves handling live virus and requires an environment with a high biosafety level (BSL). Single-use systems can greatly reduce the risk of cross-contamination and simplify the complexity of cleaning validation.

Steering Committee: The highest decision-making body, formed jointly by the SEC and the owner, responsible for overseeing project progress, approving plan changes, and controlling budget and schedule.

Analogy

Think of this project as "converting a traditional china-plate restaurant into a delivery kitchen that uses disposable tableware." Company X had originally planned a conventional stainless steel (non-SUS) line but later decided to adopt single-use technology. The building is already built (like a kitchen space that already exists), and the task now is to redesign and install all the equipment inside as single-use systems.

Key Notes

Pay particular attention to the project schedule target: only 18 months from the start of Basic Design to completion of OQ. This would be almost impossible with a traditional stainless steel line, but SUS removes a great deal of piping installation, weld inspection, and CIP/SIP validation, greatly shortening the build schedule. This is one of the core business advantages of SUS.

The Steering Committee is also worth learning from: it gives the SEC and the owner a structured communication channel and avoids project delays or quality problems caused by information asymmetry.

Practical Application

As a CDMO, if you are building a gene therapy SUS line for a client, this PEP example offers an excellent structural reference. You can apply its framework for scope definition, organization, and quality strategy directly and then adjust the details to your own needs. The Steering Committee design in particular is important for collaboration between a CDMO and a sponsor.

9.3 Contract Documents

9.3 Contract Documents

Basic Design:

  • Contract document
  • Project scope
  • Project organization
  • Reference group

Detailed Design and Execution:

  • Contract document
  • Project proposal
  • Project contents (basic design report)
  • Project UR
  • Project methodology
  • Process module diagram
  • Layouts for floors 0 and 2 with BSL and GMP classification
  • List of equipment and materials
  • Impact assessment GMP
  • Permits to be submitted
  • Main project time schedule
  • Definition of milestones
  • List of man-hours and travel time
  • Procurement strategy
  • Project organization
  • Manuals and training documents
  • Definitions of GMP and GEP

9.3.1 Customer X Deliverables

Customer X is responsible for providing:

  • Drawings of existing building
  • Information on available utilities and other information in existing building
  • Process know-how and product requirements and specifications
  • User Participation
  • Reviewer and approver of documents

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Contract Documents: This section distinguishes the contract documents for two phases: Basic Design and Detailed Design and Execution. Contracting in phases like this is common in large pharmaceutical engineering projects and can reduce the owner's risk.

User Requirement / UR: The requirements specification provided by the owner, which is the foundation of the whole design. In an SUS project, the UR needs to cover special requirements such as material compatibility of single-use components and extractables/leachables specifications.

Analogy

Contract documents are like a prenuptial agreement: before the project "marries," both parties must clearly agree on their responsibilities, deliverables, and rights and obligations. The Basic Design contract is like the "engagement," confirming the general direction; the Detailed Design contract is the "wedding," where every detail is written out.

Key Notes

Note that Customer X's delivery responsibilities include "Process know-how and product requirements." This is especially important in an SUS project because the selection of single-use components (such as bag film and tubing material) must be based on process needs and product characteristics. If the owner cannot supply this information on time, it directly affects the SEC's design work.

Practice Questions

  1. Why split the contract documents into two phases, Basic Design and Detailed Design? What benefit does this have for project risk management?
  2. If the owner delays providing process know-how, what knock-on effects does this have on an SUS project?

9.4 Scope

9.4.1 Objectives of the Scope

The GMPX project activities such as value engineering and thorough schedule control of all design, construction, commissioning and qualification (C&Q), and changes will have a high importance throughout the project.

9.4.2 Scope Supervision

The scope and progress will be supervised by the steering committee. Changes in the scope will be handled by change orders as described in Section 4.6.

9.4.3 Scope of Engineering

All engineering required to supply and build the process building is within the scope of the SEC. The scope comprises design development of the technical documents prepared during basic design to a detailed design status (i.e., the status required for manufacturing, installation, commissioning, operation, and maintenance of the facility), with all the installed equipment.

The basic design documents have been reviewed and approved by Company X and constitute the project baseline, setting the specific requirements for the project. Any changes from this baseline are to be taken up through the change management procedure.

Reference documents (including client inputs) used during basic design become backup documents. In case of contradiction between requirements specified in the basic design documents and requirements specified in any of the reference documents, then the latter shall take precedence, unless such requirement is superseded by a subsequent change management.

During detailed design the information set by the basic design package is used to develop construction documentation.

9.4.3.1 Design Steps Overview

The SEC will provide a complete design of the new manufacturing suite in the following steps:

  • Front-end definition
  • Basic design
  • Detailed design

9.4.3.2 Basic Design

The SEC will prepare:

  • Project proposal with executive summary project contents
  • Project plan
  • Biosafety level impact assessment

9.4.3.3 Detailed Design

The SEC will provide engineering with:

  • Approved and signed piping and instrumentation diagrams
  • Approved building layout
  • Approved main documents such as UR, PEP, and QAP

9.4.4 Scope of Equipment and Systems

9.4.4.1 General

The scope includes all architectural considerations for cleanroom and mechanical spaces (with exception of the outer building walls) and finishes, building installations for HVAC, utility piping, safety, process piping, process equipment, electrical, and automation.

9.4.4.2 Spare Parts

The SEC will provide spare parts required for commissioning, testing, and qualification.
