The SEC will also provide critical spare parts for two years from installation date.

9.4.4.3 Consumables

The SEC will supply the consumables, such as filters, gaskets, lubricant, lamps and lights, fluorescents, and bags, that are required until end of commissioning. Subsequent consumables shall be provided by Company X.

9.4.4.4 Disposables

Disposable bags for cell expansion will be supplied by the SEC based on the UR for the bags in a limited amount only if necessary for IQ and OQ purposes.

9.4.4.5 Utilities

The running cost for utilities during construction and commissioning and qualification (C&Q) will be accounted directly to Company X. All temporary utilities needed only during construction and C&Q will be supported by the project.

9.4.5 Change Management

Management of project change is essential to successful project execution. The project will implement a change management process that will provide for timely identification of deviations or changes in project scope.

All change orders shall be recorded in a change-order log.

See QAP for change control of documents and C&Q activities.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Value Engineering: Looking for lower-cost alternatives without reducing function or quality. In an SUS project, this may include evaluating single-use components from different suppliers and optimizing the piping layout to reduce the number of connections.

Project Baseline: The approved Basic Design documents form the project baseline. Any change that deviates from the baseline must go through the formal change management procedure. This is the key mechanism for preventing scope creep.

Design Steps: Front-end Definition → Basic Design → Detailed Design is the standard three-phase design process. The level of detail increases at each phase, and so do the resources invested and the commitment made.

Analogy

The three design phases are like painting an oil painting: Front-end Definition is the composition (deciding what to paint), Basic Design is the pencil underdrawing (fixing proportions and structure), and Detailed Design is the finished colored work (every detail in place). If the underdrawing is wrong, correcting it during the coloring stage is very expensive. This is why review and approval at the Basic Design phase matter so much.

Key Notes

Special considerations for disposables in SUS projects: Note that 9.4.4.4 says single-use bags are provided "in limited quantities, only where necessary for IQ and OQ." This is because single-use components have a limited shelf life; if they are bought in large quantities too early, they may expire before PQ or routine production. The procurement strategy for an SUS project must take component shelf life into account.

Document precedence principle: When the Basic Design documents conflict with the reference documents, the reference documents take precedence (unless superseded through later change management). This principle ensures that the owner's original requirements are not diluted by interpretation during the design process.

Practice Questions

  1. In an SUS project, what concrete cost savings can Value Engineering bring? Give three examples.
  2. Why are single-use bags provided only in limited quantities at the IQ/OQ stage? What are the risks of buying large quantities early?
  3. Why is Change Management listed as a separate section? In an SUS project, what are the most common triggers for change?

9.5 Project Execution Approach

9.5.1 General

As shown in the diagram below, many project activities will overlap. Detailed design, construction and installation, and C&Q will overlap. This figure is schematic and does not apply to the project time frame.

Project Phase Flow (schematic):

DD PHASE → Construction and Installation → Installation Commissioning (Static Part of SAT) → Walk Down → Documented Mechanical Completion → IQ → OQ → ATO, CCC Care Custody and Control → Operational Commissioning (Operational Part of SAT) → Handover Package (All Documents)

SAT — Site Acceptance Test
IQ — Installation Qualification
OQ — Operational Qualification
ATO — Approval to Operate
CCC — Care, Custody, and Control

9.5.2 Detailed Design (DD)

9.5.2.1 General

The SEC's operation department appoints and enrolls projects' design resources as are required by a project's scope and schedule. The request comes from the project manager or project leads by the project manager's approval.

9.5.2.2 Design Disciplines

The SEC splits engineering into the following disciplines for this project:

Architectural

  • Cleanroom

Process technology

  • Upstream (cell culture and virus infection)
  • Downstream (product purification)
  • Fill and finish
  • Packaging (inspection and packaging)
  • Labeling
  • Support (dishwasher, autoclave)

Utilities

  • Black piping
  • HVAC
  • Electrical
  • Clean utilities

Automation

QA is involved during the DD phase to review and watch over GMP requirements as an independent part of the project. See QAP, Doc. No. GMPX-QAP-07-001.

9.5.2.3 Engineering Organization

The project engineering manager leads and directs all engineering activities. The engineering manager is primarily responsible for providing backup to the leads in matters of coordination between the disciplines and/or for each discipline. There is a project design lead appointed for each discipline.

9.5.2.4 Design Execution

Detailed design and construction will be overlapping and partially concurrent activities.

During detailed design, detailed construction and installation drawings, specifications, schematics, test sheets, and other documents will be generated at a pace and content as is required by construction and installation.

The release of construction and installation documents is preceded by a series of reviews and approvals, internal to the SEC as well as together with the client's representatives.

See the QAP and C&Q plan for more details about the review and approval process.

9.5.2.5 Design Documents

Governing design documents will be sent to Company X for review and approval. Such documents are listed in the document progress table.

Detailed design documents that are required for construction and installation only will not be issued to Company X for review and approval unless such issuing is required to obtain building permits.

9.5.2.6 Permits and Authority Approvals

  • Building permit: QVIM June 2007
  • Authority review and approval before any construction activity starts
  • Occupancy permits (if applicable); need to be applied for by Company X

9.5.2.7 As-Built Drawings

Approved documents that need to be as-built have to be identified in a later stage.

These documents will be redlined at the site during construction and C&Q.

Redlined documents can be used as as-built documents for C&Q and handover of the systems. The work will be a joint effort of the SEC, vendors, and, if applicable, Company X.

These documents will thereafter be reissued (CAD) as revisions as-built by the SEC.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Concurrent Engineering: The PEP states explicitly that DD, construction, and C&Q will overlap. This is the core strategy of fast-track construction: compressing the overall schedule through parallel working.

As-Built Drawings: During construction, the actual installation often deviates from the original design. Redlines mark these differences directly on the original drawings on site, and the SEC eventually reissues the drawings formally as the As-Built version.

Design Disciplines: The engineering work is split into four main areas (architecture, process technology, utilities, and automation), each further subdivided. This ensures that every discipline has a responsible Design Lead.

Analogy

Concurrent engineering is like fitting out a house while it is still being built: while the walls of the first floor are still going up, the drawings for the second floor are already being prepared. This can shorten the schedule substantially, but it demands very close communication and coordination between teams; otherwise the pipe penetrations left in the first-floor walls may not match the second-floor piping design.

Key Notes

QA independence: Note that 9.5.2.2 specifically stresses that QA takes part in the DD phase as an "independent role" reviewing GMP compliance. This follows the basic GMP principle that the quality assurance function must be independent of production and engineering to avoid conflicts of interest.

Tiered document approval: Not every design document needs owner approval. Only the "Governing Design Documents" are sent to the owner. Detailed drawings used purely for construction are controlled internally by the SEC. This is a pragmatic approach that keeps the owner from being swamped with documents.

Figure Analysis

The project phase flow chart clearly shows the full life cycle from DD to Handover. Key milestones include:

  • Documented Mechanical Completion: The formal confirmation point that construction is complete
  • ATO (Approval to Operate): The key threshold for approval to operate, indicating that the system has passed IQ/OQ
  • CCC (Care, Custody, and Control): The turning point at which the system passes from the SEC to the owner's management

Practice Questions

  1. Why should QA be involved as an independent role as early as the DD phase? What problems might occur if QA only becomes involved at the C&Q stage?
  2. Is Concurrent Engineering easier to implement in an SUS project than in a traditional stainless steel project? Why?
9.5.3 – 9.5.4 Subcontracting, Procurement & Commissioning

9.5.3 Supervising Engineering Contractor's Subcontracting and Procurement

9.5.3.1 General

The SEC will subcontract and procure material and equipment.

Subcontractors for design, support material, installations, and transports will be appointed by the SEC without prior information to Company X.

9.5.3.2 Subcontract Documents

Such subcontract documents that constitute technical specifications will be presented to Company X insofar as such documents relate to GMP equipment, systems, or components.

9.5.3.3 Equipment Log

Status of purchasing, design, fabrication, testing, and delivery of major equipment will be reported at project meetings.

9.5.3.4 Expediting

9.5.3.4.1 Equipment

During the complete delivery phase from contract to completed handover, the SEC supervises and checks the vendors' work and progress. This is made according to the contract with the specific vendor. A delivery of equipment or system is expedited in the following steps:

  • Follow up milestone achievements according to agreed schedule continuously during design, construction, and testing at the vendor's workshop. This is performed by visiting the vendor and/or following up progress reports from the vendor. If deemed to be required, such follow-up will encompass the vendor's major subcontractors also.
  • Review and approve the vendors' design documents.
  • Perform and report FAT.
  • Perform receiving check of equipment.
  • Supervise and check installation and start-up.
  • Supervise and attend qualification work, and review and approve qualification documentation (if applicable).
  • Check and approve final project documentation.

The monitoring, expediting, and reporting of progress of work is executed by the respective design leads.

9.5.3.5 Material

Purchasing and supply of material other than equipment will be monitored, expedited by the project's purchaser.

9.5.3.6 Subcontractors

All subcontractors for construction and installation activities will be contracted by the SEC on behalf of Company X.

9.5.3.7 Procurement

All purchase orders during detailed design and orders of equipment will be signed by the SEC's project manager and finally approved by Company X and placed in the name of Company X.

This will be described in more details in the procurement strategy and the purchase plan.

9.5.4 Commissioning and Qualification

Execution of C&Q is described in the commissioning and qualification plan GMPX-C&Q-07-001 and in the QAP Doc. no. GMPX-QAP-07-001. The execution can be summarized as follows.

9.5.4.1 Factory Acceptance Test (FAT) and Site Acceptance Test (SAT) for Systems Assessed as Indirect Impact and Direct Impact

Suppliers and contractors of indirect- and direct-impact systems must, if applicable according to SQP during FAT/SAT, demonstrate to the SEC that all requirements, including functions' "as-built" documentation, are fulfilled and available and that the deliverables operate as required. In situations where the supplier or contractor is not in a position to perform FAT/SAT as expected, the SEC, in cooperation with the supplier or contractor, must perform additional tests, possibly as commissioning.

FAT/SAT shall be prepared in a manner that ensures that all C&Q requirements are tested and documented and fulfill the acceptance criteria at the completion of SAT.

When the machine or product has been assembled at the site and adequately connected and run-in tested by the supplier or contractor, the SAT is carried out. This takes place in a way similar to the FAT, according to SEC-approved SAT protocols. For indirect-impact and direct-impact systems, SEC QA should review protocols and test plans in the planning of SAT to ensure that approval of the SAT report is obtainable. The SAT is finished with the SEC and SEC QA approval of the SAT report.

9.5.4.2 Commissioning Indirect-Impact Systems and Direct-Impact Systems

For systems assessed as direct or indirect impact, fulfillment of C-requirements shall be demonstrated through commissioning. The work package owner (WPO) has the responsibility to ensure that commissioning activities are well planned and adequately executed, documented, and reported. The Q-coordinator is responsible for overall coordination and follow-up.

Vendor SAT is part of the commissioning.

9.5.4.3 No-Impact Systems

For systems assessed as having no impact, fulfillment of requirements must be demonstrated and documented according to GEP based on the tradition in the discipline.

The WPO is responsible for planning and executing the commissioning activities for no-impact systems and to ensure that the activities are documented in a way that allows the SEC to illustrate that all requirements are met.

9.5.4.4 Qualification

For systems assessed as direct impact, fulfillment of Q-requirements in relation to installations and operations shall be demonstrated through installation qualification and operational qualification (IQ/OQ). Qualification and especially prerequisites before the qualification can start shall be described in the protocols. IQ and OQ must be carried out according to the approved protocols.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Impact Assessment: Systems are classified into three categories: Direct Impact, Indirect Impact, and No Impact. This classification determines the depth and rigor of C&Q:

  • Direct Impact: Systems that directly affect product quality (such as bioreactors and filling equipment) require full IQ/OQ
  • Indirect Impact: Systems with indirect impact (such as HVAC and purified water systems) require FAT/SAT plus commissioning
  • No Impact: Systems that do not affect product quality (such as lighting) only need to be commissioned according to GEP standards

C-requirements vs Q-requirements: C-requirements are engineering requirements verified through commissioning; Q-requirements are GMP requirements verified through formal qualification (IQ/OQ). This distinction is the science- and risk-based approach to verification recommended by ASTM E2500 and ISPE.

Analogy

Impact Assessment is like infection-risk tiering in a hospital: the operating room (Direct Impact) needs the strictest disinfection and monitoring, the ward corridor (Indirect Impact) needs basic cleaning standards, and the parking lot (No Impact) needs only general maintenance. The level of control in each area is proportional to its impact on patient safety.

Key Notes

The special nature of FAT/SAT in SUS: In an SUS project, FAT is usually performed at the single-use component supplier's plant and focuses on verifying the component quality control system and manufacturing consistency; SAT is performed at the user's site to verify how the components perform under actual process conditions.

Procurement placed in the owner's name: Although the SEC is responsible for procurement, orders are placed in the name of Company X. This ensures that the owner keeps ultimate control of the supplier relationship and also helps with the handover of warranty and after-sales service.

Practical Application

In a CDMO's SUS project, the result of the Impact Assessment directly determines the validation workload and budget. A common mistake is to classify too many systems as Direct Impact, which inflates validation cost. The pragmatic approach is to classify strictly according to each system's actual impact on product quality, with a scientific rationale supporting the classification decision. For example, if an SUS mixing bag is used only for buffer preparation, it could be classified as Indirect Impact rather than Direct Impact.

Practice Questions

  1. In an SUS bioreactor system, which components are Direct Impact? Which might be Indirect Impact?
  2. If a supplier cannot perform FAT at its plant, how should the SEC handle it? What effect does this have on the project schedule?
  3. What practical benefits does the distinction between C-requirements and Q-requirements bring to validation efficiency?

9.6 Organization

9.6.1 Supervising Engineering Contractor's Roles and Responsibilities

An overview of the SEC's roles and responsibilities is presented in the following table.

9.6.2 Company X Organization

For details of the Company X organogram, refer to the Company X organogram in (4286) S9.13 References. Roles and responsibilities between the SEC and Company X are defined in Section 2.6 Steering Committee.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

RACI-style role definitions: Although this table does not use a formal RACI matrix format, it clearly defines the main responsibilities of each role. In an actual project, it is advisable to expand these roles further into a RACI matrix (Responsible, Accountable, Consulted, Informed) to avoid ambiguity in responsibilities.

Clash Control: The Engineering Manager is responsible for "Physical Coordination" and "Clash Control," which means making sure that the designs of different engineering disciplines do not conflict spatially (for example, piping running through a structural beam). Although an SUS facility has less process piping, spatial coordination is still needed among HVAC ducts, cable trays, and the support frames for single-use components.

Analogy

The SEC's organization is like a football team: the Project Manager is the head coach (overseeing the whole), the Engineering Manager is the captain (coordinating the positions), the Design Leads are the specialist coaches for each position (training the forwards, midfielders, and defenders), QA is the referee (independently overseeing compliance with the rules), and the Document Controller is the record keeper (making sure the data from every match is recorded accurately).

Key Notes

Note that the C&Q Manager's responsibility is to "plan and coordinate testing," not to "execute testing." This means the actual test execution may be carried out by the Design Leads or subcontractors, while the C&Q Manager handles overall planning and coordination. This division of work is common in large projects and prevents the C&Q Manager from becoming a bottleneck.

Practice Questions

  1. If you were the CDMO's project lead, what additional roles would you add to the SEC's organization to make sure the special needs of an SUS project are met?
  2. Why does Project QA need to align with both the SEC's quality standards and Company X's regulatory requirements? How might the two differ?

9.7 Quality

9.7.1 Project Quality Strategy

A validation master plan (VMP) is produced by Company X for the project. This VMP will be used to provide the basis for all validation and quality activities during the lifecycle of the project.

The SEC is responsible for carrying out the commissioning and qualification activities (IQ and OQ) unless otherwise described in the VMP. The subsequent process qualification, cleaning validation, and process validation are carried out by Company X and are not included in this project.

The VMP is the overall document for validation in the project and will be master and basis for qualification of equipment facilities and utilities. A separate VMP will be written for process validation.

The qualifications sequence of CP, IQ, OQ, and PQ activities are described in details in the QAP.

9.7.1.5 Partner/Vendor/Supplier Qualifications

Partners and suppliers describe their "normal" test in the tender documents (for instance, via examples of test plans). If possible, the tests are performed on the GMP level when relevant.

  • Tests must address all requirements.
  • Scope of the test activity is determined in the contract (the partners and suppliers draft QAP).
  • After this, the SEC prepares a draft of IQ and OQ protocol in which test plans from the supplier may be entered, if relevant.
  • The suppliers test planning may be adjusted to achieve maximum synergy.
  • The SEC approves supplier's final QAP.

Time schedule and economy of the project includes a completed IQ and OQ with the SEC as the final step. The validation involves the active participation of the end user and Company X-QA. The scope of this is described in the basic design phase.

9.7.2 Quality Activity Plan (QAP)

The project's QAP manages all quality activities and quality documentation to be prepared. The QAP is made in compliance with the requirements in the VMP.

9.7.3 Document Control

Document control is handled in accordance with the QAP.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

VMP (Validation Master Plan): The VMP is the highest-level document for all validation activities and is written by the owner (Company X). It defines the validation scope, strategy, roles and responsibilities, and acceptance criteria. In this project, the VMP covers equipment qualification (IQ/OQ), while process validation (PV) has its own dedicated VMP.

QAP (Quality Activity Plan): The QAP is the execution-level document under the VMP, listing in detail each quality activity, its owner, schedule, and deliverables. The VMP can be seen as the "constitution of quality" and the QAP as its "implementing rules."

Supplier Qualification Synergy: The PEP proposes a very pragmatic approach: integrating the supplier's standard test plan into the SEC's IQ/OQ package to avoid duplicate testing. This is especially important in SUS projects because single-use component suppliers usually already have well-established factory test procedures.

Analogy

The relationship among the VMP, the QAP, and the IQ/OQ Protocols is like a legal system: the VMP is the "constitution" (highest guiding principles), the QAP is the "law" (specific rules), and each IQ/OQ Protocol is the "standard operating procedure" (specific execution steps). Each level must comply with the one above and must not contradict it.

Key Notes

Simplified cleaning validation in SUS projects: Note that the PEP says "cleaning validation" is the responsibility of Company X and outside the scope of this project. In an SUS facility, because product-contact parts are single-use, cleaning validation in the traditional sense is greatly reduced, but cleaning validation may still be needed for the exterior of reusable equipment and for the environment.

Clear delineation of validation responsibilities: The SEC is responsible up to OQ, and Company X takes over from PQ. This handover point must be very clear, because the quality of the completed OQ directly affects whether PQ can proceed smoothly.

Practice Questions

  1. Why is the VMP written by the owner rather than the SEC? How does this affect the formulation of the validation strategy?
  2. What are the benefits of integrating the supplier's test plan into the IQ/OQ package? What risks might there be?
  3. In an SUS project, how does the scope of cleaning validation differ from that of a traditional stainless steel facility?

9.8 Risk Management

9.8 Risk Management

There will be a number of risk analyses performed during the project. Their main purpose is to identify risk early and create activities to reduce the risk.

The risk analyses are:

  • BD Project Risk Analysis 1
  • BD BSL and GMP Risk Analysis with Focus on the Layout
  • DD Project Risk Analysis 2
  • DD HSE Risk Analysis on the Execution Phase

9.8.1 Documentation

  • Language and templates — Project language is UK English with the exception of documents used for Finnish craftsmen. The SEC templates are used when adequate.
  • Organizing project data — Data storage must be planned for and organized at the start of the project in order to properly archive the information during project closeout. Proper setup at the start of the project will help make it possible to utilize data efficiently, minimize rework, and ensure complete electronic records retention.
  • Project documentation — In basic design the planned documents are described in the object breakdown structure. The quality managed documents are described in the QAP. The document delivery of the suppliers and partners are described in the tender documents or in the contract of the project.
  • Technical tools and standards — For each skill or profession (construction, architecture, bulk process, utility, etc.) it is necessary to determine and develop tools and standards to ensure continuous engineering of the project parts. The lead engineers manage these tools and standards. Specification tools and standards are being prepared in basic design.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Phased risk analysis: The PEP plans four risk analyses, carried out during the Basic Design (BD) and Detailed Design (DD) phases. This reflects the principle of "progressive risk management": as design detail increases, the focus of risk analysis shifts from the macro level (project risk, layout risk) to the micro level (execution risk, HSE risk).

BSL (Biosafety Level) Risk Analysis: Because this project involves handling live virus, BSL risk analysis is especially important. It must assess the risk of virus escape, personnel exposure, and environmental release, and ensure that the facility design controls these risks effectively.

Analogy

Phased risk analysis is like a pilot's checklist: one before takeoff (BD Risk Analysis), one during cruise (DD Risk Analysis), and another when preparing to land (HSE on Execution). Each phase faces different risks and needs different checking priorities. Doing it once is not enough; it must be reassessed at every key milestone.

Key Notes

Foresight in electronic record management: 9.8.1 stresses planning data storage at the very start of the project. This is especially important in the modern GMP environment because regulatory requirements (such as 21 CFR Part 11 and EU GMP Annex 11) set strict expectations for the integrity, traceability, and long-term retention of electronic records. If missing documents or incompatible formats are discovered at project close-out, the cost of remediation will be very high.

Practice Questions

  1. In an SUS project, which risks do traditional stainless steel projects not need to consider? (Hint: supply chain, component compatibility)
  2. Why perform BSL and GMP risk analysis as early as the Basic Design phase? What might happen if it is postponed to the Detailed Design phase?

9.9 Health, Safety, and Environment

9.9 Health, Safety, and Environment

An initial HSE evaluation has been started. The first version of this plan will be issued and approved before any construction activity starts. This plan will be updated during the construction with new items and activities.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

HSE (Health, Safety, and Environment): The HSE plan is a document required by regulation and must be approved before construction starts. In an SUS facility that handles live virus, HSE must also give special consideration to biosafety risks, such as exposure to viral aerosols and the inactivation of waste single-use components.

Key Notes

The HSE plan is also a living document, updated continuously as construction progresses. This is because the risks on a construction site change with each work activity: early on they may be structural construction risks, while later they may involve the handling of chemicals and biological materials.

9.10 Commissioning and Qualification

9.10 Commissioning and Qualification

When the SEC finds it beneficial, an all-day meeting will be called in order to approve documents and/or carry out reviews. This will be scheduled no less than one week in advance and with duration from one day to several days.

When the SEC finds it beneficial, an all-day commissioning day will be called in order to correct errors and carry through cleanroom documents. This will be arranged no less than one week ahead and with duration from one day to several days.

The SEC is responsible for carrying out or supervising and reporting all qualification activities up to and including OQ.

Company X will participate in the test activities.

Company X provides QA resources during the OQ test period.

Company X is responsible for the calibration and maintenance after OQ.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

C&Q handover point: This section clearly draws the dividing line between the SEC and Company X in validation activities. The SEC's responsibility ends when OQ is complete; calibration, maintenance, and PQ after OQ are all taken over by Company X. This is the standard handover model in the industry.

Analogy

This is like the handover process when buying a new car: the dealer (SEC) is responsible up to bringing the car to you and completing all the checks (IQ/OQ); after delivery, servicing, fueling, and routine maintenance (PQ, calibration, maintenance) are the owner's (Company X's) responsibility. But both parties must be present at delivery to confirm the condition of the car (Company X's QA resources take part in OQ).

Key Notes

Transfer of calibration and maintenance responsibility after OQ: This detail is easily overlooked. Company X must build its own calibration and maintenance capability (including SOPs, staff training, and spare parts inventory) before OQ is complete; otherwise a management vacuum may appear after the SEC withdraws.

9.11 Handover and Operation Support

9.11 Handover and Operation Support

The project is handed over to the customer in three steps: 1) ready to run, 2) handover, and 3) final handover. Final handover is the last part of the project.

The project is handed over to the customer with the participation of the SEC project manager, relevant SEC and/or contract project engineers, and representatives from the customer of his or her own choosing.

If the customer wishes to put the area into use before the handover has taken place, the SEC project manager shall ensure that a physical examination of the area takes place together with the customer or the user and that an agreement on operation and maintenance responsibilities is made.

Prior to final handover, deviation lists will be resolved by a cleanup team.

Warranties from third-party vendors, suppliers, and subcontractors are handed over to Company X.

PQ and PV activities are not part of this project. The SEC recommends the client to plan on a PQ support team. SEC will supply resources for support in PQ/PV phase if Company X so wishes. The SEC resources will work under Company X supervision and management during PQ/PV.

Company X has full responsibility for running and maintaining equipment after handover. This must be carried out without invoking any delays to the project.

Documentation will be handed over in binders and/or electronic copy as specified by Company X at final handover, and direct to Company X document control. Where the SEC has produced or obtained from a supplier an electronic copy, this will be provided with the final documentation.

Optimizing program and parameters is not within the scope of this project. Education and training are to be defined. (See C&Q plan.)

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Three-Step Handover:

  • Ready to Run: Equipment is fully installed and has passed OQ, and operation can begin
  • Handover: Responsibility for operating and maintaining the system is formally transferred from the SEC to Company X
  • Final Handover: All deviations are closed, documentation is complete, the warranty is transferred, and the project formally ends

Deviation Lists / Punch Lists: Before handover, a list is compiled of all outstanding issues. The Cleanup Team is responsible for resolving these items one by one before Final Handover.

Analogy

The three-step handover is like taking over a new home: the first step, "pre-acceptance" (Ready to Run), confirms that the hardware is complete; the second step, "key handover" (Handover), is when you can move in; the third step, "final payment" (Final Handover), is when all defects have been repaired, the warranty papers handed over, and the contractor has left the site. Each step needs sign-off from both parties.

Key Notes

Planning the link to PQ/PV: The PEP states explicitly that PQ/PV is outside the scope of this project, but the SEC advises the owner to set up a PQ support team early. This is very pragmatic advice: many projects discover only after OQ that they lack the internal capability to execute PQ, which delays time to market. When taking on an SUS project, a CDMO should include the manpower and resource needs for PQ/PV in the plan at the PEP stage.

Risk control for early use: If the owner needs to use the facility before formal handover, the PEP requires a site inspection and a signed agreement on operation and maintenance responsibilities. This clarifies the boundary of responsibility and avoids disputes over accountability when problems arise.

Practical Application

For a CDMO, the Handover phase is the critical moment that decides whether the project succeeds. It is advisable to add a "Knowledge Transfer Plan" to the PEP to make sure the SEC's technical team passes all operating knowledge and maintenance experience to the CDMO's operations team before withdrawing. In SUS projects especially, the installation techniques for single-use components, aseptic connection practices, and troubleshooting experience are "tacit knowledge" that is often more valuable than written documents.

Practice Questions

  1. Why design a three-step handover rather than a single handover? What are the key deliverables of each step?
  2. If unresolved deviations remain at Final Handover, how should they be handled? Can the handover proceed with open deviations?
  3. Why must the SEC work "under the supervision and management of Company X" when providing support in the PQ/PV phase?

9.12 Abbreviations

9.12 Abbreviations

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

This abbreviation list is a standard component of a PEP. In an actual project, it is advisable to place the abbreviation list at the beginning of the PEP (rather than in an appendix), because readers need to consult it frequently while reading.

Pay particular attention to the special meaning of the following abbreviations in SUS projects:

  • GEP (Good Engineering Practice): The standard applied to No Impact systems
  • BSL (Biosafety Level): A key design parameter in gene therapy product manufacturing
  • SEC (Supervising Engineering Contractor): The core executing role in this example

Key Notes

The distinction between GEP and GMP is the key to understanding the C&Q strategy: GEP applies to systems that do not directly affect product quality, with lower requirements that still need to be documented; GMP applies to systems that directly affect product quality, with formal validation documentation and approval processes. In an SUS project, distinguishing the two correctly can significantly reduce validation workload and cost.

9.13 References

9.13 References

The following reference documents should be read in association with this project execution plan:

  • Level A Time Schedule
  • Project Quality Activity Plan (QAP)
  • Project Validation Master Plan (VMP)
  • Object Breakdown Structure
  • Procurement Strategy
  • Purchase Plan
  • Travel and Costs Principles

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The PEP's supporting document system: The PEP is not an isolated document; together with several supporting documents it forms a complete project management framework:

  • Level A Time Schedule: The highest-level project schedule, showing key milestones
  • QAP: The detailed plan for quality activities
  • VMP: The top-level guiding document for the validation strategy
  • Object Breakdown Structure: Breaks the facility down into manageable units
  • Procurement Strategy: Defines supplier selection and the procurement process

Key Notes

In an SUS project, the Procurement Strategy is especially important because the choice of single-use component suppliers directly affects process design and product quality. The procurement strategy needs to consider the supplier's regulatory compliance status, the availability of E&L (Extractables & Leachables) data for the components, supply chain stability (single-supplier risk), and delivery lead time.

Section Summary

  • The PEP is the core management document of an SUS project: It defines the project scope, execution approach, organization, quality control, and schedule planning, and is the common basis for all stakeholders
  • Living document: The PEP is updated continuously as the project progresses, and every major change requires a formal review and approval process
  • Impact Assessment determines the depth of C&Q: Direct Impact systems need full IQ/OQ, Indirect Impact systems need FAT/SAT plus commissioning, and No Impact systems need only GEP-level commissioning
  • Dividing line between the SEC and the owner: The SEC is responsible for design, construction, procurement, and validation (up to OQ); the owner is responsible for developing the VMP, executing PQ/PV, and subsequent operation and maintenance
  • Three-step handover mechanism: Ready to Run → Handover → Final Handover, ensuring an orderly transfer of responsibility and a complete handover of documentation
  • Special considerations for SUS projects: Shelf life management of single-use components, E&L data needs, single-source supplier risk, and the simplified scope of cleaning validation
  • Quality document hierarchy: VMP (constitution) → QAP (law) → IQ/OQ Protocols (SOP), each level building on the one above without contradiction
  • Risk management throughout: Risk analysis is performed in phases from BD to DD, covering project risk, GMP/BSL risk, and HSE risk
A3

Section A3: Appendix: Training & Refs

p135-p145

PDA Technical Report No. 66 (2014): Single-Use Systems | Sections 10.0 – 11.0 | p135 – p145

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the design logic and example table structure of a Training Plan for introducing Single-Use Systems (SUS)
  • Master the elements of a training plan: cross-functional staffing, internal/external trainers, scheduling, and the approval process
  • Review all 122 references cited in TR66 to understand the regulatory, standards, and technical literature landscape of the SUS field
  • Identify the category of each reference (ICH guidelines, USP general chapters, ISO standards, ASTM methods, PDA technical reports, journal articles, etc.) and where it applies in SUS practice
10.0 Appendix III: Training Requirements Example

Original Text

SUS Implementation Training Plan

The following table presents an example SUS Implementation Training Plan showing the organization and planning elements required when introducing single-use systems into a manufacturing facility.

The training plan matrix captures the following key elements:

  • Training Organization: The name of each training module
  • Provisional Date / Realization Date: Planned vs. actual delivery timeline
  • Department assignments: Personnel across departments (Names 1–20) with check marks indicating who attends which training
  • Training Module details: Module name, INT/EXT (internal vs. external trainer), duration/frequency, and trainer's name

The example training modules include:

Approvals (Date/Signature)

  • Manufacturing
  • Quality
  • Qualified Person
  • Human Resources
  • Finance

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

SUS Implementation Training Plan: This is an essential personnel-readiness document when introducing single-use systems. It is not just a class schedule but a systematic Competency Building Blueprint, ensuring that everyone who touches SUS has sufficient knowledge and skills before operating.

INT vs. EXT (internal vs. external training):

  • INT (Internal): Delivered by qualified in-house trainers, typically for company-specific processes such as warehouse receipt and waste handling
  • EXT (External): Delivered by the SUS supplier or external experts, typically for technical modules that require vendor knowledge, such as equipment operation, calibration, and integrity testing

Analogy

Imagine your plant switching from conventional stainless steel equipment to single-use systems. It is like a restaurant changing from ceramic dishes to disposable tableware. It looks "simpler", but every step is handled differently: warehouse staff must learn to store plastic bags correctly (temperature and humidity control), production staff must learn how to install and remove them, and QC staff must learn new integrity testing methods. Going live without systematic training is as dangerous as letting a cook who has never been trained on a microwave use one.

Key Notes

Key design principles of the training plan:

  • Cross-functional coverage: From warehouse and production support to production operations and QC, everyone who touches SUS needs the corresponding training modules
  • Phased implementation: Complete the basic operations first in Q1 (receipt, installation, basic use), then the advanced items (calibration, waste handling) in Q2/Q3
  • Five-party approval: Sign-off by Manufacturing, Quality, QP, HR, and Finance ensures the training plan has cross-functional endorsement
  • Realization Date: Comparing it with the Provisional Date lets you track training progress, which is important compliance evidence during GMP audits

Practical Application

In a CDMO environment the SUS training plan is more challenging because:

  • Different clients may use SUS components from different suppliers, so personnel need multi-brand operating skills
  • Staff turnover is relatively high, so a sustainable training system is needed rather than a one-off activity
  • Client audits often ask to see training records, so document management must be rigorous

It is advisable to build an "SUS training matrix database" that standardizes training modules, so that new hires can quickly be matched to the training courses they need.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does "Bioreactor Bags: Reception & Storage" use internal trainers (INT), while "Preparation & Integration" uses external trainers (EXT)? Analyze from the perspective of technical expertise.
  2. Why does the Finance department also need to approve the training plan? How does this relate to SUS cost management?
  3. If you were a CDMO training manager, how would you design an SUS training framework that serves multiple client projects at the same time?

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Reference Framework: The 122 references in TR66 make up a complete body of knowledge for single-use systems. Understanding how they are categorized and layered gives you a "knowledge map" of the SUS field.

These references can be grouped into the following layers:

  • Layer 1 - Regulatory framework: ICH guidelines (Q6A-Q11), 21 CFR, EMA guidelines → define "what must be done"
  • Layer 2 - Standards and methods: USP general chapters, ISO standards, ASTM methods → define "how to test and verify"
  • Layer 3 - Industry guidance: PDA TRs, BPSA recommendations, ISPE guides → define "best practices"
  • Layer 4 - Technical literature: Journal articles, conference papers → provide "scientific basis and case studies"

Analogy

Imagine constructing a building. ICH guidelines are like the building code (the minimum safety standards); USP/ISO/ASTM are like engineering standards and material specifications (how to test concrete strength); PDA Technical Reports are like the architect's handbook (best design practices); and journal articles are like engineering case studies (lessons learned from buildings others have already built). You would not start construction after reading only one layer; you need a full understanding to make sound design decisions.

Key Notes - Key applications of each reference category

ICH Q8-Q11 series: This is the quality management philosophy underlying SUS implementation. The Quality by Design (QbD) concept in Q8 guides how to systematically assess the impact of SUS on product quality; the Quality Risk Management (QRM) framework in Q9 is the methodological basis for SUS risk assessment; the Pharmaceutical Quality System (PQS) in Q10 ensures that SUS change management is backed by a complete quality system.

USP <87>/<88> biological reactivity tests: These are key compendial methods for evaluating the biocompatibility of SUS materials. <87> is an in vitro test (cytotoxicity) and <88> is an in vivo test (animal implantation). At the SUS material selection stage, these tests are the first gate in deciding whether a material is suitable for contact with the drug product.

Extractables & Leachables (E&L) literature cluster: TR66 cites a large body of E&L literature (Refs 27-31, 36, 38-46, 96-102), reflecting that E&L is the most technically challenging area of SUS implementation. These references cover the complete chain from analytical methods and safety thresholds to risk assessment strategies.

Key Notes - Economic and environmental assessment literature

TR66 specifically cites several SUS economic analysis references (Refs 53, 58-63), which is quite uncommon in a technical report. It reflects PDA's recognition that the decision to adopt SUS is not purely a technical question but requires a comprehensive assessment of Total Cost of Ownership (TCO).

Environmental impact references (Refs 64-70, 82) are also heavily cited, showing that the waste management challenge arising from the "single-use" nature of SUS has become an industry focus. When presenting proposals to clients, a CDMO needs to be able to address both economic benefit and environmental responsibility.

Practical Application

How to use this reference list in a CDMO:

  • Supplier evaluation: Require suppliers to provide biocompatibility test reports that meet USP <87>/<88> and ISO 10993, and an E&L data package consistent with BPSA recommendations
  • Change management: Use the ICH Q9 QRM framework for risk assessment, and refer to the practical cases in PDA TR54
  • Client proposals: Cite the economic analysis methods in Refs 59-63 for TCO comparison, and Refs 64, 68, and 82 to address environmental impact questions
  • Regulatory filings: Ensure that SUS-related CMC documentation cites the correct ICH, USP, and ISO standards

Practice Questions

  1. Why do Extractables & Leachables references account for such a high share (15.6%) of TR66's references? What technical challenge in the SUS field does this reflect?
  2. How do ICH Q8 (QbD), Q9 (QRM), and Q10 (PQS) each come into play at different stages of SUS implementation? Please give examples.
  3. If you were building an SUS knowledge base, how would you prioritize these 122 references? Which are "must read" and which are "for reference"?
  4. Compare USP <87> (in vitro) and <88> (in vivo) biological reactivity tests: what role does each play in SUS material evaluation?

Reference Statistics

The 122 references by category:

SUS implementation practice and economic evaluation (19.7%) and quality/supply chain/validation (20.5%) account for the largest shares, which shows that TR66's core focus is "how to actually put SUS into practice" rather than a purely technical specification discussion.

Section Summary

  • Training plan (Appendix III): SUS implementation needs a systematic training plan covering cross-functional personnel in warehouse, production support, and production operations. Training modules should distinguish internal (INT) from external (EXT) trainers and be phased over time (basic operations → advanced calibration → specialized skills). Five-party approval (Manufacturing, Quality, QP, HR, Finance) ensures the plan is fully endorsed.
  • Reference system: The 122 references in TR66 form a four-layer body of knowledge: regulatory framework (ICH) → standards and methods (USP/ISO/ASTM) → industry guidance (PDA/BPSA/ISPE) → technical literature (journals/conferences). Each layer answers a different question: "what must be done" → "how to test and verify" → "best practices" → "scientific basis".
  • E&L is the core challenge: Extractables & Leachables references make up as much as 15.6% of all references, reflecting that the chemical interaction between SUS materials and the product is the most technically demanding topic, requiring comprehensive management from analytical methods and safety thresholds to risk assessment.
  • Economics and environment both matter: TR66 heavily cites SUS economic analysis and environmental impact assessment literature, emphasizing that the decision to adopt SUS must consider both Total Cost of Ownership and waste management, not just the initial purchase cost.
  • Practical value for CDMOs: This reference list is the best starting point for a CDMO to build an SUS knowledge base. Using the category index, you can quickly find the authoritative basis needed for different scenarios such as supplier evaluation, change management, client proposals, and regulatory filings.
